Topic: a story about a big argument in my family that ended with laughter

Text:
By Maria Lopez

Last weekend, my family had a big argument. It started because my brother said it was my turn to take out the trash. I said, "No way! You did it last time!" My dad laughed and said, "This is a crime against cleanliness!"

My sister joined in and said, "We need a better method to decide!" My mom suggested we use evidence. "Look at the calendar!" she said. But then we found a digital photo of me doing it last week. "That’s not fair!" I cried.

My dad, who is as stubborn as a stone, said, "We should go to a gallery and argue about art instead!" That made us laugh. Then we remembered we had to go to the store for ice cream. "Let’s make a deal," said my sister. "Whoever buys the ice cream gets to pick the movie!"

We even made an advertising poster for our "peace agreement." Mom said this was a good education in teamwork. Now we take turns and share ice cream. The argument ended with laughter—and full stomachs!

Questions:
Q1: Why did Maria’s family start arguing?
Correct: Maria and her brother disagreed about who was responsible for taking out the trash.
Distractors:
1. They argued about who forgot to buy groceries at the store. / The store is mentioned later, not as the cause of the argument.
2. Maria’s dad said taking out the trash was a serious crime. / The word crime is used humorously, not as the root issue.
3. They fought over whose idea was better for their education. / The word education appears only at the end as a lesson, not as the argument’s topic.
---
Q2: What did they use as "evidence" to prove who took out the trash?
Correct: They found a digital photo showing Maria had done it before.
Distractors:
1. They looked at the calendar to check whose turn it was. / The calendar was suggested but not used as evidence.
2. They found crumbs as proof that someone had eaten in the kitchen. / Crumbs are not mentioned; the evidence was a photo.
3. They used a stone as a symbol to represent whose turn it was. / The stone is used metaphorically, not literally as evidence.
---
Q3: How did the argument end?
Correct: They made a deal to share ice cream and laughed about the whole thing.
Distractors:
1. They decided to visit a gallery to calm down. / The gallery was a joke during the argument, not the resolution.
2. They called the dentist to help them stop arguing. / The dentist was mentioned as a joke, not part of the solution.
3. They wrote an official advertising campaign to stop future arguments. / They made a fun poster, not a serious campaign.

By [Author]

In a small village, there lived a friendly giant named Ben. He was as big as a lorry, but his heart was kind. Ben lived in a tall tower near the river, where he helped all the villagers. However, he had one specific fear: mice.

Every night, Ben saw evidence of tiny footprints in his tower. He was scared, but he didn’t want to admit it. The village president, an old man named Tom, noticed Ben’s worry. One day, Tom brought two teenage twin brothers, Leo and Max, to help.

“Ben,” said Tom, “you must face your fear. Let’s build a bridge over the river together!” Ben agreed. As they worked, Leo and Max told jokes, and soon Ben laughed. He realized mice were just small creatures, not dangerous.

That night, Ben saw a mouse in his tower. Instead of running, he smiled. “Hello, little friend,” he said. The mouse didn’t run away! From then on, Ben was brave, and the village celebrated his courage.

Correct: The village president and two teenage twin brothers.
Distractors:
1. His twin brother.

3. A lorry driver scared the mice away.

Correct: He smiled at a mouse instead of running away.
Distractors:
1. He built a bridge over the river.
2. He asked the president for help.
3. He trapped all the mice in his tower.
Explanation:
The text describes Ben’s final action: smiling at the mouse. "Built a bridge" was part of the process but not the proof. "Asked the president" is wrong—he accepted help but didn’t rely on it. "Trapped mice" contradicts the kind interaction shown.



Topic: a story about a time I went fishing with my dad

Text:
By Alex Rivera

Last summer, my dad and I went fishing by the old pond. Before we left, he checked his fishing rod and downloaded a new app to find the best spots. "Fish are here," he said, pointing to the water where a frog jumped.

We sat against a tree, casting our lines at a distance from the bank. I tried to do some "mathematics"—like how long to wait—but dad laughed. "Fishing isn’t math!" he said. Then I joked, "I’ll die if I don’t catch anything!" Dad just smiled.

We spent less time than planned, but I had fun. On the way home, dad read a book about fish while I told him about my "almost-catch." It wasn’t the biggest trip, but it was special.

Questions:

Distractors:
1. To check the weather for the day.
2. To measure the fishing rod.
3. To listen to music while fishing.
Explanation:
The text says, "downloaded a new app to find the best spots." The correct answer matches this. Distractor 1 is wrong because weather isn’t mentioned. Distractor 2 is wrong because the app wasn’t for measuring. Distractor 3 is wrong because the app was for fishing, not music. A weak reader might pick 1 or 2 because apps can be used for many things, or 3 because they associate apps with entertainment.
---
Q2: What does Alex’s joke about "dying" show about his feelings?
Correct: He was excited but also joking about being nervous.
Distractors:
1. He was very tired from sitting too long.
2. He didn’t like fishing and wanted to leave.
3. He was scared of the frog in the pond.

The joke "I’ll die if I don’t catch anything!" shows humor and excitement, not tiredness (1), dislike (2), or fear (3). Distractor 1 is wrong because Alex says he had fun. Distractor 2 is wrong because he calls it "special." Distractor 3 is wrong because the frog is mentioned casually. A weak reader might pick 1 or 3 because sitting can cause tiredness, or frogs can seem scary.
---
Q3: How was the fishing trip different from what Alex expected?
Correct: He thought they would catch fish, but they didn’t catch any.
Distractors:
1. They spent more time than planned.
2. Alex’s dad didn’t bring a book to read.
3. The pond had no frogs, which surprised Alex.
Explanation:
The text says, "We spent less time than planned" and "I told him about my 'almost-catch'" (no fish caught). The correct answer matches this. Distractor 1 is wrong because they spent less time. Distractor 2 is wrong because dad did read a book. Distractor 3 is wrong because a frog was mentioned. A weak reader might pick 1 if they focus on "fun," 2 if they overlook the book detail, or 3 if they miss the frog mention.

Distractors:
1. She was tired.
2. She was confused.
3. She wanted to leave early.
Explanation:

Topic: a story about a time I compared prices before buying something

Text:
Last month, I wanted to buy a new chair for my study. I can find excellent deals online, but I like to see things before I buy. So, I went to three stores.

The first chair looked comfortable, but the instruction book was hard to understand. The second one was cheap, but the repair cost would be high if something broke. Price was a big factor for me, but durability mattered too.

At the third store, I saw a chair with a stone-colored cover. It was not the cheapest, but it had a strong frame. The salesperson said it would last for years. I also checked the battery—it was not dead like some others!

I studied physics in school, so I know good quality can save money in the long run. In the end, I bought the third chair. Now, I am happy with my choice!

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator go to three different stores before buying a chair?
Correct: To compare prices and see the chairs in person before deciding.
Distractors:
1. Because the first store did not have any chairs left.
2. Because the salespeople gave bad advice.
3. Because they only sold expensive chairs.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s explanation that the narrator "went to three stores" to compare options. The distractors are wrong because the text does not mention running out of chairs, bad salespeople, or only expensive chairs.
---

Correct: Because it would be expensive to repair if it broke.
Distractors:
1. Because it was not as comfortable as the first one.
2. Because the instruction book was too difficult.
3. Because it had a dead battery.

Explanation:
The correct answer comes from the text: "the repair cost would be high if something broke." The distractors are wrong because comfort was not mentioned for the second chair, the unclear instruction book was for the first chair, and only the third chair’s battery was checked.

Correct: They chose the chair that balanced price and durability.
Distractors:
1. They picked the cheapest chair available.
2. They only looked at the color of the chairs.
3. They trusted the salesperson’s opinion completely.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s focus on "price was a big factor" and "durability mattered." The distractors are wrong because the narrator did not pick the cheapest chair, color was just one detail, and they used their own judgment, not just the salesperson’s advice.

We were so active that we didn’t notice the time.

By Thumper the Rabbit

Thumper the rabbit had one big dream: to fly. Every morning, he made a strict schedule to practice. "I will be the winner of the sky!" he said. But how?

One day, he saw an ancient owl sitting on a tree. "You are the director of the flying school?" Thumper asked. "Yes," said the owl. "But flying is not easy."

Thumper tried to copy the birds, flapping his arms. He wore bright red pants to feel brave. He even chatted with the birds, but they laughed. "You’ll never fly like us!" they said.

One evening, he saw an express train flying in the sky. "Maybe I can fly too!" he thought. "Did it really matter if I failed?" he asked himself. No, it didn’t. So he kept trying.

Topic: an adventure story about a foggy day

Text:
The Foggy Invention
By Liam Carter

One foggy morning in the desert town, Jake, an engineer, walked to his factory. His employer, Mr. Hayes, was always angry about the thick fog. "We need fresh air here!" Jake said.

Jake had a specific idea—a recipe for clean air. He worked hard, but Mr. Hayes wanted to marry the idea to his own business. "This is definitely mine!" Jake shouted.

---
Q3: What might happen if Jake didn’t protect his invention?
Correct: The town would remain dangerous with poor air quality.
Distractors:
1. Mr. Hayes would fire Jake. / The fog would disappear on its own. / Jake would become the new employer.
Explanation:
The text states the town would "suffer" without Jake’s invention. The correct answer paraphrases this. The other options are wrong because:
- No firing is mentioned.
- The fog’s disappearance depends on Jake’s work.
- No promotion is suggested.



Topic: an adventure story about a desert

Text:
"By Jake Carter"

Last month, my friends and I went on an adventure in the desert. Our target was an oasis, but the land was flat and empty. Recently, we saw a closed canyon—it looked dangerous!

We were nervous because we had to compete for water. My friend Sam said, "We must take control!" We climbed into our old board truck and drove actively across the sand. We made a recording of our journey with a small camera.

At sunset, we reached the oasis. We were safe!

Distractors:
1. They walked the whole time.
2. They rode camels.
3. They took a recording device.
Explanation:
The text says they used a board truck. Distractor 1 is wrong—they used a vehicle. Distractors 2 and 3 are false (no camels or recording for travel).

Correct: They wanted to reach an oasis.
Distractors:
1. They wanted to compete in a race.
2. They wanted to make a recording.
3. They wanted to control the desert.
Explanation:
The text says their target was an oasis. Distractor 1 is wrong—they competed for water, not a race. Distractors 2 and 3 are false (recording was secondary, and they didn’t aim to control the desert).



Topic: an adventure story about visiting an exotic country

Text:
By Alex Carter

Last summer, I visited Bali for the first time. The train from the airport was slow, but the general atmosphere was exciting. My friend suggested an alternative—a faster bus—but I wanted to see the countryside. The hotel had a small fridge in the room, which was unusual, and breakfast was included in the price.

Our guide, Wayan, mentioned a hidden waterfall somewhere in the jungle. We hiked for hours and found it! On the way back, I made a deal with a local vendor for fresh mangoes. After the long walk, we took a rest at a roadside stall. The meal was simple—just rice and grilled fish—but it was delicious.

I loved Bali’s mix of adventure and rest!

Distractors:

The text says Alex "wanted to see the countryside," so he prioritized views over speed. The bus was an alternative (faster), and cost/friend’s preference aren’t mentioned.

Distractors:
1. Breakfast was not included. / The room was very small. / There was no air conditioning.

Topic: a fairy tale about a princess who preferred gardening to ruling

Text:
The Princess and the Rose Garden
Princess Elara loved gardening more than ruling. Every morning, she woke early to tend her flowers. Her instructor, an old gardener, taught her to plant seeds in a small bowl and care for them with love. "A particular rose," she whispered, "will bloom only for those who listen to the earth."

The king worried about an upcoming war. "Daughter," he said, "you must focus on governance. The kingdom needs a strong ruler, not a gardener!" But Elara smiled. "Father, my plants have a medical benefit. The village’s sick children recover when they smell my roses."

One day, a soldier brought news: the enemy’s army was weak from disease. "Ah!" Elara cried. "My garden can help!" She sent her roses to the battlefield. The enemy’s danger faded—their soldiers, too, began to heal.

The king stood in her garden, watching the sunrise. "You have saved us," he said. But Elara shook her head. "I want none of the crown. Let someone else rule." She opened the old cupboard where she kept her tools. "I’ll stay here, where the soil remembers my name."

The war ended without bloodshed, and the kingdom thrived. The king sighed. "Very well. But promise me one thing: teach others to grow."

Questions:
Q1: Why did Princess Elara refuse to become queen?
Correct: She preferred her passion for gardening over the responsibilities of ruling.
Distractors:
1. She was afraid of the danger involved in ruling the kingdom.
2. Her father had forbidden her to garden, so she rebelled.
3. She wanted to travel the world instead of staying in the castle.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches "I want none of the crown" and her love of gardening. Distractor 1 confuses danger (enemy’s weakness) with her personal fears. Distractor 2 invents a conflict not in the text. Distractor 3 introduces unrelated details.
---
Q2: How did Elara’s gardening help the kingdom during the war?
Correct: Her roses had healing properties that weakened the enemy’s army.
Distractors:
1. She grew food to feed the soldiers, making them stronger.
2. Her garden became a hiding place for spies to gather information.
3. The king used her flowers to create a medical potion for his troops.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with "the enemy’s army was weak from disease" and "sent her roses to the battlefield." Distractor 1 assumes gardening = farming. Distractor 2 adds espionage, which isn’t mentioned. Distractor 3 misinterprets "medical" as a potion rather than the roses’ scent.
---
Q3: What did the king realize about Elara’s skills by the end of the story?
Correct: Her gardening skills were more valuable to the kingdom than political power.
Distractors:
1. She was secretly training an army in her garden.
2. Her roses could only grow in times of war, making them rare.
3. He decided to make her the kingdom’s instructor for gardening.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects "You have saved us" (gardening’s impact) and "none of the crown" (rejecting power). Distractor 1 misreads "tools" as weapons. Distractor 2 incorrectly limits the roses’ growth. Distractor 3 assumes promotion, but the king only asks her to teach others.



Topic: a fairy tale about a village where it rained lemonade once a year

Text:
The Lemonade Rain
In a small village, it rained lemonade once a year. The scene was magical—the sky turned golden, and droplets flying through the air smelled sweet. The lemonade washed the land, cleaning the dirt from the streets. Children laughed as they fished it in buckets, shouting, "Excellent!"

The old speaker, Mrs. Hattie, smiled. "This tradition started long ago," she said. "Our ancestors loved lemonade so much, they asked the sky for it every year." The villagers’ profiles shone with happiness as they sipped the drink. Even a tiny insect, caught in the rain, seemed to enjoy the taste.

"Dear friends," Mrs. Hattie said, "this is our gift from the sky!" And every year, the lemonade rain made their village the happiest place in the world.

Questions:
Q1: What did the villagers do with the lemonade when it rained?
Correct: They collected it in buckets and drank it happily.
Distractors:
1. They used it to wash their clothes.
2. They gave it to the insects.
3. They sold it to other villages.
Explanation:
The text says, "Children laughed as they fished it in buckets, shouting, 'Excellent!'" The correct answer matches this. "Wash their clothes" is wrong because the lemonade washed the land, not clothes. "Gave it to the insects" is wrong because the insect was just caught in the rain, not given lemonade. "Sold it" is unsupported by the text.
---
Q2: Why did the lemonade rain happen in the village?
Correct: It was a tradition started by their ancestors who loved lemonade.
Distractors:
1. Because the sky was made of lemonade.
2. To make the insects happy.
3. Because the villagers asked for it every morning.
Explanation:
The text explains, "Our ancestors loved lemonade so much, they asked the sky for it every year." The correct answer paraphrases this. "Sky was made of lemonade" is wrong because the rain is a tradition, not a literal sky. "To make insects happy" is wrong because the insect is just a detail. "Every morning" is wrong because it happens once a year.
---
Q3: How did the old speaker describe the lemonade rain?
Correct: She called it a gift from the sky.
Distractors:
1. She said it was a punishment for being lazy.
2. She explained it was made by fairies.
3. She warned them not to drink too much.
Explanation:
The text says, "‘This is our gift from the sky!’" The correct answer matches this. "Punishment" is wrong because the tone is happy. "Made by fairies" is wrong because the text attributes it to ancestors. "Warned not to drink" is wrong because the villagers drank it happily.



Topic: a fairy tale about a lonely cloud that made friends with a mountain

Text:
The Lonely Cloud and the Mountain

Once upon a time, a lonely cloud named Puffy floated high above a tall mountain. "I wish I had a friend," he sighed. One day, Puffy tried to reach the mountain’s peak, but the wind throw him back. "Hello!" called the mountain in a kind manner. "I am the owner of this rock!"

Puffy was surprised. "You can talk?" "Of course!" laughed the mountain. "I work in the Cloud Department—well, mostly I just sit here." Puffy smiled happily. "Let’s be friends!" They talked for hours. The mountain reviewed his old stories, and Puffy told jokes.

"Shall we progress together?" asked Puffy. "Easily!" said the mountain. They traveled side by side, and Puffy threw snowflakes like confetti. At sunset, Puffy waved. "I’ll visit again soon!" The mountain nodded, and Puffy floated away, his heart full.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Puffy the cloud feel lonely at first?
Correct: Because he had no friends in the sky.
Distractors:
1. He missed his family in the Cloud Department. / Wrong—no family is mentioned.
2. The mountain was too far away to talk to. / Wrong—they did talk.
3. He was too small to play with other clouds. / Wrong—size isn’t an issue.

Explanation:
The text states Puffy "wished he had a friend." Weak readers might pick 1 (assuming Department = family) or 2 (misreading reach as a distance problem).
---
Q2: What did the mountain and Puffy decide to do together?
Correct: They agreed to travel side by side.
Distractors:
1. They planned to throw snowflakes at each other. / Wrong—they threw snowflakes after deciding to travel.
2. The mountain taught Puffy how to talk. / Wrong—the mountain could already talk.
3. Puffy stayed with the mountain forever. / Wrong—they parted at sunset.

Explanation:
The text says, "'Shall we progress together?'" The correct answer paraphrases this. Weak readers might pick 1 (focusing on throw without context) or 3 (assuming visit again = staying).
---
Q3: How did Puffy show his happiness at the end of the story?
Correct: He promised to visit the mountain again.
Distractors:
1. He danced in the sky with joy. / Wrong—no dancing is mentioned.
2. The mountain gave him a gift. / Wrong—no gifts are given.
3. He became the owner of the mountain. / Wrong—Puffy was never the owner.

Explanation:
The text says, "'I’ll visit again soon!'" The correct answer paraphrases this. Weak readers might pick 1 (assuming happily = dancing) or 2 (misreading owner as a gift).



Topic: a fairy tale about a mouse who lived in a library and learned to read

Text:
Milo was a tiny mouse who lived in a library whose shelves were full of books. He loved stories but couldn’t read—it was his worst problem. One day, he saw a book about flying machines and thought, "If I could read, I’d know everything!"

The librarian, Mrs. Pawsley, thought he was crazy. "Mice don’t read!" she said. But Milo kept trying. The books were old, and their dry pages made his nose itch. Worse, a sneaky virus (dust!) hid in the corners, making some books look like rubbish.

Milo studied the library’s system: where the books lived, how they smelled, and the exact spot where the light hit the pages. He had to pay attention—no distractions! One rainy evening, during the worst storm of the year, he finally understood. "Aha!" he cheered. Now he could read—and dream of flying through stories.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Milo want to learn to read?
Correct: To understand everything, like the stories in books.
Distractors:
1. To impress the librarian.
2. Because he wanted to fly like the books in the story.
3. Because his friends could read and he felt left out.
Explanation:
The text says Milo "loved stories" and "wanted to know everything" after seeing a book about flying. The correct answer matches this. Distractor 1 is wrong because the librarian’s opinion doesn’t motivate him. Distractor 2 misinterprets his dream as a goal. Distractor 3 is unsupported—no friends are mentioned.
---
Q2: What made reading difficult for Milo at first?
Correct: The books were old and had a virus (dust) that made them dry and hard to read.
Distractors:
1. The librarian wouldn’t let him borrow books.
2. The words were too big for his tiny eyes.
3. He didn’t know the exact system of the library.
Explanation:
The text explains the dry books and virus (dust) as the problem. Distractor 1 is wrong because the librarian didn’t stop him. Distractor 2 ignores the dust/virus detail. Distractor 3 describes his solution, not the initial difficulty.
---
Q3: How did Milo finally learn to read?
Correct: He studied the library’s system and paid close attention to the books.
Distractors:
1. He waited until the worst storm passed and the books were wet.
2. The librarian taught him how to read.
3. He found a book without the virus in it.
Explanation:
The text states he "studied the system" and "paid attention" during the storm. Distractor 1 reverses cause/effect (the storm happened while he learned). Distractor 2 is false—the librarian didn’t help. Distractor 3 is wrong because he adapted to the virus, not avoided it.



Topic: a story about a morning I woke up earlier than everyone else

Text:
By Emma Carter

Last summer, I went to a camp in the mountains. One morning, I woke up before everyone else. The air was heavy with silence, and there was no evidence of movement outside my tent. I didn’t want to make noise—there was a law about quiet hours!

I looked at my phone. It was connected to the camp’s Wi-Fi, but the battery was low. I needed power to use it. Then I remembered my clever idea: I started singing softly. A few minutes later, my female friend Lisa opened her eyes. She smiled and said, “You’re up early!” Soon, others woke up too.

The camp rules said no loud noises, but my quiet singing worked. By the time the sun rose, everyone was happy and ready for breakfast.

When I was ten, I kept a diary for a single unusual reason. My parents were busy, and I felt a gap in my life. I didn’t know if this feeling existed for others, but it was real for me.

I used a narrow notebook and wrote direct thoughts every night. One day, I described how I missed my old school. Half of my friends had moved away, and I didn’t know how to fill the empty space. I also wrote about riding my bike—my wheel was my escape. Sometimes, I threw away rubbish thoughts, like when I worried too much.

The diary helped me land on a new idea: feelings don’t always need words. But writing them down made them smaller and easier to understand.

The third is wrong because losing a notebook isn’t in the text.

1. Only about their favourite school subjects.

Topic: an adventure story about a bicycle trip that took much longer than planned

Tom and I planned a short bike trip to the river, but we got lost on the first day. We took a wrong turn and discovered a small path through the woods. The temperature dropped, so we stopped at an old church to rest. Inside, we found a blank map on the wall and some biscuits left by visitors.

We had a discussion about going forward—Tom wanted to turn back, but I thought we could recycle the map. Suddenly, I felt sick with a disease—maybe a cold. We decided to wait until morning.

The next day, the earth was wet from rain, but we followed the map. After hours of riding, we finally saw the river! It took us three days instead of one, but we laughed about our adventure.

Topic: a personal story about learning a new skill

Text:
By Maria Lopez

I’ve always loved music, but nothing prepared me for how strongly it would change my life after my accident. Last summer, I fell while hiking and injured my chest. The doctor said I couldn’t lift heavy things or play sports for months. At first, I felt useless—until my sister gave me a second-hand guitar. It was musical in a way I hadn’t expected: the first chords I played were strangely relaxing, like a warm bath for my soul.

A few weeks later, I joined a small campout with friends in the woods. We set up a tent near a lake, and I brought my guitar, hoping to play for everyone. But halfway through my first song, a string snapped! My friends laughed, but I was determined to fix it. The nearest mall was an hour away, so I spent the morning riding my bike there, searching for a repair shop. By the time I got back, my fingers ached, but I practiced until my playing sounded perfectly in tune.

Now, I’m proud of how far I’ve come. Music isn’t just a hobby—it’s a way to heal. And though my chest still reminds me of that fall, my guitar reminds me that even broken things can be fixed, and even painful experiences can lead to something beautiful.

Explanation:

Distractors:
1. She lost her guitar in the woods and had to search for it all day.
2. Her friends refused to listen to her play because they thought she was bad.
3. She couldn’t find a place to set up her tent near the lake.
Explanation:
The correct answer directly states the guitar string snapped. Distractor 1 confuses "broke" with "lost"; distractor 2 misrepresents the friends’ laughter as criticism; distractor 3 ignores the guitar issue entirely.
---
Q3: How did Maria feel after she fixed her guitar and played it well?
Correct: She was happy with her progress and proud of how much she had improved.
Distractors:
1. She felt embarrassed because her friends had seen her struggle.
2. She decided to quit playing guitar because her fingers still hurt.
3. She realized she needed a better guitar and went to buy a new one immediately.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects her pride in improvement. Distractor 1 misinterprets laughter as embarrassment; distractor 2 ignores her continued practice; distractor 3 contradicts her repair of the old guitar.

1. She regrets not learning guitar sooner because it would have healed her faster.

Explanation:

Topic: an adventure story about a mountain trip

Text:
By [Author]

Lena and Tom had always dreamed of climbing Mount Kailash, but they were wrong to ignore the guide’s warnings. "The weather changes fast here," he had reminded them, but they laughed it off. Their practical gear—sturdy boots and a map—felt useless when they took the wrong path.

Halfway up, a local villager protested, "This trail is cursed!" Tom rolled his eyes, but Lena felt embarrassed when they saw the frame of a broken tent ahead—a sign of past climbers who had underestimated the mountain. Their enemy that day wasn’t the altitude but their own arrogance.

As dusk fell, the sky darkened. The guide’s prediction of a storm had been ignored, and now they were trapped. A distant drum echoed through the valleys, reminding them of the legends they’d scoffed at. By midnight, they huddled in a cave, judging their choices harshly.

Back in the village, the old man smirked. "Told you so." Lena sighed. The mountains had framed their failure in a breathtaking but humbling lesson.

Clara tended to avoid crowds, so she spent her free time in the park or by the river, capturing quiet scenes with her camera. Topic: a story about an unexpected act of kindness

Text:
Unexpected Kindness
By Maria Chen

Following a long day at work, I sat in my usual café, exhausted. The cozy atmosphere and soft music usually helped me relax, but tonight, I just wanted to escape. As I sipped my coffee, I noticed a woman at the next table printing something on her phone. She seemed distracted, her emotion hard to read.

Then, something unexpected happened. The woman stood up, walked to the counter, and paid for my coffee. "Here," she said, handing the barista an extra note. "For the lady in the corner." My excitement turned to confusion—I didn’t recognize her. She smiled and left without a word.

I couldn’t define why it meant so much, but her gesture stayed with me. Maybe kindness doesn’t need a basis in familiarity. The shift in my mood was instant: from tiredness to warmth. Later, I reflected on how often we overlook small acts. The café’s usual luxury of silence felt different now—filled with the quiet power of an anonymous kindness.

Questions:
Q1: Why was Maria confused by the woman’s act of kindness?
Correct: She didn’t recognize the woman, so she couldn’t understand why a stranger would help her.
Distractors:
1. She thought the woman was trying to trick her.
2. She had expected someone famous to pay for her coffee.
3. She was annoyed because she didn’t like being helped by strangers.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s detail ("I didn’t recognize her"). Distractor 1 misinterprets "distracted" as "suspicious." Distractor 2 introduces an unrelated idea (fame), and distractor 3 contradicts the emotional shift (excitement, not annoyance).
---
Q2: What does Maria mean when she says, "Maybe kindness doesn’t need a basis in familiarity"?

Topic: a personal story about achieving a long-term goal

Text:
My Dream of Hosting a Festival
Since I was a child, organizing events has been my greatest attraction. I was mad about the idea of hosting a music festival, but the thought also made me pale with nerves. Everyone said it was impossible—I had no experience, and the local council had strict rules.

The first real issue was funding. I didn’t know where to start, so I asked my uncle, who had worked with a mild-mannered organizer of a small western-style festival. He invited me to help, and I learned so much. The organizer was patient and taught me how to deal with issues like permits and volunteers.

At first, I felt overwhelmed. The pressure made me pale, and I even got mad at myself for doubting my abilities. But my uncle reminded me that every big event starts small. Finally, I decided to go for it. With his help, I planned my own festival. It wasn’t perfect, but it was mine—and the best part was seeing people enjoy it.

Questions:

Correct: They lacked experience and faced strict rules.
Distractors:
1. They had never attended a festival before. / Wrong: The text says they organized events since childhood, not that they’d never attended one.
2. The festival was too expensive to plan. / Wrong: Cost was an issue, but not the reason for nerves.
3. Their family did not support the idea. / Wrong: No mention of family opposition.

Explanation:
The text states, "I had no experience... strict rules," which directly explains the nerves. Option 1 misinterprets "organizing" as "attending." Option 2 confuses the issue (funding) with the emotional cause (inexperience/rules). Option 3 introduces an unsupported assumption.

Distractors:
1. He gave them money to start the festival. / Wrong: The uncle’s help was about knowledge, not money.
2. He organized the festival for them. / Wrong: The narrator did the organizing after learning.
3. He convinced the council to approve the idea. / Wrong: The council’s rules were a separate issue; the uncle’s role was educational.

Explanation:
The text says, "asked my uncle... invited me to help... learned so much." Option 1 assumes financial aid. Option 2 misinterprets "help" as "doing it for them." Option 3 incorrectly attributes council approval to the uncle.
---
Q3: What does the phrase "made me pale" suggest about the narrator’s emotional state?
Correct: They were extremely anxious or scared.
Distractors:
1. They were sick or unwell. / Wrong: "Pale" here is metaphorical (nerves), not physical illness.
2. They were embarrassed. / Wrong: The text links "pale" to nerves, not embarrassment.
3. They were excited. / Wrong: "Pale" + "nerves" = negative emotion (fear/anxiety).

Explanation:
The text pairs "pale" with "nerves," indicating fear. Option 1 ignores the metaphorical use. Option 2 confuses "nerves" with "embarrassment." Option 3 contradicts the negative connotation of "pale" in this context.
---
Q4: What was the narrator’s final decision after facing difficulties?
Correct: They chose to proceed with the plan despite their fears.
Distractors:
1. They gave up and asked someone else to host it. / Wrong: The text says they decided to go for it.
2. They postponed the festival until next year. / Wrong: No mention of postponement.
3. They changed their plan to a smaller event. / Wrong: The goal remained the same (hosting a festival).

Explanation:
The text states, "Finally, I decided to go for it." Option 1 contradicts this. Option 2 assumes delay from "difficulties." Option 3 misinterprets "not perfect" as a change in scale.

Topic: a story about the book that changed my life

Text:
The Book That Changed My Life
By Maria Lopez

My primary memory of books is the old, leather-bound story that my grandmother gave me when I was seven. The cover had a strange, soft fur-like texture, and I spent hours running my fingers within its pages. Back then, I wasn’t a confident reader, but the words inside made me feel content in a way nothing else did. I used to pretend I was the hero of the adventures, imagining myself climbing mountains or outsmarting villains.

However, the real magic was how it made me want to write. I wasn’t happy with just reading—I wanted to create something better. That book gave me the charge to start my own stories, scribbled in the margins of my notebooks. Even now, years later, the lessons from those pages remain with me. I still believe in the power of imagination, and I’m still proud of the little girl who found wonder in a book.

Questions:

Correct: She found its texture unusual and comforting.
Distractors:
1. The cover was made of real animal fur. / Wrong: Text says "fur-like texture" (not real fur).
2. She liked the book because it had bright colors. / Wrong: Focus is on texture, not colors.
3. The cover was hard to touch, which made it unique. / Wrong: Text says "soft... fur-like" (not hard).
Explanation: The text describes the cover as having a "soft... fur-like texture" that made Maria "feel content." Distractors 1 and 3 contradict this (real fur/hardness), while distractor 2 misinterprets "special" as visual rather than tactile/emotional.
---
Q2: How did the book influence Maria’s writing habits?
Correct: It inspired her to start writing her own stories.
Distractors:
1. She began reading books more carefully after this. / Wrong: Focus is on writing, not reading.
2. The book taught her grammar rules she didn’t know before. / Wrong: No mention of grammar; focus is on creativity.
3. She stopped pretending to be the characters and wrote about real life. / Wrong: Text says she continued imagining ("used to pretend" + later wrote stories).
Explanation: The text states, "I wanted to create something better... scribbled in my notebooks." Distractors 1 and 2 misrepresent the influence, while 3 incorrectly assumes she abandoned imagination.
---
Q3: What does Maria imply about her reading skills before discovering this book?
Correct: She wasn’t very confident as a reader at first.
Distractors:
1. She was already an advanced reader who enjoyed complex books. / Wrong: Text says "wasn’t a confident reader."
2. She struggled because the book was too difficult for her age. / Wrong: Focus is on emotional connection, not difficulty.
3. She only read books that her grandmother chose for her. / Wrong: Text doesn’t limit her reading.
Explanation: The phrase "I wasn’t a confident reader" directly answers this. Distractors 1 and 2 overinterpret her skill level, while 3 adds unsupported restrictions.
---
Q4: Why might Maria still feel proud of the little girl who read this book?
Correct: Because it represents her discovery of imagination and creativity.
Distractors:
1. She remembers the book as her first prize-winning story. / Wrong: No mention of prizes.
2. The book was rare and valuable, so she treasures it. / Wrong: Focus is on emotional impact, not material value.
3. She became a famous writer because of this book. / Wrong: Text doesn’t claim fame.
Explanation: The text explains pride in "the power of imagination" and "the little girl who found wonder." Distractors 1 and 3 assume achievement-based pride, while 2 misreads "proud" as possession-based.



Topic: a story about a volunteer experience

Text:
Volunteering in the Aftermath
Last summer, I joined a team responding to an explosion in a nearby town. The first night, we couldn’t even breathe properly—the air was thick with dust and the substance of burnt wires. A child’s bite from a broken glass panicked us, but we stayed calm. Our intelligence was tested when we had to define priorities: treat injuries or secure the area.

The battle wasn’t just physical. Some volunteers wanted to rush, but we knew profit wasn’t the goal—just helping. By making contact with locals, we realized the real reward was giving them hope. Though exhausted, I left knowing this was the most rewarding experience I’d ever done.

By Mara Voss

I’m an artist whose work has traveled equally between galleries and the outdoors. Many people know me for my large-scale installations, but my early pieces used unexpected materials like coal, which I mined locally. My creative process begins with fitness—I paint outdoors to stay energized, and the changing light pulls me into new ideas. Some of my most popular works are custom commissions for public spaces, like the mural in the park. The community benefits from these projects; they bring people together and spark conversations.

I think art should be accessible, so I offer workshops where participants can try different techniques. My goal is to show that creativity isn’t just for professionals—it’s for everyone. Whether I’m working with coal or bright acrylics, I want my art to have a global impact, one brushstroke at a time.

Topic: a story about finding a magic mirror that shows the future

Text:
The Magic Mirror
When I was young, my father lost his job because of unemployment, and we moved into a small, damp accommodation above a bakery. One rainy evening, while exploring the attic, I found a strange object wrapped in cloth—a solid silver mirror with a royal crest engraved on its deep, unbreakable frame. At first, it looked like a normal mirror, but when I peered into it, I gasped. Instead of my reflection, I saw a wonder-filled vision of my future: myself as an old man, holding a book written in golden ink.

I touched the glass, and the image shifted, showing me my children’s faces. The highlight of the mirror was its ability to reveal the future, but it also carried a warning: "Beware the choices you make." That night, I understood its power. Though I was still young, the mirror showed me deep truths about life—some happy, some frightening. I kept it hidden, using it only when necessary, for it was not just a mirror; it was a guide.

Questions:

Correct: To use it carefully, as its power was both valuable and risky.
Distractors:
1. He was afraid someone would steal it.
2. He didn’t want his father to see it.
3. The mirror was too heavy to carry around.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says he used it "only when necessary", implying controlled use.
- Distractor 1: "Hidden" might suggest theft, but the issue is power, not security.
- Distractor 2: No mention of hiding it from family; the warning is personal.
- Distractor 3: "Solid" describes durability, not weight.

Distractors:
1. He stopped using the mirror altogether.
2. He decided to leave his family to avoid the future shown.
3. He showed the mirror to his father for advice.
Explanation:
- Correct: The warning ("Beware the choices you make") implies caution.
- Distractor 1: He kept it hidden but used it, not abandoned it.
- Distractor 2: The future included children, so he stayed.
- Distractor 3: No evidence he shared it; the warning is individual.
---
Q3: What does the phrase "the highlight of the mirror" suggest about its most important feature?
Correct: Its ability to show the future was its most striking quality.
Distractors:
1. The mirror’s frame was the most beautiful part.
2. It could only show the past, not the future.
3. The mirror’s warnings were its most important aspect.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text describes the future visions as the highlight.
- Distractor 1: The frame is royal and solid, but not the "highlight."
- Distractor 2: The text explicitly says it showed the future.
- Distractor 3: The warning is secondary to the future-revealing power.
---
Q4: What can we infer about the narrator’s family life from the text?

2. His father was angry about the mirror.

- Distractor 1: The children’s faces contradict this.

Topic: a story about a magical pencil that brings drawings to life

Text:
Last summer, I came across a strange pencil in a small shop near my school. It looked ordinary, but the shopkeeper warned me, "This one’s special—it brings drawings to life." I laughed, but I bought it anyway. That night, I drew a cake with fresh flour and icing. When I stepped back, the cake was suddenly pulled out of the sketchbook and began to rise in the air like a hot-air balloon!

The next day at school, my drawing of a drummer started beating a drum in the middle of the classroom. The staff rushed in, thinking it was a prank, but the drummer just kept playing. I was so embarrassed that I hid my notebook. Meanwhile, my friend Leo’s drawing of a cat started chasing mice around the cafeteria, attracting a crowd.

The shopkeeper later explained that the pencil had rules: "Every drawing costs a small tax—one hour of your time to fix any mess." I had to spend the afternoon cleaning up flour from the walls and explaining to the principal why my sketches were causing chaos. The result was that I had to promise never to draw anything dangerous again.

Now, I only use the pencil for small things—a bird that sings, a teapot that pours tea—but I still keep an eye on my account of magical hours. After all, you never know when a drawing might take after its creator and start demanding more freedom!

Questions:
Q1: Why did Emma hide her notebook after the drummer incident?
Correct: She was ashamed because her magical drawings were causing a scene in class.
Distractors:
1. She was worried the pencil would break if she used it again.
2. The school staff confiscated her notebook.
3. She didn’t want anyone to see her secret drawings.
Explanation:
The text states Emma was "embarrassed", meaning she felt shame, not fear or secrecy. The staff didn’t take her notebook, and the issue was the performance, not the drawings themselves. A weak reader might focus on the pencil’s strangeness or assume authority would punish her.
---
Q2: What was the "tax" for using the pencil?
Correct: The user had to spend an hour cleaning up any problems caused by their drawings.
Distractors:
1. A small fee had to be paid to the shopkeeper every month.
2. The drawings would disappear after one day.
3. The user had to draw a portrait of the shopkeeper.
Explanation:
The text specifies a "time tax" (cleaning up), not money or a time limit. The shopkeeper never mentions a portrait. A weak reader might assume "tax" means money or invent a logical but unsupported rule.
---
Q3: How did Emma’s drawings affect the school environment?
Correct: They created chaos, like a drummer playing in class and a cat chasing mice in the cafeteria.
Distractors:
1. The drawings scared the students into leaving the school.
2. The principal banned all artistic activities.
3. The drawings helped with school performances.
Explanation:
The text describes chaos (e.g., a drummer in class, a cat chasing mice), not fear or bans. A weak reader might assume authority would overreact or focus only on the drummer’s talent without considering the broader disruption.
---
Q4: What does "take after its creator" imply about the pencil’s magic?
Correct: The drawings might start behaving like Emma herself.
Distractors:
1. The drawings would look exactly like Emma’s real-life personality.
2. The pencil would stop working if Emma stopped drawing.
3. The drawings would only obey Emma’s commands.
Explanation:
The phrase suggests behavioral similarity, not appearance or control. The text implies unpredictability ("demanding freedom"), so a weak reader might literalize the phrase or assume magic is controllable.



Topic: a fairy tale about a woman who talks to birds and they help her

Text:
In the quiet village of Eldermere, people often mentioned whispers in the wind—no one knew where they came from. One evening, Liora heard them too, following the sound to a clearing where a small bird lay injured. Its wing was pierced by a poisonous thorn, and its breathing was slow. Without hesitation, she pulled it out, and something strange happened: the bird tilted its head and spoke. "You saved me," it chirped. A relation had formed between them.

But the forest was not safe. Hunters had arrived, using nets coated with a strange chemical to kill the birds. Liora’s mood grew darker with each cry. One night, she convinced the birds to trust her. "There’s a hidden port in the old oak," she whispered. "It leads to the Whispering Glade, where no hunters dare go." The birds agreed, their wings fluttering in unison. As they flew ahead, their breathing sounded like a soft lullaby, guiding her through the shadows.

By dawn, Liora stood at the edge of the glade, alive and free. The birds had saved her—and in return, she had given them a voice.

Correct: She became hopeful and protective when she realized the bird trusted her.
Distractors:
1. She felt sad because the bird was dying. / She was afraid the hunters would find her. / The village gossip made her angry.
Explanation: The text shows her mood shifted positively when the bird spoke to her ("something strange happened"), not negatively. Sadness or fear are introduced later with the hunters.
---
Q2: What did the hunters use to kill the birds in the forest?
Correct: Nets coated with a harmful chemical substance.
Distractors:
1. Poisonous arrows. / Loud noises to scare them. / Traps set on the ground.
Explanation: The text explicitly states "nets coated with a strange chemical." The other options contradict this detail.
---
Q3: How did Liora convince the birds to help her escape the hunters?
Correct: She promised them safety in the Whispering Glade.
Distractors:
1. She threatened the hunters with magic. / She offered them food. / She carried them to safety herself.
Explanation: The text describes her promise ("where no hunters dare go"), not threats, food, or physical carrying.
---
Q4: What does the port in the old oak represent in the story?
Correct: A secret entrance to a place where the hunters feared to go.
Distractors:
1. A prison for the birds. / A trap set by the hunters. / A magical gateway to another world.
Explanation: The port is framed as a refuge ("Whispering Glade, where no hunters dare go"), not a threat or portal.



Topic: a story about protecting a magical forest from someone who wants to cut it down

Text:
The Guardian of Eldermere

Deep in the southern part of the kingdom stood Eldermere, a forest so valuable that even the king protected it. But when a businessman arrived, he argued that cutting the trees would boost the economy. The villagers were confused—how could something so magical be worthless?

They needed to act fast. The elders organized communication between villages, sharing stories of the forest’s power. Many spent time outdoors, feeling its peace. One day, a child discovered a hidden path leading to the forest’s heart. There, the trees glowed faintly, and everyone felt relaxed, as if the magic itself was guiding them.

The businessman scoffed, but when he followed the path, he became confused, unable to see clearly. The villagers knew what lay ahead: a battle not just for wood, but for the soul of their land. United, they stood firm, ready to protect Eldermere.

Explanation: The path leads to the forest’s heart, symbolizing deeper understanding. Distractors 1–3 misrepresent its purpose (conflict, fear, treasure are not linked to the path).
---

Correct: It disorients him, making him unable to see clearly.
Distractors:
1. It makes him feel relaxed like the villagers.
2. It turns him into an animal.
3. It forces him to apologize.
Explanation: The text says he becomes "confused" and "unable to see clearly." Distractors 1–3 introduce effects not described (relaxation, transformation, apology).
---

Correct: It ensures they can collectively resist the businessman’s threats.
Distractors:
1. It makes the forest’s magic stronger.
2. The businessman respects united groups.
3. They need unity to find the hidden path.
Explanation: Unity allows them to "stand firm" against the businessman. Distractors 1–3 incorrectly link unity to magic, respect, or path-finding, which aren’t supported by the text.



Topic: a story about buying something online that looked completely different from the picture

Text:
My Terrible Online Shopping Experience

Last week, I saw an amazing-looking vase in the "home decor" category on an online store. The price in the quotation seemed reasonable, so I decided to buy it without thinking twice. I even confirmed the order quickly, excited about the deal.

When the package arrived, I was shocked. The vase was completely different from the picture—smaller, uglier, and made of a cheap material. My first emotion was anger. I had wasted money on something unnecessary, and now I had to deal with it.

I tried to contact customer service, but their communication was terrible. They kept asking me to send photos, and I almost lost my tongue in frustration. Finally, I forced myself to return it, but the process took forever.

This experience was a clear symptom of why I avoid online shopping. Next time, I’ll hold my tongue and check reviews first!

Topic: a story about a new app that made my life easier

Text:
My Life After the "EasyDo" App
By Alex Carter

Before I found the EasyDo app, my days felt like a never-ending race. Every morning, my inbox was pouring with emails, and I’d spend hours pulling together orders for my small business. It was unfair—I worked from home, but managing goods and deadlines made me feel like I was back in an office. One evening, my friend mentioned EasyDo, a new app that promised to simplify tasks. Slightly skeptical, I downloaded it.

The first thing I did was order some goods for a client. Instead of waiting for a package to arrive and then manually updating my notes, the app tracked everything. My package was delivered, and I got a notification—no need to pull it out of the mailbox and check it myself. Even my remote work became easier. If I forgot a meeting, the app sent a reminder. Tasks that used to take hours now took minutes.

Life wasn’t normal anymore. Before, my days were pale—just work, stress, and exhaustion. Now, with EasyDo, I had time to breathe. It wasn’t a miracle, but it made my life slightly easier, and that was enough to change my routine forever.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Alex initially feel skeptical about downloading EasyDo?
Correct: He was doubtful because it seemed too good to be true at first.
Distractors:
1. He had heard bad reviews about the app before.
2. His friend had never used the app successfully.
3. He didn’t trust apps that promised to solve all his problems.
Explanation:
The text states Alex was "slightly skeptical" after his friend mentioned the app, implying doubt but not external criticism (Distractor 1) or general distrust (Distractor 3). Distractor 2 is wrong because the friend’s mention suggests they had used it positively. A weak reader might assume skepticism comes from negative experiences.
---

Correct: It automatically tracked deliveries and updated his records without manual input.
Distractors:
1. He no longer needed to check the mailbox for packages.
2. The app delivered his packages faster than before.
3. He could now order goods without using his computer.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s detail about automatic tracking and notifications. Distractor 1 is too absolute—the text doesn’t say he stopped checking the mailbox. Distractor 2 introduces an unsupported claim (speed). Distractor 3 misrepresents the app’s function (no mention of mobile ordering).
---

Correct: His daily routine became more efficient and less stressful.
Distractors:
1. He started working in an office again.
2. The app made his life completely perfect.
3. He had to follow a strict schedule every day.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s contrast between stressful and time to breathe. Distractor 1 contradicts the remote work context. Distractor 2 exaggerates ("completely perfect" vs. "slightly easier"). Distractor 3 misrepresents the app’s benefit (it reduced, not added, rigidity).
---
Q4: Which of these best describes the main benefit of EasyDo for Alex?
Correct: It saved him time by automating repetitive tasks.
Distractors:
1. It helped him make more money from his business.
2. He could now work from anywhere in the world.
3. The app replaced his need for a physical mailbox.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s focus on efficiency and automation. Distractor 1 introduces income (not mentioned). Distractor 2 overgeneralizes ("anywhere in the world" vs. remote work from home). Distractor 3 is unsupported—the text doesn’t claim the mailbox was eliminated.



Topic: a story about finding a magical stone that gives you one wish

Text:
Last weekend, I was hiking in the woods when I found a smooth, gray stone half-buried in the dirt. At first, I thought it was just another imaginary treasure, but something about its shape made it seem special. When I knocked it lightly, it glowed faintly—a sign I originally believed was my tired eyes playing tricks. Still, I couldn’t ignore the obvious magic.

A local elder later told me the stone had been passed down for generations. "Originally, it belonged to a woman who used it to help others," she said. "She never wished for herself, only for the benefit of her village." I laughed—sounding silly, even to myself. Who wouldn’t want wealth or fame?

But as I held the stone, I realized the pleasure of its magic wasn’t in the wish itself, but in the attitude you brought to it. If I wished for something selfish, would it even work? I decided to test it: I knocked twice and whispered, "Let my family be healthy." The stone pulsed warmly. Maybe the type of wish mattered more than the wish itself.

Now, I don’t know if it was real or just my imagination, but I’ll never forget the lesson. Some things are worth more than gold—like knowing your count of blessings is already full.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the narrator hesitate to believe the stone is magical at first?
Correct: They doubted its magic because it seemed too unreal and the glow could be explained by their tired eyes.
Distractors:
1. They were afraid the stone would disappear if they touched it.
2. The stone was too heavy to be magical.
3. They had heard stories about dangerous magical stones before.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures the narrator’s skepticism ("I thought it was imaginary") and rational explanation ("tired eyes"). Distractor 1 ignores the text’s focus on doubt, not fear. Distractor 2 introduces unsupported details (weight). Distractor 3 assumes prior knowledge not in the text. Weak readers might pick these if they focus on glowed without considering attitude.
---
Q2: How does the elder’s story about the stone influence the narrator’s decision?
Correct: The elder’s story made the narrator realize the stone’s power was tied to selfless wishes, not personal gain.
Distractors:
1. It made the narrator angry that someone else had used the stone first.
2. The narrator decided to wish for wealth after hearing the story.
3. The elder warned the narrator not to use the stone at all.
Explanation:
The correct answer links the story ("help others") to the narrator’s reflection ("type of wish"). Distractor 1 misinterprets "I laughed" as anger. Distractor 2 contradicts the narrator’s rejection of selfish wishes. Distractor 3 ignores the elder’s positive framing. Weak readers might choose these if they overlook the narrator’s thoughtful process.
---
Q3: What does the narrator learn about the "type" of wish that might work?
Correct: The narrator realizes that thoughtful, altruistic wishes (for others) are more likely to be granted.
Distractors:
1. Only wishes for invisible things (like health) are granted.
2. The stone grants wishes if you knock it three times.
3. The stone only works for people who are already rich.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "type of wish" and "selfless" from the text. Distractor 1 narrows wishes to intangibles (text doesn’t limit this). Distractor 2 invents a rule (narrator knocks twice). Distractor 3 adds a condition not in the text. Weak readers might pick these if they focus on specific details (health, knock) without inferring the broader lesson.
---
Q4: What is the narrator’s final attitude toward the stone’s magic?
Correct: They value the stone’s lesson about kindness over its literal magic.
Distractors:
1. They are disappointed the stone didn’t grant a visible miracle.
2. They think the stone’s magic is real but boring.
3. They plan to test the stone again immediately.
Explanation:
The correct answer infers the narrator’s growth ("worth more than gold") from the ending. Distractor 1 ignores the positive tone. Distractor 2 misreads "not in the wish itself" as negativity. Distractor 3 assumes an unresolved plot (text ends reflectively). Weak readers might choose these if they expect action or a clear "magic or not" resolution.



Topic: a story about a ghost who lives in an old library

Text:
The Ghost of Blackwood Library
The ghost of Blackwood Library has lived among the shelves for over a century, feeling a deep connection to the books and the building’s history. Once, the library won an award for preserving rare manuscripts, but now it struggles with poverty—fewer visitors and crumbling walls. The ghost possibly helped restore a damaged book decades ago, though no one knows for sure.

At night, the ghost slips along a hidden path between the shelves, feeling a strange charge of energy when the librarian, Mrs. Hargrove, arrives. She is aware of the ghost’s presence but never speaks of it. The ghost is drawn to a mystery: a missing manuscript from the 1800s, possibly hidden to protect it from thieves. The ghost tried to make contact with a visiting scholar but was ignored.

Still, the ghost hopes for improvement. If the library’s funds improve, perhaps its former glory will return. Until then, the ghost watches over the books, a silent guardian of knowledge.

Questions:

Correct: The ghost has lived there for over a century and possibly contributed to preserving its history.
Distractors:
1. The ghost was awarded a key to the library for its service.
2. The ghost is afraid of being discovered and wants to hide.
3. The ghost was once a librarian who died in the building.
Explanation:
- Correct: Paraphrases the ghost’s long-term presence and possible role in restoration.
- Distractor 1: Incorrectly attributes an award to the ghost (library won it).
- Distractor 2: Misinterprets "connection" as fear-based hiding.
- Distractor 3: Unsupported assumption about the ghost’s past identity.
---

Correct: Mrs. Hargrove accepts the ghost’s presence without acknowledging it.
Distractors:
1. Mrs. Hargrove is terrified of the ghost and avoids the library at night.
2. Mrs. Hargrove ignores the ghost because she believes it’s imaginary.
3. Mrs. Hargrove tries to communicate with the ghost using signals.
Explanation:
- Correct: Reflects her aware silence (acceptance, not fear or denial).
- Distractor 1: Contradicts the "charge of energy" (no fear implied).
- Distractor 2: "Aware" ≠ "disbelieving."
- Distractor 3: No evidence of signals; silence is passive.

Correct: The manuscript was possibly hidden to protect it from thieves, and the ghost may have helped preserve it.
Distractors:
1. The manuscript was stolen by a previous librarian who framed the ghost.
2. The ghost hid the manuscript to keep it from being sold.
3. The manuscript contained secrets about the town’s poverty, so it was suppressed.

- Distractor 1: Introduces unsupported drama ("framed").
- Distractor 2: Reverses ghost’s role (observer vs. actor).
- Distractor 3: Misconnects poverty (library’s state) with manuscript’s content.
---
Q4: What evidence in the text suggests the library’s condition has worsened over time?
Correct: The library once won an award but now faces poverty and crumbling walls.
Distractors:
1. The ghost refuses to leave because the library is now too dark.
2. Mrs. Hargrove has stopped maintaining the library due to personal poverty.
3. The missing manuscript is evidence that the library was robbed recently.
Explanation:
- Correct: Contrasts past (award) with present (poverty, crumbling).
- Distractor 1: "Darkness" is not mentioned as a reason for the ghost’s attachment.
- Distractor 2: Poverty refers to the library, not Mrs. Hargrove.
- Distractor 3: "Missing" ≠ proven recent theft (ignores possibly).



Topic: a story about finding a mysterious map hidden in an old book

Text:
The Hidden Map
Liam had always been fascinated by old books, so when his previous aunt died, he inherited her private collection—though most of it was just dusty novels. While cleaning his cluttered garage, he stumbled upon a leather-bound journal hidden behind a stack of tools. Inside, a page had come loose, revealing a mysterious map drawn in faded ink. The map simply showed a winding river and a symbol of a cross, but what made it strange was a rusted chain mark near the bottom.

Liam’s aunt had lived in poverty her whole life, yet she’d always needed something more. The map consisted of only these elements, but the riverbed in the drawing was dry—impossible, since it was still flowing today. The location was close to his hometown, buried in the hills where his family had once farmed. Was the map a clue to hidden treasure? Or just another forgotten relic?

3. The map was part of a famous historical document. / Wrong: It’s a private journal page, not widely known.

Explanation: The correct answer connects the aunt’s poverty and need for "something more" to the map’s potential value. Distractors misread the map’s purpose or overgeneralize its significance.

Distractors:

2. It proves the map is from the 1800s. / Wrong: The text doesn’t specify the era.
3. The chain was used to mark the map’s creator. / Wrong: The chain likely symbolizes a lock or treasure, not a signature.

Explanation: The correct answer interprets the chain as a clue to a hidden object. Distractors misread the chain’s function or era.
---

3. The mapmaker wanted to show a future drought. / Wrong: The map is historical, not predictive.

Explanation: The correct answer notes the riverbed’s dry state vs. its flowing depiction. Distractors misinterpret "dry" as disappearance or pollution.



Topic: a story about a time I saved money for a long time to buy something special

By Maria Rodriguez

When I was ten, my passion for coins began. My grandfather gave me an old iron coin, and I became obsessed. I dreamed of owning the rarest one in the shop window—a original Roman coin worth 50 euros. But I was unable to buy it immediately; it cost more than my monthly pocket money.

For months, I saved every cent. I skipped outdoor games with friends to add coins to my jar. Sometimes, I even held my tongue when my parents offered me money, knowing I’d regret spending it. My siblings laughed, saying I was crazy, but I didn’t care. In comparison to the Roman coin, their toys seemed worthless.

Finally, after six months of working like iron, I had enough. The day I handed the shopkeeper the money was exciting—I felt a sense of release. The coin was even more beautiful in person. Some money remained in my pocket, but my attitude had changed. I realized that patience and discipline could turn dreams into reality.

3. She wanted to impress her grandfather with her discipline.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects Maria’s internal motivation (meaningful purchase). Distractor 1 ignores the jar; 2 misreads holding her tongue as parental control; 3 confuses the initial gift with the lesson learned.

Distractors:
1. They admired her for being so responsible.
2. They tried to save money too after seeing her success.

Explanation:
The text says siblings laughed, implying mockery. Distractor 1 misinterprets laughed as admiration; 2 adds unsupported cause/effect; 3 invents jealousy.

Distractors:
1. Money is more important than friendships.
2. Her grandfather was the best teacher she ever had.
3. She should have bought cheaper coins instead.
Explanation:
The lesson is about self-discipline. Distractor 1 misreads worthless toys; 2 ignores her own effort; 3 contradicts her pride in the rare coin.

Q4: Which word best describes Maria’s feeling when she finally bought the coin?

Distractors:

2. Disappointment.
3. Guilt.
Explanation:
The text describes excitement ("sense of release"). Distractor 1 confuses release with relief; 2 ignores the positive outcome; 3 is unsupported by the text.



Topic: a story about a small step that made a big difference in my life

Text:
By Maria Lopez

When I was approximately ten years old, I climbed a rope for the first time. It was near a statue of a local hero in our town square, and I remember hearing the bell of the church ring as I reached the top. My friends laughed, saying, "You’re brave!" but I didn’t feel scared—I just wanted to do it again. Later, I learned that the statue was mentioned in stories about a ghost who protected the town. No one believed it, but the legend made the square feel magical.

Everyone in our small town was educated, but money was tight. That rope climb was my first taste of success. It gave me confidence, and as a result, I joined the school sports team. Without that small step, I might not have developed the discipline to study later. Now, when I teach children, I always reference that day. I tell them how one moment can change everything. That rope taught me that even the simplest actions can lead to valuable development.

3. She used it to impress the ghost.
Explanation:
The text explains that the rope climb gave Maria confidence, which led to joining the sports team and developing discipline. The other options are unsupported: the rope’s uniqueness, its family connection, or its use to impress the ghost are never mentioned. Distractors:

Every evening at sunset, the island of Luminara disappears, leaving scientists and tourists baffled. Locals are proud of their home’s mystery but frustrated by its impact. Topic: an adventure story about a team of deep-sea explorers who find a functioning ancient mechanism

Text:
The competitive team of deep-sea explorers, led by marine biologist Dr. Elena Vasquez, had spent months searching for lost artifacts. Among them was Liam Carter, a recent graduate in engineering, eager to prove himself. One stormy night, their submarine’s sonar detected something unusual—a tiny metallic object half-buried in the seabed. When they retrieved it, they found a royal mechanism, its surface covered in strange coloured symbols and a faded label that read: "Property of the Crown."

As Liam examined it, the mechanism suddenly clicked into action, its gears whirring to life. However, there was an issue: the power source was unstable, flickering like a dying star. "We ought to report this immediately," Elena said, but before they could decide, their radio crackled with a warning. A criminal group, rumored to be after ancient technologies, had been spotted nearby. The team’s competitive rivalry over who would publish the discovery suddenly felt trivial compared to the danger.

Questions:
Q1: Why was Liam excited to be part of the expedition?
Correct: He wanted to demonstrate his skills as a new engineering graduate.
Distractors:
1. He had always dreamed of finding royal treasures.
2. He was competing with Elena to lead the team.
3. The expedition promised high financial rewards.
Explanation:
The text specifies Liam was a graduate eager to "prove himself," not driven by dreams, leadership rivalry, or money. Distractors 1–3 introduce unsupported motivations.
---
Q2: What problem did the team encounter when the mechanism activated?
Correct: The power source was unstable and flickering.
Distractors:
1. The mechanism emitted a loud noise that damaged the submarine.
2. The symbols on it began to glow dangerously.
3. It required a specific password to operate.
Explanation:
The text describes an "unstable, flickering" power source. Distractors 1–3 are unsupported (no noise, glowing, or passwords mentioned).
---
Q3: How did the team’s discovery create tension between them?
Correct: They argued over who should get credit for the discovery.
Distractors:
1. Elena wanted to destroy it, while Liam wanted to keep it.
2. The criminal group offered to buy it, splitting the team.
3. They disagreed on whether to activate the mechanism at all.
Explanation:
The text notes a competitive rivalry over "who would publish the discovery." Distractors 1–3 misrepresent the conflict (no destroy/keep debate, no sale offer, mechanism was already active).
---
Q4: What does the label on the mechanism suggest about its origin?
Correct: It belonged to a royal owner (e.g., a king or queen).
Distractors:
1. It was created by a criminal organization.
2. The label was a warning about its dangers.
3. It was part of a scientific experiment.
Explanation:
The label reads "Property of the Crown," implying royalty. Distractors 1–3 are contradicted by the text (criminals are later threats, no warning, no science context).



Topic: an adventure story about exploring a cave

Text:
Jake had been unemployed for months, so when he heard rumors about a hidden cave near his village, he decided to explore it. He wasn’t the type of person who carried a compass—just his intelligence and a flashlight. The entrance was covered in thick vines, but inside, the air smelled damp and cool. Strange sounds echoed through the tunnels, like a distant production of dripping water.

As Jake walked deeper, the walls glowed with crystals, and the ceiling was covered in stalactites that looked like frozen waterfalls. He pretended he wasn’t nervous, though the cave seemed to stretch on forever. The only light came from a pool of water except for the faint blue glow of bioluminescent fungi. Then, he turned a corner and saw it: a vast underground court, where stalagmites rose like the pillars of a forgotten palace.

Jake felt a rush of excitement. He picked up a small crystal to remember the day, but then hesitated. What if he got lost? The cave was magical, but he knew he had to leave—forever was too long, even for adventure. Using his intelligence, he marked the walls with chalk and retraced his steps. As he stepped back into the sunlight, he smiled. The cave’s secrets would have to wait for another day.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Jake decide to explore the cave?
Correct: He was unemployed and wanted to escape boredom.
Distractors:
1. He wanted to prove he was brave. / His friend dared him to go inside. / He hoped to find treasure hidden in the cave.
Explanation:
The text states Jake was unemployed and implies he sought adventure ("heard rumors... decided to explore"). The correct answer avoids overinterpreting "pretend" (bravery) or assuming social/treasure motives. Distractors 1–3 are unsupported: bravery is implied but not the reason, no friend is mentioned, and no treasure is described.
---
Q2: What made the cave’s underground chamber special?
Correct: It had towering stalagmites shaped like palace pillars and glowing fungi.
Distractors:
1. It was filled with waterfalls and rivers. / There was a hidden door leading to another cave system. / The air smelled like flowers instead of dampness.
Explanation:
The chamber is described as a "court" with stalagmites and bioluminescent fungi. Distractor 1 confuses stalactites (ceiling) with waterfalls. Distractor 2 introduces unsupported complexity. Distractor 3 contradicts the damp and cool air description.
---
Q3: How did Jake ensure he wouldn’t get lost?
Correct: He marked the walls with chalk to create a map.
Distractors:
1. He followed the sound of dripping water. / He counted his steps to retrace them later. / He asked a local guide for directions.
Explanation:
Jake used his intelligence to "mark the walls with chalk." Distractor 1 ignores the lack of reliability in sounds. Distractor 2 is a common but unsupported strategy. Distractor 3 assumes external help, which isn’t mentioned.
---
Q4: What does “the cave seemed to stretch on forever” suggest about Jake’s experience?
Correct: The cave was so large and confusing that it felt endless.
Distractors:
1. The cave was actually infinite in size. / Jake was afraid of being trapped inside. / The cave was well-lit except for one dark corner.
Explanation:
The phrase is figurative, emphasizing the cave’s vastness. Distractor 1 takes it literally. Distractor 2 misfocuses on fear rather than size. Distractor 3 contradicts the text ("only light came from a pool" + "except for fungi").



Topic: an article about a place that is famous for tourism, focusing on the experience of the people who live there year-round

Text:
By Maria Rodriguez

My village, Santa Clara, sits on a cliff overlooking the sea. For decades, we were just fishermen and producers of olive oil, but now tourism has changed everything. The impact is huge—currently, half our income comes from visitors. My brother, Carlos, is the main organizer of our summer festival, but he doubts whether it’s sustainable. "Too many tourists damage the old streets," he says. "We pray the spirit of our traditions doesn’t sink into forgetfulness."

What makes Santa Clara special is its rare blend of history and nature. Every morning, locals gather at the church to pray for good weather, a tradition that goes back centuries. Meanwhile, our olive producers struggle to compete with mass-produced oils. Carlos says, "We love the visitors, but we must protect what’s ours."

Questions:
