Correct: Tourism has caused damage to the village’s old streets.
Distractors:
1. Tourism has made the village too quiet. (Wrong: Text says tourism brings income, not silence.)
2. Fishing has become more difficult because of tourists. (Wrong: No mention of fishing conflicts.)
3. The village’s traditions have completely disappeared. (Wrong: Text says they doubt traditions will sink, not vanish.)
Explanation: The text explicitly states "Too many tourists damage the old streets." The other options misrepresent the text’s focus on physical damage, economic shifts, or exaggerated tradition loss.
---
Q2: Why does Carlos doubt the future of the festival?
Correct: He worries that too many visitors will harm the village’s traditions.
Distractors:
1. He thinks the festival costs too much to organize. (Wrong: Text mentions damage, not costs.)
2. He believes tourists don’t enjoy the festival. (Wrong: No evidence of visitor dissatisfaction.)
3. He wants to move the festival to a different location. (Wrong: No mention of relocation.)
Explanation: Carlos’s doubt is tied to "damage" and "spirit of traditions." The distractors ignore this context, focusing instead on costs, visitor opinions, or logistics.
---
Q3: How do locals show their cultural traditions?
Correct: They gather at the church to pray for good weather every morning.
Distractors:
1. They perform traditional dances for tourists. (Wrong: No mention of dancing.)
2. They sell handmade crafts in the market. (Wrong: Text focuses on praying, not selling.)
3. They hold competitions for the best olive oil. (Wrong: No mention of competitions.)
Explanation: The text describes "locals gather at the church to pray," a clear cultural practice. Distractors introduce unrelated activities (dancing, selling, competitions).
---
Q4: What challenge do the olive producers face?
Correct: They struggle to compete with mass-produced olive oil.
Distractors:
1. They can’t find enough workers to harvest olives. (Wrong: No labor shortage mentioned.)
2. Tourists buy all the olive oil, leaving none for locals. (Wrong: Text doesn’t link tourism to oil sales.)
3. The government has banned olive production. (Wrong: No policy mentioned.)
Explanation: The text states "struggle to compete with mass-produced oils." Distractors introduce labor issues, tourism-oil conflicts, or government bans—none of which are supported by the text.



Topic: a story about watching the northern lights for the first time

Text:
My First Time Seeing the Northern Lights

Last winter, my friend and I drove for hours to reach a remote cottage near the Arctic Circle. The sky was dark, and the wind howled like a hurricane, but we were determined to see the northern lights. Eventually, just after midnight, the first faint glow appeared on the horizon. It was killing me with its beauty—I had never seen anything like it.

The lights spread across the sky, dancing in shades of green and purple. There was a similarity to a hurricane, but instead of destruction, there was only wonder. I tried to analyse what I was feeling, but words failed me. My friend, usually so calm, was mad with excitement.

A local fisherman gave us a tip: "Wait until the clouds clear." Sure enough, the display grew even more spectacular. By dawn, I understood why people travel thousands of miles just to witness this. It wasn’t just light—it was magic.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator and their friend drive for hours to the cottage?

Distractors:
1. They were running away from a hurricane. / They wanted to test their car’s durability. / They were following a tip about a rare natural phenomenon.
Explanation:
- The text mentions wind but not a hurricane as a reason for fleeing. A weak reader might misread "howled like a hurricane" as a literal storm.
- The car’s durability is never mentioned.
- The "tip" refers to the fisherman’s advice, not the reason for driving.
---
Q2: How did the narrator react emotionally when they first saw the northern lights?
Correct: They were overwhelmed by the beauty and felt speechless.
Distractors:
1. They felt scared because the lights were too bright. / They laughed because the experience was funny. / They immediately wanted to leave.
Explanation:
- The text describes awe ("killing me with beauty"), not fear.
- Laughter is not mentioned.
- There’s no indication they wanted to leave—they stayed to watch.
---
Q3: What did the local fisherman’s advice help them understand?
Correct: That waiting for the clouds to clear could improve the view.
Distractors:
1. The best time to see the lights was at noon. / The lights would disappear if it rained. / They should have brought warmer clothes.
Explanation:
- The text specifies waiting for clouds to clear, not noon.
- Rain is not mentioned.
- Clothing is irrelevant to the advice.
---
Q4: What does the narrator compare the northern lights to, and why?
Correct: A hurricane, because both involve powerful forces, but the lights bring wonder instead of destruction.
Distractors:
1. A firework display, because both are colorful. / A storm, because both are loud. / The sun, because both light up the sky.
Explanation:
- The text explicitly compares the lights to a hurricane ("similarity to a hurricane").
- Fireworks are not mentioned.
- Loudness is not described.
- The sun is not compared—it’s about the effect (wonder vs. destruction).



Topic: a news article about linguists documenting endangered languages

Text:
Linguists Race to Save Dying Languages
When Sarah Chen lost her job as a translator, she never expected to become a language hunter. Now, she travels to remote villages to document endangered tongues, often working heavily under pressure. Last year, in Papua New Guinea, she was amazed to find a dialect where words bend the usual grammar rules—verbs change based on the listener’s mood.

Many linguists used to rely on old tape recorders, but today’s projects, like The Last Words brand, combine technology with tradition. In one village, Sarah met elders who wore colorful costumes during rituals, their speech filled with sounds no one outside the community understood. The elders were brave to let her record, even though outsiders rarely visit.

Globalization is shifting how these languages survive. Some young people speak only the dominant language, but festivals—sometimes held in makeshift stadiums—keep traditions alive. Sarah warns that without urgent action, these voices will disappear forever.

Correct: She took up the work after becoming unemployed as a translator.

3. She was inspired by a documentary about language preservation.
Explanation:
- Correct: Text explicitly links her career change to unemployment ("lost her job as a translator").
- Distractor 1: Incorrect—no mention of lifelong dreams or funding issues.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect—no government involvement is described.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect—her motivation was personal (unemployment), not external inspiration.

Correct: The dialect had unusual grammar rules where verbs change based on the listener’s mood.
Distractors:

2. Speakers used a sign language instead of spoken words.
3. The words sounded identical to a well-known language.
Explanation:
- Correct: Text describes "words bend the usual grammar rules—verbs change based on the listener’s mood."
- Distractor 1: Incorrect—no information about writing systems is provided.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect—only spoken language is mentioned.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect—dialect is described as unique, not similar to another language.

Q3: How do modern projects like The Last Words differ from older methods?
Correct: They combine technology with traditional recording methods.
Distractors:
1. They only use digital tools, rejecting all traditional methods.
2. They focus on teaching the languages to children.

- Correct: Text states "today’s projects... combine technology with tradition."
- Distractor 1: Incorrect—text implies integration, not replacement.
- Distractor 2: Incorrect—no mention of educational programs.
- Distractor 3: Incorrect—goal is preservation, not translation.

Distractors:

2. It forces communities to abandon their traditional costumes.
3. It leads to more festivals, which actually help preserve the languages.

- Correct: Text explains "Globalization is shifting how these languages survive... some young people speak only the dominant language."

Topic: an adventure story about a superhero

Text:
By Alex Carter

The mission was supposed to be simple: steal the villain’s collection of experimental weapons before he used them. But when I found the entry point—a rusted maintenance tunnel behind the old factory—I knew things wouldn’t be easy. The villain, Dr. Vex, had booby-trapped everything. As I bent low to avoid a laser grid, my wrist scanner beeped. My teammate, Mira, warned me over the comms: “There’s a pressure plate near the technical core—one wrong step, and it’ll trigger a killing field!”

I pressed on, but the deeper I went, the more obvious it became that someone had betrayed us. Mira’s signals were delayed, and then—she quit the channel entirely. Alone, I reached the core room, where Vex’s prized invention hummed ominously. It was a disappointing victory; not only had we lost a teammate, but the device was far more advanced than we’d expected.

As I prepared to escape, Vex’s laughter echoed through the tunnels. His arrogance was his downfall—he’d left the exit unguarded, assuming no one would dare challenge him. Little did he know, his feature of overconfidence would be his undoing.

Distractors:
1. Mira is distracted by another mission.
2. The comms system is malfunctioning.

Explanation: The text says “Mira’s signals were delayed, and then—she quit the channel entirely,” implying intentional abandonment, not technical failure or speed issues.
---
Q3: How does Vex’s arrogance contribute to Alex’s success?
Correct: His overconfidence leads him to leave the exit unguarded.
Distractors:
1. Vex underestimates Alex’s technical skills.
2. Vex is distracted by his collection of weapons.
3. Alex uses Vex’s feature of arrogance to trick him.
Explanation: The text states “His arrogance was his downfall—he’d left the exit unguarded,” directly linking his overconfidence to Alex’s advantage. The other options are not supported.

Topic: an adventure story about discovering a twin

Text:
The Twin in the Photo

I had always felt like my life was a performance, but I never knew why. Every weekend, I escaped outdoors, hiking through the woods near my home. One rainy afternoon, I climbed into my grandfather’s attic to avoid the storm. Among old boxes, I found a photography album. Inside was a photo of a boy who looked exactly like me, wearing a faded cloth jacket just like the one I owned.

Confused, I showed the photo to my friend, who said, "That’s weird. Maybe it’s your uncle?" But a tip from the librarian changed everything. "Check the records from 1995," she said. "There’s a boy named Ethan who disappeared from town." My attitude shifted from skepticism to excitement.

I tracked down Ethan’s address. When I knocked on the door, a tall boy answered. "You’re my twin," he said with a smile. I was glad to finally have answers, but Ethan’s story was stranger than mine. His parents had kept him hidden because of a family feud. "We’re not just living next door," Ethan explained. "Our parents made us immediate strangers for years."

Now, I understand why my life always felt like a performance—it was someone else’s script.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Liam feel his life was like a "performance"?
Correct: He felt his life lacked authenticity, as if he were following a script written by others.
Distractors:
1. He was an actor trying out for a play.
2. He was unhappy because he lived alone.
3. He felt pressured to succeed in school.
Explanation:
The text explains Liam’s life felt "like a performance" because he lacked control over his own story (paraphrased from "someone else’s script"). The distractors misinterpret "performance" literally (acting, loneliness, school pressure), ignoring the metaphorical context.
---
Q2: How did Liam discover Ethan’s identity?
Correct: A librarian gave him a tip about town records from 1995.
Distractors:
1. He found Ethan’s name in his grandfather’s diary.
2. Ethan contacted him first after seeing the photo.
3. His friend recognized Ethan from school.
Explanation:
The text states the librarian’s tip led Liam to Ethan’s name in records. Distractors 1 and 3 confuse details (diary vs. album, friend’s speculation), while 2 reverses the order of events.
---
Q3: What was the reason Ethan’s parents kept him hidden?
Correct: They had a family feud that made them separate the twins.
Distractors:
1. Ethan’s parents were afraid of kidnappers.
2. Liam’s parents didn’t want him to have a twin.
3. Ethan was adopted and his parents wanted to protect him.
Explanation:
The text explicitly mentions a family feud as the reason. Distractors 1 and 3 introduce unsupported ideas (kidnappers, adoption), while 2 incorrectly blames Liam’s parents.
---
Q4: What does the phrase "we’re not just living next door" suggest about Liam and Ethan’s relationship?
Correct: Their relationship was shaped by a secret history and separation, not just proximity.
Distractors:
1. They were actually cousins, not twins.
2. Their parents didn’t want them to meet because they hated each other.
3. They were separated by a border and couldn’t communicate.
Explanation:
The phrase implies their connection was deeper than living nearby due to their secret history. Distractors 1 and 3 contradict the text (they are twins; no border mentioned), while 2 misrepresents the feud as personal hatred rather than a conflict between parents.



Topic: a letter to my future self

Text:
Dear Future Me,

I’m writing this from a café by the river, where I used to spend hours watching the water flow. Surely, you’ll have forgotten how much I loved that tiny village located near the mountains—it required patience, but the views treated me like a king. Remember the old oak tree where we’d knock on its trunk for luck? Don’t be careless with those small joys; they’re the weapon against forgetfulness.

If you ever feel lost, reference this letter. The past isn’t just memories; it’s a map. And if you’re ever tempted to ignore the people who matter, ask yourself: Would past me approve?

With love,
Present Me

Questions:

1. "The writer believes small joys can become harmful if not managed." / Wrong: The text frames them as a safeguard, not a risk.
2. "It’s a metaphor for how happiness can disappear suddenly." / Wrong: The focus is on preservation, not sudden loss.
3. "The writer is warning about the dangers of taking life too lightly." / Wrong: No warning tone is present; the emphasis is positive.

Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s metaphor ("weapon against forgetfulness"). Distractor 1 misinterprets the metaphor as negative; 2 ignores the protective framing; 3 introduces an unsupported caution.

Correct: The writer views the past as a guide for making future decisions.
Distractors:

2. "It means the past is unreliable and shouldn’t be trusted." / Wrong: The tone is affirmative, not skeptical.
3. "The writer is saying history will repeat itself if ignored." / Wrong: No cyclical claim is made.

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s use of "map" as guidance. Distractor 1 contradicts the letter’s nostalgic tone; 2 misrepresents the positive framing; 3 introduces an unrelated idea.

Q3: How does the mention of the "old oak tree" contribute to the letter’s tone?
Correct: It adds a sentimental, personal touch by recalling a shared experience.
Distractors:
1. "It symbolizes a place where the writer felt unsafe." / Wrong: The context is positive ("knock for luck").
2. "The tree represents the writer’s isolation from others." / Wrong: The tone is warm, not lonely.

2. "It’s a way to say the past was better and shouldn’t be changed." / Wrong: The letter is forward-looking.
3. "The writer thinks future choices will be judged by external critics." / Wrong: The judgment is internalized ("past me").

Explanation:
The correct answer captures the self-reflective purpose. Distractor 1 confuses self-recognition with values; 2 contradicts the letter’s tone; 3 externalizes the judgment incorrectly.



Topic: a story about a person who inspired me

Text:
The Editor Who Saved My Stories

When I was 19, I sent my first novel to a publisher and got an official rejection letter. The poison of self-doubt crept in—I almost quit writing. Then I met Clara, a famous editor who ran a writing program for young authors. Her talent for spotting raw potential was undeniable, and she saw something in my messy stuff that I’d given up on.

Clara didn’t just praise my work; she helped me shift my thinking. "Your stories are good," she said, "but you’re holding back." At first, I felt embarrassed by her honesty, but her words stuck with me. She pushed me to keep writing, even when I doubted myself. The program she organized was intense—weekly workshops, critiques, and deadlines—but it forced me to grow.

One day, I faced another rejection. This time, instead of quitting, I determined to prove Clara wrong. She had believed in me when no one else did, and that belief became my fuel. Years later, I published my first book. Clara’s push had turned my fear into fire.

2. She paid for their writing classes.
3. She compared their work to famous authors.

1. Her demand that the narrator write faster.
2. Her help in finding a literary agent.

Topic: a story about a lottery winner who donated all the money to charity

Text:
A Lottery Winner’s Generous Choice
By Maria Lopez

When 22-year-old Jake Martinez won $5 million in the lottery last year, his life changed overnight. At first, he considered buying a house or traveling, but his view of money shifted after reading about a policy in his home country that encouraged charity. "Many people would have spent it worse," he said, "but I felt innocent pride in helping others."

Jake claimed the full amount and donated it all to a children’s hospital. "I wanted to deliver something meaningful," he explained. The charity was thrilled, and the story even entertained news outlets for weeks. There’s a similarity between Jake’s act and other young philanthropists, like Malala Yousafzai, who also prioritized others over personal gain.

At first, Jake felt a sense of loss—he had dreamed of a big house—but he knew his choice was right. "I’m glad I didn’t keep it," he said. Now, he volunteers at the hospital, inspiring youth across the country to think beyond themselves.

3. He thought keeping the money would make him feel worse later.

Distractors:

Explanation:

Topic: a story about a very noisy neighbor and how I solved the problem

Text:
By [Your Name]

Living next to Maria was like sharing a wall with a rock concert. Her loud music and late-night parties made me want to sink into the floor. I tried talking to her, but she just laughed and said she’d stop—though she never did. Even the headline in our local newspaper, "Neighbors at War Over Noise," didn’t shame her into changing.

One evening, after another frozen silence when I begged her to turn it down, I snapped. The sunlight reflected off my curtains, mocking my frustration. I couldn’t even breathe without hearing her bass thumping. That night, I decided to take action. I pulled the heavy curtains shut and decorated my windows with thick, soundproofing panels. The change was immediate. My view of the street was blocked, but the peace was worth it.

When Maria asked why I’d changed my windows, I explained the problem. Surprisingly, she apologized and even helped me reflect on how our conflict had spoiled our spirit of neighborliness. The solution attracted praise from others in the building, and for the first time in months, I slept without waking up to noise.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator initially avoid confronting Maria?
Correct: The narrator hoped Maria would stop the noise on her own without direct conflict.
Distractors:
1. Maria threatened the narrator if they complained.
2. The narrator was too shy to speak up.
3. The noise wasn’t actually that bad.
Explanation:
The text shows the narrator tried talking to Maria but she ignored them ("she just laughed and said she’d stop—though she never did"). There’s no mention of threats, shyness, or the noise being mild. A reader might assume conflict implies threats or overlook the extreme description of the noise.
---
Q2: How did the narrator’s solution improve the situation beyond just reducing noise?
Correct: It helped repair the damaged relationship with Maria.
Distractors:
1. It made the narrator’s apartment more attractive to renters.
2. Maria moved out after seeing the changes.
3. The narrator won a local award for creativity.
Explanation:
The text states Maria apologized and they reflected on their relationship, showing reconciliation. The other options are unsupported: no mention of renters, Maria moving, or awards. "Attracted praise" refers to neighbors, not formal recognition.
---
Q3: What does the phrase "the air felt frozen" suggest about the narrator’s emotions?
Correct: The narrator was so angry they felt emotionally paralyzed.
Distractors:
1. The narrator was cold because of the weather.
2. The narrator was excited about solving the problem.
3. The narrator was too tired to react.
Explanation:
The phrase is metaphorical, describing frustration ("I snapped") and despair ("want to sink into the floor"). It’s not literal weather or excitement. A reader might misread "frozen" as exhaustion or overlook the anger context.
---
Q4: Which of these would not have been an effective way to solve the problem?
Correct: B) Ignoring the noise completely.
Distractors:
1. A) Talking to Maria politely.
2. C) Installing soundproofing panels.
3. D) Writing a formal complaint to the landlord.
Explanation:
Ignoring the noise is ineffective because the text shows Maria didn’t respond to direct communication ("she never did [stop]"). Talking (A) was tried but failed, but it’s still a reasonable attempt. Soundproofing (C) worked, and a complaint (D) is plausible but not mentioned—though less effective than the narrator’s creative solution. A reader might overlook that talking failed or assume legal action is always effective.



Topic: a story about sending a message to a wrong person

Text:
By Emma Carter

Last weekend, I was preparing a note for my friend Jake to meet me at the stadium for a concert. The version I wrote was clear: "See you at Gate 3—don’t be late!" But in my hurry, I left it on the board in the community center instead of my personal message board at home.

By the time I arrived, half the crowd thought I was the one who’d forgotten the time. The soil outside the stadium was muddy from the rain, and I slipped on it, nearly losing my breath from embarrassment.

The situation was complex—Jake was annoyed because he thought I’d stood him up, and the stadium staff blamed me for causing a scene. It felt unfair, but I knew I had to fix it. There was a battle ahead: explaining the truth without making things worse.

Topic: a story about how I use modern technologies to learn English

Text:
How Tech Saved My English

In my childhood, I struggled with English grammar. Teachers gave us worksheets, but I often ignored the rules. Everything changed when I discovered modern technology. Now, I use apps like Duolingo and Memrise daily. I created a language-learning account on each platform and fastened my headphones before starting lessons. The shiny new features, like instant feedback, made learning feel like a treatment for my language problems.

However, the internet isn’t always helpful. While studying, a shiny helicopter ad popped up, and I almost ignored my vocabulary list! Apart from distractions, I absolutely recommend these tools. My progress was tracked, and I could review mistakes easily. Even when I felt tired, the apps kept me motivated.

Today, I can’t imagine learning English without technology. It’s not just about apps—I also watch YouTube videos and join online forums. Modern tools have made my language journey smoother apart from the occasional ad break!

Topic: a story about a time I felt truly grateful

Text:
By Alex Rivera

Last winter, I was battling a terrible cold that left me pale and weak. My marathon training had become a measure of my discipline, but my body refused to cooperate. While I lay in bed, my friend Maria donated her evening to bring soup and books—something I hadn’t expected.

Her import to my recovery was the basis of my gratitude. She wasn’t just bringing food; she reminded me of the element of community I’d forgotten. My coach had pushed me to set smaller goals, but Maria’s kindness showed me that support comes in unexpected forms. I realized then how much I’d taken my health—and my friends—for granted.

As I sipped the soup, I felt a warmth that had nothing to do with the broth. Maria’s gesture wasn’t about fixing my cold; it was about reminding me that even in a battle, there are people who care. I’ll never forget that night, when her simple act became the foundation of my strength.

Distractors:

3. The coach was also sick, so he understood the narrator’s struggle.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures the contrast ("goals" vs. "support"). Distractor 1 mixes roles; distractor 2 reverses their actions; distractor 3 introduces unsupported detail.

Correct: The narrator had overlooked how much they depended on others.
Distractors:
1. The narrator had always valued community highly.
2. The narrator thought community was unimportant in sports.
3. Maria was part of the narrator’s training team.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects "I’d forgotten the element of community." Distractor 1 misreads forgotten; distractor 2 adds unsupported context; distractor 3 confuses social and technical roles.

Correct: The narrator was very ill and exhausted.
Distractors:
1. The narrator was injured and couldn’t walk.
2. The narrator was training hard and felt strong.
3. The narrator had just finished the marathon.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "terrible cold... pale and weak." Distractor 1 misinterprets battle; distractor 2 contradicts the text; distractor 3 assumes completion of the marathon.



Topic: a story about an unusual person I met while travelling

Text:
The Man Who Stood Out
By Maria Kowalski

Last summer, while travelling in Lisbon, I met a man who changed my perspective on life. His name was João, and he was an original performer who sang regularly on the streets near the river. What made him stand out was his substance—his voice had a natural flow, and his lyrics were deeply personal, often about injustice. Tourists would stop to listen, but locals would personally avoid him, saying he was mad for wasting his talent.

One evening, I saw him leading a protest against a corrupt politician. He shouted, "This is madness! We need to vote for change!" His boat almost sank in the storm that followed, but he stuck to his beliefs, refusing help until the crowd rescued him. Later, I learned he had been a victim of bad luck—his family had lost everything in a fire, and he blamed the government for not helping.

I was personally affected by his story. João’s passion made me realize how important it is to stand up for what you believe in, even when others think you’re mad.

Questions:
Q1: Why did João perform on the streets?
Correct: He used his talent to raise awareness about injustice.
Distractors:
- He enjoyed singing in front of crowds. / He needed money to survive. / He was trying to become famous.
Explanation:
The text states João’s lyrics were about injustice, so his goal was awareness, not fame or money. A weak reader might focus on "tourists would stop to listen" and assume he performed for enjoyment or attention.
---
Q2: What happened during the protest João organized?
Correct: A storm nearly caused his boat to capsize.
Distractors:
- The police arrested him. / The crowd turned against him. / He was injured by a protester.
Explanation:
The text describes a storm, not police action or violence. A reader might assume protests involve arrests or injuries, ignoring the storm detail.
---
Q3: How did João’s family background influence his beliefs?
Correct: He held the government responsible for his family’s suffering after a fire.
Distractors:
- His family was wealthy before the tragedy. / He believed in passive resistance. / He wanted to leave the country.
Explanation:
The text says his family lost everything, implying poverty, not wealth. "Passive resistance" is incorrect because he led a protest. Emigration isn’t mentioned. A reader might misinterpret "lost everything" as past wealth or assume injustice leads to leaving.
---
Q4: What effect did João have on the narrator?
Correct: His story inspired her to be more courageous in standing up for beliefs.
Distractors:
- She decided to move to Lisbon. / She started performing herself. / She lost interest in protests.
Explanation:
The text says she realized the importance of standing up, not that she moved or performed. A reader might assume a life-changing encounter leads to relocation or action, ignoring the focus on inspiration.

Topic: a story about how I found my career path

Text:
By Maria Rodriguez

When I was young, I had a fear of failing at anything creative. My parents, both immigrants, wanted me to study medicine, but I hated science. In high school, I almost dropped out after an embarrassing mistake in an art assignment—I painted over a classmate’s work by accident. The teacher said I had no talent, and I believed her.

Then, in my junior year, I took a photography class. The first time I held a camera, I felt something click. For my final assignment, I decided to shoot portraits of immigrants in my neighborhood, including my parents. It wasn’t easy—I spent months borrowing cameras and learning on my own. I had almost no resources, but I was determined.

The project turned out better than I expected. I compiled the photos into a small album and wrote a short essay about what the images represented: stories of struggle, hope, and identity. When I showed it to my teacher, she was stunned. "This isn’t just an assignment," she said. "This is art." That was the moment I realized photography wasn’t just a hobby—it could be my career.

Topic: a story about a time I realized I was wrong about something

Text:
Title: The Day I Realized I Was Wrong
Last summer, I was set in my prediction that eastern fashion would never become fashionable. I had always kept eastern and western styles separate, assuming they couldn’t mix. My aim was to prove that traditional eastern clothing was outdated, so I dismissed anything beyond Bollywood-style saris as irrelevant.

One evening, my friend Priya told me about her grandmother’s plot to revive an old weaving technique. "People think it’s just for tourists," she said, "but it’s actually deeply connected to our history." I scoffed—until she showed me photos of modern designers blending eastern patterns with fashionable streetwear. Suddenly, I saw the connection I’d missed. I realized I had been trying to cheat myself by ignoring evidence just to fit my opinion.

It took real effort to admit I was wrong. I spent weeks researching, even visiting a local textile workshop. The plot thickened when I learned the technique Priya described was used in 19th-century eastern royal courts. My aim shifted from criticism to curiosity. Now, I collect books on eastern fashion and even wear a kurtah to parties—something I’d never do before.

Topic: a story about an unusual skill that helped me in life

Text:
The Needle and the Fireworks
When I was a child, I loved making tiny sculptures with a needle and wire. My grandmother taught me to be slow and careful, saying that even small mistakes could have big consequences. One day, while watching fireworks, I noticed something unusual: the way the rockets moved before they exploded. I started following their paths, trying to predict exactly when they would burst.

At first, it seemed like a silly game, but I practiced every summer. Then, when I was 16, I saw a group of fireworks explode near a gas station. If I hadn’t been watching closely, the escape route would have been blocked. My quick thinking helped us all get to safety.

Years later, this skill came in handy again. I worked as a transport inspector, checking for gas leaks in trucks. My ability to spot small changes—like a slow hissing sound or unusual movements—helped me avoid accidents. People often ask how a childhood hobby could be useful, but that’s the beauty of it: sometimes, the most unusual skills save your life.

2. Because their grandmother asked them to.
3. To become a professional fireworks designer.

Distractors:
1. The narrator was injured by flying debris.

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s warning about blocked escape routes. Distractor 1 assumes injury; distractor 2 introduces an unrelated fire; distractor 3 implies family conflict, which isn’t mentioned.

Correct: It helped them detect gas leaks by observing small changes.
Distractors:
1. It allowed them to repair trucks more quickly.
2. They used it to predict accidents before they happened.
3. Their skill made them a better leader for their team.

Topic: a story about a time capsule

Text:
A Time Capsule for the Future
Last year, my friends and I decided to create a time capsule. We wanted to bury something that future generations could pull out in 20 years. The battle over where to store it was fierce—some wanted it in my uncle’s garage, but others argued it needed to be somewhere official, like under the school’s court garden. In the end, the school management chose a spot near the library, which was convenient for everyone.

We included basic supplies like old photos, letters, and a USB with music. My friend Jake personally added his favorite book, saying, “This isn’t commercial—it’s for history!” We sealed it in a waterproof box and buried it on June 1st. When we retire from school in five years, we’ll dig it up to add more items before hiding it again.

I personally think this project will be more meaningful than any commercial souvenir. It’s a way to leave a mark on the future.

Q4: What is the main reason the author gives for why the time capsule is special?
Correct: It allows them to leave a meaningful legacy for future generations.
Distractors:
1. It will be more valuable than commercial souvenirs. / The author enjoys digging up old items. / It’s a school requirement for all students.
Explanation:
The author calls it “more meaningful” and “a way to leave a mark,” not “valuable” or “enjoyable.” Distractor 1 confuses “special” with “expensive”; distractor 2 ignores the legacy focus; distractor 3 contradicts the voluntary nature.



Topic: a story about finding money on the street

Text:
Finding a Fortune on the Street
Last week, while walking near the harbor, I found a wallet on the pavement. It wasn’t just any wallet—it was decorated with gold embroidery, and inside were £500 in old grade banknotes. The owner’s ID said his name was James Carter, a sailor who worked on a ship docked nearby. I knew I had to prove it was mine, but how?

First, I checked the age of the money—some bills were from the 1990s, so they weren’t new. Then, I thought about visiting the ship’s branch office to ask if anyone recognized James. But necessarily, I had to act fast before someone else found it. I even considered a scientific approach, like checking fingerprints, but that seemed too complicated.

In the end, I decided to keep it temporarily while I figured out what to do. I moved the wallet to my desk, where it wouldn’t get lost. The experience made me think about honesty—would I return it, or was this my lucky break? Maybe I’d expand my savings, but I still felt guilty.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator initially think about visiting the ship’s branch office?
Correct: To ask if anyone recognized the owner, James Carter.
Distractors:
1. To return the wallet immediately.
2. To ask for a reward for finding it.
3. To check if the ship’s crew had reported it stolen.
Explanation:
The text states the narrator wanted to "ask if anyone recognized James," not return it or seek a reward. A weak reader might assume "branch office" implies returning it (Distractor 1) or assume the wallet was stolen (Distractor 3), but the text focuses on identifying the owner.
---
Q2: What does the phrase "old grade banknotes" suggest about the money?
Correct: The money was rare or valuable because it was from an earlier time.
Distractors:
1. The money was damaged or torn.
2. The bills were counterfeit.
3. The money belonged to a specific historical period.
Explanation:
"Grade" refers to the condition/value of collectible items (e.g., old coins). Distractor 1 ignores this; Distractor 2 contradicts the text (no mention of forgery); Distractor 3 is partially true but too narrow—the focus is on value, not just history.
---
Q3: How did the narrator’s plan to use a scientific method fail to be practical?
Correct: It was too complicated or time-consuming to carry out.
Distractors:
1. The narrator didn’t have the necessary equipment.
2. Scientific methods are always unreliable for this situation.
3. The money was already spent by someone else.
Explanation:
The text says the method "seemed too complicated," not that tools were missing (Distractor 1) or science is unreliable (Distractor 2). Distractor 3 is unsupported—the wallet was found intact.
---
Q4: What two opposing feelings did the narrator experience after deciding to keep the money?
Correct: Guilt and excitement (or temptation).
Distractors:
1. Anger and relief.
2. Fear and curiosity.
3. Pride and sadness.
Explanation:
The text explicitly mentions "felt guilty" and implies temptation ("lucky break"). Distractor 1 introduces anger (not mentioned); Distractor 2’s curiosity is irrelevant; Distractor 3’s pride is unsupported.



Topic: a story about an unusual reading club

Text:
The Backwards Book Club

Our reading club is different. Instead of starting at the first page, we read books backwards—from the last sentence to the first. The idea came from our coach, a retired literature teacher who believed central themes often hide in the ending.

At first, members suffered from confusion. "How can we understand the story if we don’t know the beginning?" they asked. But after a few sessions, they began to appreciate the twist. Reading backwards forces us to focus on clues and predictions, not just plot.

We meet directly after work in a quiet café. The rules are simple: give honest feedback, no interruptions, and proper discussion etiquette. Some say it’s silly, but the club is ours, and we love the challenge.

One member, Sarah, used to hate reading. Now she says, "I see books differently." Maybe that’s the point—great stories reveal themselves in unexpected ways.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the club read books from the last page?
Correct: To encourage readers to analyze the story’s clues and make predictions about what happens earlier.
Distractors:
1. To save time by skipping unnecessary details at the beginning.
   - Explanation: The text mentions confusion at first, not efficiency. The goal is analysis, not speed.
2. Because the coach thinks endings are more interesting than beginnings.
   - Explanation: The coach believes central themes hide in endings, not that endings are inherently more interesting.
3. To confuse readers and make discussions more difficult.
   - Explanation: The text says members appreciate the challenge, implying it’s intentional but not malicious.
---
Q2: How did Sarah’s attitude toward reading change?
Correct: She now enjoys reading and finds books more interesting because she sees them in a new way.
Distractors:
1. She started reading faster because the backwards method is easier.
   - Explanation: The text says she used to hate reading, not that the method made it easier.
2. She now only reads books with strong endings.
   - Explanation: The text doesn’t mention a preference for endings—just that her perspective changed.
3. She stopped reading entirely because the club’s method was too confusing.
   - Explanation: The text says she now sees books differently, implying she still reads.
---
Q3: What is one rule the club follows during discussions?
Correct: Members must give honest feedback without interrupting others.
Distractors:
1. Everyone must read aloud from their book.
   - Explanation: The text doesn’t mention reading aloud—only discussing.
2. Only the coach is allowed to speak first.
   - Explanation: The text describes feedback rules, not speaking order.
3. Members must bring snacks to share during meetings.
   - Explanation: The text doesn’t mention food or social rules beyond discussion etiquette.
---
Q4: What does the coach believe about book endings?
Correct: That important themes in a book are often revealed in the ending.
Distractors:
1. That endings should always be happy to make the book enjoyable.
   - Explanation: The text doesn’t specify happiness—only that central themes hide there.
2. That readers should ignore endings and focus only on the beginning.
   - Explanation: The opposite is true—they read backwards to study endings.
3. That endings are unimportant and can be skipped.
   - Explanation: The coach’s belief is that endings contain key ideas, not that they’re unimportant.



Topic: a story about a person who can become invisible

Text:
By Clara Hartwell

When I was twelve, I noticed something strange about myself. During a school race, I could simply disappear whenever I wanted. At first, I thought it was a trick of the light, but I soon realized I could vanish at will. I used this ability regularly—first to avoid chores, then to sneak into the school library after hours.

One evening, I decided to use my power for something more exciting. My crush, Emma, was attending a romantic concert in the park. Surely, no one would suspect me if I followed her unseen. I watched as she laughed with friends, her face glowing under the lights. It was the closest I’d ever felt to her.

But my invisibility had consequences. While spying, I accidentally knocked over a table, damaging a famous musician’s guitar. The nation’s newspapers called it a "mysterious accident." I felt guilty—was this an achievement or a curse? Possibly, my ability came from an old family legend, but I’d never known anyone else who could do it.

Now, I wonder if I should use my power at all. It’s changed my life, but not always for the better.

Questions:

Correct: She noticed it during a school race when she could disappear at will.
Distractors:
- She saw her reflection disappear in a mirror. / She realized it after reading about a similar case in a book. / She accidentally vanished while running away from a bully.
Explanation:
The text explicitly states she realized her ability "During a school race." The other options introduce elements not present in the story (mirror, book, bully).
---
Q2: How did Clara use her invisibility in a romantic way?
Correct: She followed her crush, Emma, to a concert in the park.
Distractors:
- She sent Emma invisible love letters. / She used her power to make Emma fall in love with her. / She spied on Emma’s ex-boyfriend to gather information.
Explanation:
The text describes Clara following Emma to a concert ("I decided to use my power... to follow her unseen"). The other options imply actions not mentioned (letters, manipulation, ex-boyfriend).
---
Q3: What unintended problem did Clara cause while using her power?
Correct: She accidentally knocked over a table and damaged a musician’s guitar.
Distractors:
- She tripped and hurt herself. / She made Emma’s friends think she was a ghost. / She was caught by security and banned from the park.
Explanation:
The text says she "knocked over a table, damaging a famous musician’s guitar." The other options are unsupported (injury, ghost story, security ban).
---
Q4: What does Clara suggest might be the origin of her ability?
Correct: She thinks it might come from an old family legend.
Distractors:
- She believes it’s a side effect of a scientific experiment. / She suspects it’s a government experiment gone wrong. / She thinks it’s a punishment for a past life sin.
Explanation:
The text states, "Possibly, my ability came from an old family legend." The other options introduce unrelated ideas (science, government, karma).



Topic: a story about a competition in a magic school

Text:
By Elara Moonstone

The entrance to the Grand Magic Competition was hidden behind a curtain of illusions. Every year, students from the Arcane Academy competed in challenges that tested their skills—poetry, potion-making, and even illusions. This year, the final task was a duel: contestants had to recite a spell while performing a magical act.

Liam, a quiet student with a talent for poetry, was nervous. His opponent, a bold sorcerer named Kael, smirked as the rules were announced. "To earn ten points, you must bury a diamond beneath the enchanted garden before sunrise. Rarely does anyone succeed."

A photographer, assigned to document the event, snapped pictures of the contestants. Meanwhile, Liam studied the garden. The diamond was small but heavy, and the soil was thick with magic. He dug carefully, but the moment he placed the gem underground, the earth trembled. The diamond vanished—not buried, but erased from reality.

Kael laughed. "You failed! The rules say the diamond must stay hidden!" But the judges hesitated. "The total count of points isn’t just about winning," said Headmaster Veyra. "It’s about creativity. Liam’s spell changed the diamond’s nature—that’s worth five points!"

In the end, Kael won, but Liam’s unique approach earned him praise. The photographer later wrote in her notes: "Magic isn’t just about following rules. Sometimes, you have to see beyond them."

Questions:
Q1: Why was Liam nervous during the competition?
Correct: He was worried about performing well in the poetry-and-magic duel.
Distractors:
1. He had never competed in a magic duel before. / Kael had threatened to use dark magic against him. / He was afraid the enchanted garden would trap him.
Explanation:
The text states Liam was "nervous" during the duel, not because of inexperience, threats, or garden dangers. The correct answer focuses on his anxiety about the task itself.
---
Q2: What happened to the diamond when Liam tried to bury it?
Correct: It disappeared from reality instead of being buried.
Distractors:
1. It turned into a different magical object. / The enchanted garden absorbed it permanently. / Kael stole it before Liam could bury it.
Explanation:
The text says the diamond "vanished—not buried, but erased from reality." Distractors 1 and 2 misinterpret vanished as transformed or absorbed, while 3 contradicts the timeline (Kael reacted after the event).
---
Q3: How did the judges decide to award Liam points despite his failure?
Correct: They valued his creative spellwork, even if it didn’t follow the rules exactly.
Distractors:
1. They realized the diamond was fake and couldn’t be buried. / Liam’s poetry was so moving that it convinced them. / Kael admitted Liam was more talented and deserved points.
Explanation:
The judges praised Liam’s "creativity" and "spell" (not poetry or diamond authenticity). Distractors 1 and 2 misattribute the reasoning, while 3 contradicts Kael’s reaction ("Kael laughed").
---
Q4: What lesson did the photographer learn from the competition?
Correct: Magic isn’t just about strict rules—sometimes you need to think differently.
Distractors:
1. Photographers should also learn magic to capture spells better. / Winning isn’t everything; creativity matters more. / The diamond was more valuable than the competition’s rules.
Explanation:
The photographer’s note emphasizes "seeing beyond rules" and "creativity." Distractors 1 and 2 add unsupported details, while 3 misrepresents the lesson (rules vs. creativity, not diamond value).



Topic: a personal story about introducing random choices into my life

Text:
"Introducing Random Choices"
By Alex Carter

I used to plan everything—my days, my meals, even my career in engineering. But one day, I decided to try something different: random choices. My first experiment was baking. I followed a recipe properly, but I burned the pot and ruined the cake. It was a disaster! Still, I was absolutely determined to keep trying.

Next, I let chance decide my weekend plans. I wrote destinations on slips of paper and drew one from a hat. The first choice was a tour of a small town. It was absolutely charming, and I met a leading local artist. My life started to shine in ways I hadn’t expected.

Even small luxuries, like choosing a coffee break instead of working, felt fresher. Now, I use a simple method: when stuck, I flip a coin or spin a pen. It’s not always perfect, but it keeps things exciting. Random choices turned my life into an adventure.

Distractors:

3. Alex read a book that convinced him to try spontaneity.
Explanation:
- The text states Alex "used to plan everything" and "decided to try something different," so the correct answer aligns with breaking over-planning. Distractors introduce unsupported details (friend, job loss, book).

Correct: Alex ruined the cake by burning the pot.
Distractors:
1. Alex forgot the ingredients and had to start over.
2. The cake was edible but tasted terrible.
3. Alex’s oven broke while baking.

- The text explicitly says "I burned the pot and ruined the cake." Distractors misrepresent the cause (forgotten ingredients, broken oven) or understate the failure (edible but terrible).
---

Correct: Alex wrote destinations on paper and drew one randomly.
Distractors:
1. Alex asked a travel agent for a surprise recommendation.
2. Alex flipped a coin to choose between two pre-selected options.
3. Alex let a friend pick the destination for him.
Explanation:
- The text describes "writing destinations on slips of paper and drawing one from a hat," so the correct answer matches this method. Distractors introduce unrelated decision-making processes (agent, coin flip, friend’s choice).
---
Q4: What effect did random choices have on Alex’s life?
Correct: Alex’s life became more exciting and enjoyable.
Distractors:
1. Alex gave up his engineering career completely.
2. Random choices made Alex’s life more stressful.
3. Alex’s friends thought his new approach was foolish.
Explanation:
- The text states "My life started to shine... It keeps things exciting," supporting the correct answer. Distractors contradict the positive tone (career quit, stress, friends’ disapproval) or introduce unsupported claims.



Topic: a personal story about trying unusual time-management techniques

"Managing Time the Unusual Way"
By Alex Carter

I had always struggled with time, so when I read about a religious monk who used prayer and strict routines to determine his day, I decided to try it. At first, I doubted it would work—I’m not religious! But I followed his advice: I recorded my goals on an old tape recorder every morning and repeated them like a mantra. The continuous focus was exhausting, though. I felt like I was caught in a net, unable to bend the rules.

My biggest difficulty was balancing this with my usual life. One day, I channeled my frustration into action: I kept a reference book nearby to adjust the method. Slowly, I learned to live by a schedule that suited me, not just the monk’s. Now, I use parts of his technique—just without the prayers!

Topic: a story about a person moving from the city to the countryside

Moving to the Countryside

Last year, I decided to leave the city for the countryside. The noise and pollution had made it hard to take a breath of fresh air, so I packed my bags and moved to a small village. Slowly, I adjusted. I set up a small garden behind my house, and watching the vegetables grow was fascinating. The way traditions had spread through generations in the village amazed me. I became engaged in local festivals, helping with the harvest and learning about the area’s history. Similarly, the slower pace of life suited me better than the rush of the city.

I was grateful for the change, though some things took time to understand. The village was known for its organic production, and I learned to appreciate the hard work behind every loaf of bread or jar of jam. Moving here wasn’t always easy, but I wouldn’t trade it for anything.

Topic: a detective story about deciphering a letter

Text:
The Coded Confession
By Detective Cole

When the letter arrived at the station, it was labeled as harmless—a confession from a suspect named Victor. Generally, detectives trust written evidence, but Inspector Cole wasn’t convinced. The handwriting was neat, almost too perfect, and the words seemed rehearsed. "This might be a trick," he muttered, though no one bothered to listen at first.

Victor had participated in a recent campaign against smuggling, and his sudden confession seemed like a break in the case. However, Cole noticed something strange: the ink had faded in certain spots, as if someone had revised parts of the letter. When he examined it under light, hidden words appeared—accusing another man of the crime.

Cole realized the letter was misleading. Victor, who had refused to participate in further questioning, was actually innocent. The real culprit had played a clever trick, using Victor’s name to avoid suspicion. The detective’s effective campaign to decode the letter finally uncovered the truth: Victor was guilty of nothing, while the smuggler remained at large.

Explanation:

2. The letter was traced back to the real culprit’s handwriting.
3. Victor confessed to the crime under pressure.

Topic: a story about unusual trends in interior design

Unusual Trends in Interior Design

Interior design trends are evolving rapidly, with some ideas making headlines for their boldness. One highly unusual choice is covering walls in leather, which gives a rough yet luxurious look. While traditional decor uses fabric or paint, leather adds a unique texture that represents a break from convention.

Another trend involves "living walls," where plants grow in soil embedded in the wall. These designs not only look stunning but also improve air quality. In comparison to standard wallpaper, they offer a natural and sustainable alternative.

As one designer put it, "People no longer want generic spaces—they crave something that tells a story." This sentiment explains why even helicopter landing pads are appearing in luxury homes, blending functionality with futuristic aesthetics.

Technology is also assisting in these innovations. 3D printers now create custom furniture and decor, allowing homeowners to experiment with shapes and materials. From leather to soil to helicopters, these trends prove that interior design is no longer about following rules—it’s about pushing boundaries.

Topic: an adventure story about a servant who overhears her master's secret

Text:
Clara’s Secret
Clara had worked for Lord Whitmore for five years, but his totally secretive behavior in the western estate unnerved her. One evening, she overheard him speaking to a man about a large sum from the bank. Curious, she noticed a small measure of wood hidden beneath his desk. Though educated beyond most servants, Clara knew something was wrong.

The next day, she explored the neighbourhood, where whispers of smuggling reached her ears. Back at the estate, she found a frozen ledger in the attic—proof of Lord Whitmore’s crimes. His talented lies about "investments" were effective, but Clara’s sharp mind uncovered the truth: stolen jewels and embezzled funds.

Confronting him, she revealed her findings. Lord Whitmore, usually impressive in his composure, was totally shocked. He offered a bribe, but Clara’s effective plan had already ensured the ledger was copied. Justice would come, and for once, the servant had outsmarted her master.

Questions:
Q1: Why does Clara suspect Lord Whitmore is hiding something?
Correct: She combines her overheard conversation about the bank with the hidden measure and uses her education to realize his behavior is suspicious.
Distractors:
A) She catches him stealing money from the bank.
B) She is envious of his expensive possessions.
C) Local rumors about smuggling make her suspicious.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states she overhears a conversation about money and finds a hidden object, then uses her intelligence to suspect foul play.
- A: Incorrect—she hears a conversation, not a theft.
- B: Incorrect—no mention of jealousy.
- C: Incorrect—she investigates the neighbourhood after suspecting him, not because of rumors.
---
Q2: How does Clara’s education help her uncover the truth?
Correct: It allows her to recognize the complexity of his lies and interpret the ledger’s contents accurately.
Distractors:
A) It helps her read the ledger quickly and accurately.
B) She uses her knowledge to create fake evidence against him.
C) Her education gives her the confidence to argue with him directly.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text implies her education helps her understand the ledger’s significance, not just read it.
- A: Partially true but oversimplified—her education aids interpretation, not just reading speed.
- B: Incorrect—she copies the ledger honestly.
- C: Incorrect—her confidence isn’t the focus; her analysis is key.
---
Q3: What does the "frozen ledger" symbolize in the story?
Correct: It represents untouched evidence that reveals the full extent of Lord Whitmore’s crimes.
Distractors:
A) The ledger is physically frozen in ice, hiding the crimes.
B) It proves the crimes ended when the ledger was hidden.
C) The ledger was stored in a cold, locked room.
Explanation:
- Correct: "Frozen" is metaphorical—evidence preserved over time.
- A: Incorrect—no literal ice is mentioned.
- B: Incorrect—the ledger shows ongoing crimes, not an end.
- C: Incorrect—no mention of a cold room; it’s in the attic.
---
Q4: What does Lord Whitmore’s reaction to Clara’s discovery reveal about his character?
Correct: His shock and attempt to bribe her show he is desperate to hide his guilt and fears exposure.
Distractors:
A) He is proud that Clara finally understands his business.
B) He is relieved the secret is safe with her.
C) He respects her intelligence but refuses to pay her.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text describes his shock and bribe attempt, indicating fear and desperation.
- A: Incorrect—he is shocked, not proud.
- B: Incorrect—he tries to bribe her, not trust her.
- C: Incorrect—he offers a bribe, implying fear, not respect.



Topic: a story about a deep-sea biologist who discovered a new ecosystem

Text:
Title: "A Deep-Sea Discovery That Changed Everything"

Dr. Elena Vasquez had always been qualified for extreme expeditions, but nothing prepared her for the deep-sea mission. Her love for teamwork came from her rugby days, though she’d never imagined using it to study marine life. The planning had been rushed, and on the first day, the sonar equipment failed—an embarrassing mistake that delayed the team.

Undeterred, Elena adjusted their plot, moving backwards through the planned route to avoid a storm. The cold was brutal; at one point, she nearly froze as her suit malfunctioned. Then, they found it: a glowing ecosystem unlike anything recorded. It was a kind of coral reef that thrived in near-freezing temperatures, defying all scientific models.

Elena felt a huge responsibility to determine its significance. If they hadn’t adapted, they would have missed this discovery entirely. Back at the lab, she spent months documenting the findings, proving that even the most qualified scientists must stay flexible.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Dr. Vasquez adjust the expedition’s route?
Correct: To avoid the storm after equipment failure.
Distractors:
1. To retrace her steps because she got lost.
2. Because the team voted to change direction.
3. To find a safer area for diving.
Explanation:
The text states she moved backwards to avoid a storm ("adjusted their plot... to avoid a storm"). "Got lost" is incorrect because the route change was strategic. "Team voted" is unsupported—her decision was individual. "Safer area" is vague; the storm was the specific threat.
---
Q2: What made the discovery of the coral reef surprising?
Correct: It thrived in near-freezing temperatures, unlike known reefs.
Distractors:
1. It was the first ecosystem ever discovered in the deep sea.
2. The reef emitted a strange glow that no one expected.
3. Dr. Vasquez had predicted this ecosystem before the expedition.
Explanation:
The reef’s temperature tolerance was the key surprise ("thrived in near-freezing temperatures"). It wasn’t the first ecosystem, just a new kind. The glow was noted but not the main surprise. The text implies the discovery was unexpected ("unlike anything recorded"), not predicted.
---
Q3: How did Dr. Vasquez’s rugby experience help her?
Correct: It taught her the importance of teamwork under pressure.
Distractors:
1. It helped her navigate the deep-sea equipment.
2. She used rugby tactics to plan the expedition route.
3. Her rugby background made her more physically strong for the cold.
Explanation:
The text links rugby to teamwork ("love for teamwork came from her rugby days"). Navigation/equipment and physical strength are not mentioned. "Tactics for planning" is incorrect—route changes were due to equipment failure.
---
Q4: What lesson did Dr. Vasquez learn from this expedition?
Correct: Even highly qualified scientists must adapt to unexpected challenges.
Distractors:
1. That deep-sea expeditions are always dangerous.
2. She should have spent more time planning before the trip.
3. Teamwork is more important than individual qualifications.
Explanation:
The lesson is adaptability ("must stay flexible"). Danger is implied but not the focus. While planning was rushed, the lesson is broader. Teamwork was helpful but not prioritized over qualifications in the conclusion.



Topic: a story of a person who turned their hobby into a successful business

Text:
From Hobby to Business: Lena’s Candle Story
Lena always loved making candles—it helped her define her identity after a frightening job loss. At first, her friends laughed, saying no one would pay for homemade candles. But Lena ignored the authority of their doubts. She began selling at local markets, and obviously, it wasn’t easy. Customers, though, were grateful for her creations, and soon orders spread by word of mouth.

To keep up, Lena added scented soaps plus new candle designs. Within a year, she had approximately 500 regular customers. Last month, she hosted a celebration event, realizing that her hobby had turned into a success. "I’m so happy I didn’t give up," she said, smiling.

- Distractor 1: No workshops are mentioned.

Topic: a review of a museum exhibition

Text:
By Clara Hart
This exhibition kicks off with a bold statement: medieval engineering wasn’t just about castles—it was an art form. The centerpiece is a 17th-century priest’s journal, discovered during an Arctic expedition, detailing how monks designed bridges. It’s not just fashionable—it’s thought-provoking.

The first impression is overwhelming. A import from Italy, a mechanical clock, dominates the room, while smaller artifacts, like a hanging model of a waterwheel, might be ignored. The curators clearly aimed to create an escape from modern life, and they succeeded—though some displays feel overcrowded.

One highlight is the section on medieval engineering, where interactive screens let visitors "build" a cathedral. However, the paintings hang too high for comfort, and the text panels are too small. In my view, this exhibit is worth visiting, especially for history buffs or families. Just don’t expect a quiet experience—it’s loud, busy, and brilliant.

3. Visitors can learn about Arctic exploration and its connection to medieval Europe.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states the exhibit covers medieval engineering as its core theme.
- Distractor 1: "Fashionable" is descriptive, not the focus.
- Distractor 2: The journal is one example, not the sole focus.
- Distractor 3: The Arctic expedition is context, not the main topic.
---

Distractors:
1. The text panels are too complex for casual visitors.

3. Most visitors are only interested in the large clock import.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says smaller artifacts might be ignored due to placement.
- Distractor 1: The issue is size/placement, not complexity.
- Distractor 2: Overcrowding is a weakness, but not the reason for overlooking details.
- Distractor 3: The clock is impressive, but the text doesn’t say visitors ignore other things because of it.
---
Q3: What does the reviewer suggest about the clock imported from Italy?
Correct: It could have been smuggled into Europe.
Distractors:
1. It was the most valuable artifact in the entire exhibit.
2. The clock was originally designed for a church in Italy.
3. It’s the only artifact that wasn’t discovered during an expedition.
Explanation:
- Correct: The phrase "might have been smuggled" implies speculation about its origin.
- Distractor 1: "Masterpiece" doesn’t equal most valuable.
- Distractor 2: Original purpose isn’t specified.
- Distractor 3: The journal was found on an expedition; the clock’s origin isn’t tied to one.
---
Q4: Who would benefit most from visiting this exhibition?
Correct: History enthusiasts or families would enjoy it most.
Distractors:

2. Art historians would find the priest’s journal the most useful.
3. Engineers would learn the most from the interactive screens.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text recommends it for history buffs or families.
- Distractor 1: The exhibit is loud and busy, not ideal for avoiding crowds.

Topic: an adventure story about a person who survived on an uninhabited island

Text:
Stranded in Paradise
Growing up in poverty, I dreamed of escape. One stormy night, my boat washed up on a remote island in the eastern Pacific. There were no standard amenities—just sand, palm trees, and the endless ocean. At first, survival seemed impossible, but I found an effective way to catch fish with a spear made from driftwood.

Wild birds would participate in my daily routine, stealing my bait if I wasn’t careful. I carved a property line into the sand to mark my territory. The location, in the eastern Pacific, was ideal in some ways—the eastern side had better winds for sailing—but water was scarce. I had to switch from fishing to hunting crabs near the shore.

One day, I joked that I paid my tax in coconuts. The island had a similarity to a desert, but the ocean kept me from thirst. After months, a passing ship rescued me. I’ll never forget how that tiny speck of land taught me more than any classroom ever could.

Questions:
Q1: Why did the narrator end up on the island?
Correct: He was shipwrecked there after fleeing poverty.
Distractors:
1. He was searching for treasure and got lost.
2. He was exiled from his country for a crime.
3. He was testing a new sailing route.
Explanation:
The text states he "washed up" after a storm while escaping poverty, not treasure hunting or exile. "Testing a sailing route" contradicts the accidental nature of his arrival.
---
Q2: How did the narrator initially survive on the island?
Correct: He caught fish using a spear made from driftwood.
Distractors:
1. He ate coconuts and drank rainwater.
2. He hunted birds that stole his food.
3. He built a shelter from palm leaves.
Explanation:
The text specifies fishing with a spear as the initial survival method. Coconuts and rainwater aren’t confirmed as primary sources, and shelter-building isn’t mentioned.
---
Q3: What problem did the narrator face after fishing became difficult?
Correct: He had to switch to hunting crabs because water was scarce.
Distractors:
1. He ran out of spear wood and couldn’t hunt.
2. The birds attacked him when he tried to fish.
3. He got sick from eating too many crabs.
Explanation:
The text explains he switched to crabs due to water scarcity, not tool shortages or health issues. Birds were pests, not threats.
---
Q4: What does the phrase "paid my tax in coconuts" suggest about the narrator’s attitude?
Correct: He was joking about the island’s isolation and lack of civilization.
Distractors:
1. He believed coconuts were valuable currency.
2. He was trying to attract the attention of passing ships.
3. He felt guilty for taking resources from the island.
Explanation:
The phrase is clearly humorous, referencing the island’s lack of real taxes or civilization. It’s not about currency, signals, or guilt.



Topic: an article about a person who collects unusual things

Text:
The Collector of Forgotten Sounds
Elena has always been drawn to unusual things, but her true passion lies in musical oddities. It started upon discovering a stack of yellowed sheet music in a second-hand shop. "I rarely see items like this," she says, "so I knew I had to keep them." Over time, her collection grew to include vintage records, broken pianos, and even a musical box that played a tune no one recognized.

Elena divides her collection into sections: playable instruments, historical documents, and "projects" (items she plans to restore). She documents every piece in a journal, noting where she found it and why it matters. "These must be properly stored," she insists, "or they’ll be lost forever." Yet, she admits it’s unfair how little these treasures are valued. "Museums ignore them," she says, "but to me, they’re a bank of forgotten sounds."

Her favour goes to broken instruments. "Most people throw them away," she explains. "But if you listen closely, you can hear their stories." Whether it’s a piano missing keys or a record with scratched grooves, Elena sees potential. "I don’t collect for profit," she adds. "I collect because music deserves to be remembered."

Questions:
Q1: Why does Elena keep a journal?
Correct: To record the origins and importance of each item in her collection.
Distractors:
1. To track the cost of each item for resale. / Profit motive contradicts text.
2. To list the locations where she purchased the items. / Incomplete; misses "why it matters."
3. To categorize items by their musical era. / Confuses journaling with dividing.

Explanation:
The text states she "documents every piece... noting where she found it and why it matters." The correct answer captures both where and why. Distractor 1 is wrong because she "doesn’t collect for profit." Distractor 2 ignores the purpose (history/significance). Distractor 3 misrepresents dividing (physical) as journaling.
---
Q2: What does Elena think about how museums treat her collection?
Correct: She believes museums do not appreciate their historical significance.
Distractors:
1. She thinks museums should purchase more items from her. / Text criticizes valuation, not buying.
2. She feels museums evaluate her items fairly. / Direct contradiction ("it’s unfair").
3. She hopes museums will exhibit her items more frequently. / Text focuses on value, not display.

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "it’s unfair how little these treasures are valued." Distractor 1 misinterprets "ignore them" as a call to buy. Distractor 2 ignores the negative tone. Distractor 3 introduces an unrelated idea (exhibition).
---
Q3: How does Elena feel about broken instruments?
Correct: She has a special fondness for them because they hold hidden stories.
Distractors:
1. She prefers them because they are simpler to repair. / Text mentions "projects" but not ease.
2. She collects them because they are less expensive. / Contradicts "most people throw them away" (implies rarity).
3. She believes they are more valuable than working instruments. / Text implies low perceived value ("throw them away").

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects "her favour goes to broken instruments... you can hear their stories." Distractor 1 adds unsupported detail. Distractor 2 misreads rarity as cheapness. Distractor 3 reverses the text’s implication (broken = less valued).
---
Q4: What might happen if Elena doesn’t store her items properly?
Correct: They could be damaged or lost over time.
Distractors:
1. They would increase in monetary value. / Contradicts "lost forever" (implies degradation).
2. Museums would show more interest in acquiring them. / No link between storage and museum interest.
3. She would need to reorganize how she divides her collection. / Logical gap; dividing is separate from storage.

Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases "must be properly stored... or they’ll be lost forever." Distractor 1 misinterprets "forgotten" as appreciation. Distractor 2 creates a false cause-effect. Distractor 3 confuses dividing (organization) with storage (preservation).



Topic: a story about how I proposed a creative idea at work

Text:
A Diamond Idea
By Alex Carter

I worked in a small but creative studio where we whenever had brainstorming sessions to improve our campaigns. Our latest project was dull—just another ad for a local shop. One day, I visited the central mall and saw a jewelry store with a stunning diamond display. It struck me: why not make our campaign feel like a luxury experience?

I pitched the idea to my team. At first, they were skeptical. "It’s too risky," said Jamie. "Clients won’t get it." I still believed in it, though. The diamond wasn’t just a gem—it was the perfectly timed twist our campaign needed. After some debate, they decided to pass on my suggestion and test it with one client.

To our surprise, the client loved it! They called it a pleasant change from typical ads. Within weeks, we won three new contracts. My team admitted I’d been right all along. That diamond idea wasn’t just a spark—it was the key to our success.

Questions:
Q1: Why did Alex choose the diamond as inspiration for the campaign?

Distractors:
1. He had always wanted to work with diamonds in his designs. / Why wrong? The text mentions a mall visit, not personal interest.
2. The diamond represented the client’s logo. / Why wrong? The diamond was a metaphor for luxury, not a logo.
3. His team suggested using a diamond to make the campaign stand out. / Why wrong? The idea was Alex’s; the team was skeptical.
---
Q2: What was the team’s initial reaction to Alex’s idea?
Correct: They thought it was too risky and doubted clients would understand it.
Distractors:
1. They immediately agreed it was a brilliant idea. / Why wrong? The text says they were "skeptical."
2. They asked Alex to redesign the diamond concept. / Why wrong? They debated but didn’t request changes.
3. They compared the diamond idea to past failed campaigns. / Why wrong? The text doesn’t mention past failures.
---
Q3: How did the client respond to the diamond campaign?
Correct: The client was delighted and found it a refreshing change.
Distractors:
1. The client rejected the idea because it was too expensive. / Why wrong? The client loved it.
2. The client asked for more diamond-themed ads in the future. / Why wrong? No mention of future orders.
3. The client thought the diamond idea was too similar to competitors’ ads. / Why wrong? The text says it was a pleasant change.
---
Q4: What lesson did Alex’s team learn from this experience?

Distractors:
1. They realized they should have trusted Alex from the beginning. / Why wrong? No blame implied.
2. They decided to focus only on luxury brands moving forward. / Why wrong? No shift in strategy mentioned.

Topic: an article about one of the most unusual cities in the world

Text:
Timbuktu, a city in the Sahara Desert, is indeed one of the world’s most unusual places. Its warm climate and remote location make it seem like a mirage, yet it was once the heart of trans-Saharan trade. For centuries, caravans traveled across the desert to exchange gold, salt, and slaves, bringing wealth and knowledge to the city.

Timbuktu’s complex history includes its role as a center of Islamic scholarship. Scholars wrote thousands of manuscripts by hand, covering science, law, and poetry. Today, a row of ancient mud-brick buildings, including the Sankore University, confirm its past glory. However, preserving this heritage is challenging. In the last decade, efforts have been made to update protection methods, but sandstorms and poverty threaten its ruins.

Tourism is growing, but locals worry about treatment of the site. Some fear mass visits will damage the delicate manuscripts. Despite these challenges, Timbuktu remains a symbol of resilience—a city where the past and present coexist in the desert sun.

1. The city’s desert climate made it difficult to live in.
2. It was destroyed and rebuilt multiple times.
3. The manuscripts were written in many different languages.
Explanation: The text highlights Timbuktu’s functions (trade + scholarship). Distractor 1 confuses climate with history; 2–3 are unsupported.
---

Correct: By updating protection methods over the past decade.
Distractors:
1. Building new museums to display the manuscripts.
2. Restricting all tourism to protect the site.
3. Digging up ruins to move them to safer locations.

2. The city lacks infrastructure to handle visitors.
3. Tourists prefer other desert cities like Marrakech.

Correct: It still preserves its historical traditions today.
Distractors:
