Topic: a humorous classification of different types of people in a public library or co-working space

Text:
Public libraries and co-working spaces are fundamentally the same: a gathering of people who remarkably share the same goal—silence—yet behave in ways that illustrate the chaos of human nature. If you’ve ever noticed the visible divide between those who treat the space as a sanctuary and those who turn it into a social experiment, you’ll appreciate these classifications.

First, there’s the "Treasure-Hoarder"—the person who stacks books like a dragon guarding gold. Their necessity for headphones isn’t to focus; it’s to drown out the wind of change (i.e., the librarian shushing them). Then comes the "Book Slip"—someone who slips between shelves, muttering to themselves as if decoding ancient manuscripts. Their excuse for not working? "Research."

Finally, the "Strict Librarian" (or co-working enforcer) exists to maintain order, though their rules are fundamentally arbitrary—like banning laughter but allowing loud phone calls. To illustrate, one might see them glaring at a "Silent Storm" (the person typing furiously while sipping coffee like a caffeine-fueled hurricane).

What these types share is a shared delusion: that they’re the only ones breaking the rules. Remarkably, we all recognize ourselves in at least one of them.

Topic: a reflective essay on the experience of impostor syndrome in a new professional or social role

Text:
When I accepted the promotion to team lead, I felt like the grand opening of my career had arrived. I was delighted with the opportunity, yet something gnawed at me—an unsettling sense that I was a fraud. This was the edition of my life where impostor syndrome reared its head, and I soon learned it had many components: overpreparing for meetings, fearing exposure as a "phony," and a crippling need to prove myself.

The first time it hit was during my first team meeting. Sitting in the chair at the head of the table, I felt like a bat flapping in the dark, my ideas sounding hollow despite months of preparation. Furthermore, my hands trembled as I handed out the agenda, a physical manifestation of my internal tragedy. Looking back, I realize my upbringing shaped this response—growing up in a household where perfection was expected left me with no foundation for self-acceptance.

Over time, I learned to reframe the narrative. The "tragedy" wasn’t my inadequacy; it was my brain’s way of highlighting gaps I could fill. What started as a paralyzing fear became a component of my growth, teaching me that even the most grand roles come with vulnerabilities.

Topic: an alternate history story where a key technological breakthrough happened 50 years later than it did

Text:
In an alternate 1995, artificial intelligence remained a distant dream. The crucial breakthrough in machine learning, which in our timeline revolutionized industries in the 1940s, was delayed until the 1990s due to political disputes and lack of investment. This shift reshaped the world entirely. In the small town of Meadowgrove, nearby to a now-abandoned research facility, life moved at a snail’s pace. The local residents, accustomed to outdated technology, struggled with a rating system that still ranked progress by manual labor efficiency.

The delay sparked public offence—many saw the slow pace as a national embarrassment. Without AI-driven automation, factories relied on expensive loans to stay operational, and the government scrambled to establish a new legal framework for intellectual property. By the late 1990s, Meadowgrove’s economy teetered on the edge of collapse, its future hanging like a cliff over a stormy sea. Unlike our timeline, where AI transformed healthcare, education, and transportation, this world remained entirely dependent on human effort, leaving society in a state of perpetual catch-up.

1. Assume relocation incentives (Distractor 1), though no such policy is described.
2. Confuse framework with vocational training (Distractor 2), misinterpreting the term.
3. Infer trade restrictions (Distractor 3), which are unrelated to the text’s focus.



Topic: a story about a portrait painter who realizes they are capturing the subject's soul

Text:
Elias Voss was a newly celebrated portrait painter, his stunning works adorning galleries in high artistic circles. Yet, despite his success, he often felt unfulfilled—until the day he painted Clara Delacroix.

While he worked, he realized that the lighting wasn’t just illuminating the subject’s face but revealing something deeper. Clara’s eyes, usually cold and distant, seemed to shift subtly, as if alive. Elias hesitated, convinced his technique was incorrect. But then it struck him: he wasn’t capturing her features—he was painting her soul, beating like a living organ beneath her skin. The portrait was unique in its emotional depth, something he had never achieved before.

The thought terrified him. What if he dared to show it? Clara might not understand. Worse, she might reject it. For a moment, Elias considered resigning from his career entirely. But the pull of creation was too strong.

When Clara saw the finished piece, she was satisfied. "It’s the most lifelike portrait I’ve ever seen," she remarked, unaware of the storm Elias had weathered to create it. He stood in silence, overwhelmed—not by her praise, but by the realization that he had glimpsed something unique in her, something no one else could see.

Questions:
Q1: Why does Elias initially believe his technique is incorrect?
Correct: He perceives an unnatural emotional intensity in Clara’s eyes that defies conventional portraiture.
Distractors:
- The lighting in the studio was poorly adjusted, distorting Clara’s features.
- Clara’s expressions contradicted her usual reserved demeanor, confusing him.
- He mistakenly thought his brushstrokes were too bold for a realistic portrait.
Explanation:
The text states Elias "hesitated, convinced his technique was incorrect" because Clara’s eyes "seemed to shift subtly, as if alive." This suggests he is unsettled by the emotional depth, not technical flaws (lighting) or Clara’s behavior (contradicted demeanor). A weak reader might blame the lighting or assume her expressions were inconsistent, but the focus is on the soulful quality of her gaze.
---
Q2: What does Elias compare Clara’s soul to, and why?
Correct: He likens it to a vital, pulsating force beneath her exterior because it feels dynamic and alive.
Distractors:
- He compares it to a frozen statue because her expressions were unchanging.
- He compares it to a shadow because it was intangible and elusive.
- He compares it to a mirror because it reflected his own artistic emotions.
Explanation:
The text explicitly says: "he was painting her soul, beating like a living organ beneath her skin." This is a metaphor for vitality. A weak reader might confuse "living" with stillness (frozen statue) or misinterpret the intangible nature of soul as a shadow. The mirror option is incorrect because Elias is not reflecting himself but capturing Clara’s essence.
---
Q3: Why does Elias consider resigning from his career?
Correct: He fears Clara will misunderstand or reject the portrait’s profound meaning if she understands its true depth.
Distractors:
- He believes his discovery is too spiritually dangerous to share with the public.
- He feels guilty for exploiting Clara’s trust during the sittings.
- He wants to abandon painting entirely to pursue sculpture.
Explanation:
The text explains his fear: "What if he dared to show it? Clara might not understand." His hesitation is about her reaction, not public danger or ethical guilt. A weak reader might assume his resignation stems from broader consequences (dangerous to share) or a personal moral crisis (exploiting trust), but the text focuses on Clara’s specific response.
---
Q4: How does Clara react to the portrait, and what does this reveal about Elias’s realization?
Correct: Her approval highlights that Elias’s artistic breakthrough remains unseen and unappreciated by others.
Distractors:
- She is disturbed by the portrait’s intensity and asks Elias to destroy it immediately.
- She recognizes the portrait as a deception and demands an explanation for its unnatural quality.
- She offers Elias a lucrative commission for a series of similar works.
Explanation:
The text states Clara is "satisfied" and calls it "the most lifelike portrait I’ve ever seen," with no mention of distress or deception. Her reaction contrasts with Elias’s private revelation, showing his insight is hidden from her. A weak reader might assume she detects something unnatural (disturbed by intensity) or challenges the work (recognizes deception), but the text supports only her naïve admiration.
---
Q5: What does the phrase "unique in its emotional depth" suggest about Elias’s artistic breakthrough?
Correct: His work transcends traditional portraiture by revealing an unseen, soulful dimension of his subject.
Distractors:
- He has perfected a new painting technique that other artists will attempt to replicate.
- He has accidentally created a forgery that mimics Clara’s soul with eerie accuracy.
- He has discovered a hidden emotional depth in Clara that others had overlooked.
Explanation:
The phrase emphasizes originality and depth, not technical skill (replicable technique) or deception (forgery). A weak reader might conflate "unique" with replicability (others will copy) or misinterpret the emotional capture as Clara’s hidden trait rather than Elias’s achievement. The text clearly attributes the uniqueness to Elias’s artistic revelation, not Clara’s qualities.



Topic: a psychological mystery about a person who finds a photograph of themselves from a school they never attended

Text:
Lena nearly dropped her coffee when she saw the photo: a fabulous black-and-white image of herself at age twelve, standing in front of a school she had never attended. The caption read "St. Mary’s Academy, Class of 2008"—but St. Mary’s burned down in 2005, years before Lena was born. The tragic irony left her breathless. How could this exist?

Her life had always been a struggle for independence, despite working low-wage jobs and battling healthcare costs. She had read about distribution errors in old yearbooks, but this was different. The photo’s detail was too precise—the school’s architecture, her former uniform—seemingly real. When she called the school’s booking office, they confirmed no Lena Carter had ever enrolled. Yet, the photo was undeniably her.

A former student, Mark, claimed he remembered her from orientation day. "You were quiet," he said. "But you looked just like this." Lena had no memory of him. Media outlets quickly made her the target of theories: a hoax, a repressed memory, or even a government experiment. Her exposure to the story skyrocketed, but none of it explained the photo’s origin.

Skeptical but haunted, Lena visited a psychologist, who suggested the photo might be fabricated or linked to a tragic case of mistaken identity. "Your brain might be filling gaps," the doctor said. Lena left with a booking for therapy, wondering if her independence of mind was now the biggest mystery of all.

Questions:
Q1: What does the word "tragic" primarily emphasize in the context of Lena’s photo discovery?
Correct: The impossible, ironic existence of the photo given Lena’s age and the school’s history.
Distractors:
1. It describes the sad expression on Lena’s face in the photo.
2. It refers to the school’s tragic history (burning down).
3. It suggests Lena’s life has been tragically difficult.
Explanation:
The correct answer focuses on the paradox (Lena can’t be in a school that predates her birth). Distractor 1 ignores the contextual meaning of tragic. Distractor 2 misinterprets tragic as referring to the school’s fate rather than the photo’s impossibility. Distractor 3 conflates tragic with Lena’s financial struggles, which are separate from the photo’s discovery.
---
Q2: Why does Lena’s struggle for independence make her skeptical about the photo’s origin?
Correct: She prefers rational, verifiable explanations and distrusts external or supernatural claims.
Distractors:
1. She fears losing control over her personal story if the photo is real.
2. It shows she doesn’t believe in psychological theories like repressed memories.
3. She associates independence with financial freedom, not truth-seeking.
Explanation:
The correct answer ties independence to her skepticism of unproven narratives. Distractor 1 is plausible but overpsychologizes—the text doesn’t mention control as a motive. Distractor 2 is wrong because the text doesn’t say she rejects psychology outright (she visits a psychologist). Distractor 3 misrepresents independence as purely financial, ignoring its intellectual/emotional dimension.

Q3: How does the distribution of the yearbooks relate to the mystery of the photo?
Correct: It suggests the photo may have been included by mistake or fabricated during mass printing.
Distractors:
1. It proves the photo is fake because yearbooks are always accurate.
2. It means the photo was deliberately planted in Lena’s life.
3. It shows the school distributed photos to former students.
Explanation:
The correct answer links distribution errors to possible fabrication. Distractor 1 is wrong because the text doesn’t claim yearbooks are always accurate—it mentions errors exist. Distractor 2 is unsupported (no evidence of deliberate planting). Distractor 3 is factually incorrect—the school burned down, so no distributions occurred.
---
Q4: What does the psychologist’s suggestion about "repressed memory" imply about Lena’s psychological state?
Correct: She may have unconscious mental blocks preventing her from accepting the photo’s reality.
Distractors:
1. She is suffering from a dissociative identity disorder.
2. The photo is a hallucination caused by stress.
3. She has always had unreliable memories.
Explanation:
The correct answer focuses on repressed memory as a psychological defense. Distractor 1 is overdiagnostic—the text doesn’t mention DID. Distractor 2 is wrong because repressed memory ≠ hallucination. Distractor 3 is unsupported—the text doesn’t suggest chronic memory issues, only a specific gap.
---
Q5: Why might the media’s focus on Lena make her the target of conspiracy theories?
Correct: The photo’s inexplicable nature invites wild speculation, making her a symbol for unexplained phenomena.
Distractors:
1. Because she has a history of being involved in conspiracies.
2. The media enjoys sensationalizing ordinary people’s problems.
3. She looks like a famous person, leading to identity confusion.
Explanation:
The correct answer ties the photo’s mystery to media exploitation. Distractor 1 is wrong—no history is mentioned. Distractor 2 is too cynical and unsupported. Distractor 3 is incorrect—the text doesn’t suggest resemblance to a celebrity.



Topic: an adventure story about teenagers who find themselves inside a computer game

Aisha rolled up her sleeve to check the strange device on her wrist. "The game says we’re in Sector 7," she said. "There’s an exit overseas—somewhere in virtual Tokyo." Liam, ever impulsive, grabbed a glowing sword from the air and chopped through a nearby barrier. "No time to panic!" he shouted. Jake, however, kept glancing at his status bar, which flickered ominously.

"Is this even genuine?" Aisha wondered aloud. "What if we’re stuck forever?" Liam laughed. "Then we’ll just have to win big—imagine the money we’d make if this was real! We’d pay off our mortgage in a week!" Jake groaned, but the joke lightened the mood as they pressed onward, the game’s rules still unknown.

Topic: a story about a difficult ethical dilemma I faced

Text:
Last year, I faced a dilemma that left me feeling naked in my own integrity. My boss, a dynamic leader, asked me to impose a new policy that seemed unfair to junior staff. Standing alongside my colleagues in the meeting, I watched their expressions darken as he outlined the changes. The problem wasn’t just the policy—it was the way it was being imposed: without consultation, in a narrow corridor of time.

I hesitated, torn between loyalty and the urge to expose the truth. My boss had always been fair, but this felt wrong. Meanwhile, a colleague whispered, "What’s predictable is that someone will get blamed for this." The tension arose when I realized the policy would disproportionately affect new hires, many of whom were already struggling. I bent toward honesty, but fear of backlash made me pause.

In the end, I spoke up—but not as I’d planned. Instead of confronting my boss directly, I framed the concerns as a "team discussion" to refine the policy. It wasn’t perfect, but it arose from collaboration, not coercion. The experience taught me that ethical dilemmas aren’t black and white; they’re dynamic, and the right path often lies in how you navigate the narrow spaces between options.

Questions:

Correct: To emphasize its coercive, top-down enforcement.
Distractors:
1. "It suggests the policy was implemented quickly." / "The author disliked the policy’s details." / "It was a short-term solution."
Explanation: "Imposed" conveys authority, not speed (distractor 1), content (distractor 2), or temporality (distractor 3). A weak reader might conflate "narrow corridor of time" with haste or assume "impose" means "reject."
---
Q2: What does "felt naked in my own integrity" suggest about the author’s emotional state?
Correct: They felt morally exposed and uncertain.
Distractors:
1. "They were physically vulnerable." / "They lacked professional confidence." / "They regretted past actions."
Explanation: "Naked" is metaphorical (distractor 1). Distractor 2 misreads integrity as competence. Distractor 3 ignores the present-tense dilemma.
---
Q3: How does the colleague’s comment ("What’s predictable is that someone will get blamed") reflect the workplace culture?
Correct: It implies systemic blame-shifting rather than accountability.
Distractors:
1. "The workplace values honesty." / "Colleagues feared the boss." / "Policies are always well-designed."
Explanation: The comment is sarcastic (distractor 1). Distractor 2 confuses blame with fear. Distractor 3 contradicts the negative tone.
---
Q4: Why did the author choose to frame concerns as a "team discussion"?
Correct: To avoid direct confrontation and encourage collective problem-solving.
Distractors:
1. "To appear more authoritative." / "The boss preferred group feedback." / "It was their only remaining option."
Explanation: No evidence of status (distractor 1), boss’s preference (distractor 2), or desperation (distractor 3). The author had agency.
---
Q5: What lesson does the author learn about ethical dilemmas?
Correct: They require navigating ambiguity and balancing competing values.
Distractors:
1. "Trust your instincts." / "Bosses are untrustworthy." / "Policies should be ignored."
Explanation: The text focuses on process (distractor 1), not generalizations (distractors 2–3). The author seeks refinement, not rejection.



Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was no longer a child

Text:
The summer I turned twelve, I spent my afternoons at the old dairy near my grandmother’s house, helping her bottle milk. It was a simple creation of routine—fill, cap, stack—but I loved the rhythm of it. That year, though, everything felt different. One afternoon, I noticed a tear in the quilt my mother had made for me. "I’ll fix it," I said, pulling out the needle and thread. My hands shook. The fabric frayed more as I pulled, and suddenly, I understood: I couldn’t do it right. Not like before.

The confusion hit me like a wave. For years, I’d relied on others—my mother to mend clothes, my father to fix the bicycle chain. But now, standing there with the quilt in my lap, I realized I was no longer a settler in my childhood home. I was a visitor, and the rules had changed. That evening, I stitched the quilt anyway, my stitches uneven but deliberate. It became my first true creation—a symbol of my reduction in dependence.

The finding came later, during my university thesis on coming-of-age stories. I wrote about the moment we stop seeing ourselves as children, when the world shifts from a place of comfort to a combination of challenge and possibility. That torn quilt was my first lesson: growing up wasn’t about losing childhood, but about learning to carry its fabric with you, even when it unraveled.

Questions:

Correct: To establish a sense of childhood routine and contrast it with the moment of change.
Distractors:
1. To describe the author’s favorite childhood activities.
2. To show that the author was skilled at household tasks.
3. To introduce a conflict between the author and their grandmother.
Explanation:
The correct answer fits because the dairy and quilt frame childhood, while the shift ("everything felt different") introduces the realization. Distractor 1 ignores the contrast; 2 contradicts the author’s confusion about inability; 3 is unsupported (no conflict is mentioned).
---
Q2: How does the torn quilt symbolize the author’s realization about growing up?
Correct: It represented the author’s inability to rely on others for simple tasks, marking the end of childhood dependence.
Distractors:
1. It showed the author was now responsible for household chores.
2. The quilt’s damage represented the author’s fear of failure.
3. It was a gift from the author’s mother, symbolizing lost innocence.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the confusion and reduction in dependence. Distractor 1 oversimplifies (chores ≠ realization); 2 adds unsupported emotion; 3 misreads the quilt’s role (it’s about creation, not nostalgia).
---
Q3: What does the phrase "I was no longer a settler" imply about the author’s perspective?
Correct: The author no longer saw themselves as permanently rooted in childhood but as someone transitioning to adulthood.
Distractors:
1. The author felt like an outsider in their own home.
2. The author had moved away from their childhood home.
3. The author was now a permanent adult with no childhood left.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures the metaphorical shift ("visitor" vs. settler). Distractor 1 is vague; 2 is literal (no move is mentioned); 3 contradicts the idea of "carrying fabric forward."
---
Q4: In what way was the quilt a "true creation" for the author?
Correct: It was the first thing the author made independently, despite imperfections, symbolizing growth.
Distractors:
1. It was the first thing the author made perfectly.
2. It was a gift that represented the author’s childhood.
3. The quilt’s imperfections made it more valuable to the author.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches "uneven but deliberate" and the reduction in dependence. Distractor 1 contradicts "uneven"; 2 misreads the quilt’s role; 3 is subjective and unsupported.
---
Q5: What is the author’s thesis about coming-of-age stories, and how does it connect to the quilt?
Correct: The author argues that growing up involves accepting that childhood comforts diminish, but one carries its essence forward.
Distractors:
1. Coming of age means completely leaving behind childhood memories.
2. The process involves sudden, dramatic changes.
3. It’s about replacing childhood with adult responsibilities.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects "carry its fabric with you." Distractor 1 contradicts the combination of old/new; 2 ignores the gradual shift; 3 oversimplifies the text’s nuance.

"The biggest misconception people have about me is that I’m serious all the time. When I walk into a room, some assume I’ll demand leadership and control, but the truth is hilarious—I’ve never been one to hide in a cave of rigidity. The moral of the story wasn’t just about the incident itself but how I responded. At first, I tried to alter my behavior—avoiding certain topics, laughing at jokes I didn’t understand—but the friction of pretending to be someone I wasn’t rubbed off on me. I realized that my attempt to fit in was so desperate that even a slight comment could make me feel like an outsider, which wasn’t appropriate.

What helped was recognizing that some experiences are relevant to my growth, even if they’re uncomfortable. Instead of shrinking, I decided to launch a new version of myself—one that embraced my differences rather than hiding them. Over time, the racial tensions in the group eased as I stopped trying to conform and started contributing my unique perspective.

Topic: a personal story about a conversation that fundamentally altered a key relationship

Text:
The friendship with Mark had become dull, a habit of shared lunches and polite small talk, masking a desire for something more. Our last argument began over a bill—I’d forgotten to pay my share of the coffee order, a trivial sum, but Mark’s silence at the table felt heavier than any offence I’d caused before. That evening, I questioned my agency in the situation. Had I avoided conflict to preserve the dull status quo? My old thesis from grad school resurfaced: "Conflict reveals truth." I called Mark and admitted I’d taken the bill as a sign of his disapproval, but the real issue was my fear of offence. He laughed—"It was just coffee!"—but then paused. "You’re right. I had been avoiding the conversation." The placement of blame shifted; I’d assumed his silence was judgment, but it was his own agency at work. By the next week, trust was restored. The experience was beneficial in ways I hadn’t anticipated, like seeing the globe after years of staring at a single street. I’d paid the bill for honesty with years of silence, but the view was worth it.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the narrator describe their friendship as dull at the beginning?
Correct: The narrator uses dull to highlight the lack of emotional depth in the relationship.
Distractors:

Topic: a story about a time I discovered a secret that changed my perception of my family

Text:
When I was twelve, I discovered a secret that reshaped how I saw my family. It happened one evening while my senior grandmother was cooking. The kitchen filled with the steam of boiling vegetables, and I lingered, curious about the protein-rich stew she always prepared. That night, however, I overheard her whispering to my uncle: "The timing has to be perfect, or they’ll notice."

At first, I assumed she was talking about the recipe. But the next day, I found her extensively researching food banks in the newspaper. My tendency to ignore adult conversations backfired—I realized her secret wasn’t just about money, but about how she had to work extensively to assure our family’s stability. She had hidden her assistance to elderly neighbors, fearing we’d see her as weak.

Moreover, her secrecy made me question everything. Why had she never asked for help? Was it pride, or a bet on our resilience? The revelation stung, but it also explained her exhaustion. I no longer saw her as just a cook—I saw her as a pillar, holding up our home with quiet strength.

Distractors:
1. She was planning to donate money to the food bank.

Distractors:
1. It made her suspicious of her grandmother’s behavior.

Explanation: The tendency is passive; the revelation is accidental. Options 1/3 introduce unsupported emotions; Option 2 overstates the impact.

Distractors:

Explanation: The grandmother’s secrecy was protective (Option 1 is wrong). Options 2/3 introduce unsupported emotional/behavioral shifts.



Topic: a story about the most challenging conversation I ever had to initiate

Text:
The most challenging conversation I ever had to initiate was telling my best friend I couldn’t feed his business with lies anymore. The motivation to do it was there, but the fear of ruining our friendship felt like the weight of a tonne pressing down on me. I kept bombing the attempt—postponing, rehearsing, then chickening out—until I realized avoidance would be harmful.

The date I’d set for the talk kept slipping, as if time itself was conspiring against me. Every time I imagined the words, my stomach twisted. He was charming, effortlessly disarming, and I knew my honesty might shatter that. Yet, I couldn’t unravel the chain of events that had led me to this point: his reckless spending, my silent complicity, and the looming disaster.

Finally, I picked up the phone. My voice cracked as I confessed, "I can’t do this anymore." The conversation took a sharp curve when he laughed—not in anger, but relief. "I’ve been waiting for someone to say that." He’d already sought assistance from a financial advisor. The motivation I’d lacked suddenly crystallized: honesty wasn’t just brave; it was the only way forward.

Distractors:
1. They lacked the motivation to start it.

The speaker had motivation (they wanted honesty) but avoided action due to fear. The date slipping was psychological, not logistical. The friend’s reaction was unknown; the speaker’s anxiety was about their own impact, not the friend’s actions.

Distractors:

2. The friend’s curve in behavior made the conversation unpredictable.
3. They thought the friend would feed them false hope.
Explanation:
The speaker feared their own guilt, not the friend’s reaction. "Curve" describes the conversation’s twist, not the friend’s actions. The friend’s honesty was the issue, not deception.
---
Q3: What does "the conversation took a sharp curve" suggest?
Correct: The conversation’s direction changed unexpectedly (e.g., became easier).
Distractors:
1. The speaker’s voice curved with emotion.
2. The friend’s arguments were harmful to the speaker’s confidence.
3. The chain of events led to a bombing of the plan.
Explanation:
"Curve" is a metaphor for the dialogue’s unexpected turn (positive). The speaker’s voice wasn’t described as curving, and the friend’s reaction was relief, not harm. "Bombing" was the speaker’s earlier failure.

3. Their friend’s charming personality was the real motivation.
Explanation:

Topic: a story about inheriting a meaningful but mysterious object from a relative

Text:
When my aunt passed away, I inherited a small, curved silver locket from her attic—a gift from her own aunt, who had died decades earlier. At first, I felt only miserable and bitter, resenting the weight of another object I couldn’t seem to shake. I examined it closely, turning it over in my hands, but the engraving was faded, and the tiny keyhole seemed useless. Desperately, I tried to pry it open, but it resisted. That night, I left it on my desk, its silence unnerving me.

The next morning, I noticed something strange: a faint inscription on the back, barely visible. As I cleaned it with a soft cloth, the words emerged—"For when the storm passes." My curiosity shifted from frustration to fascination. Gradually, I pieced together the story: my aunt’s aunt had carried it through a war, and it had been her only comfort. She had whispered to it, "This will bring you peace," though she never found it.

I spent weeks researching, but the mystery only deepened. Still, there was a strange victory in holding something that had survived so much. The locket wasn’t just an object; it was a bridge to a life I’d never know, and in its silence, I found a kind of understanding.

Correct: They resent inheriting another emotionally burdensome object.
Distractors:
- They were angry that the locket was damaged. / They disliked the way it looked. / They thought it was a waste of time to examine it.

The bitterness stems from the emotional weight of inheritance ("resenting the weight of another object"), not physical flaws or laziness. A weak reader might focus on "faded" (distractor 1) or overlook the deeper context (distractors 2–3).

Correct: It was meant to provide comfort during hardship.
Distractors:
- It was a warning about an upcoming crisis. / It was a riddle to be solved. / It was a religious symbol.
Explanation:
The inscription’s meaning is tied to comfort ("her only comfort"), not prediction, puzzles, or religion. Distractor 1 misinterprets "storm" literally; distractors 2–3 lack textual support.

Correct: From frustration and bitterness to fascination and acceptance.
Distractors:

The text describes a gradual emotional evolution ("curiosity shifted"), not obsession or abandonment. Distractor 1 confuses research with fixation; distractors 2–3 contradict the narrator’s final attachment.

- The locket made no sound when moved. / It reminded them of their aunt’s quiet personality. / They associated silence with loneliness.

Silence is metaphorical, representing unspoken history ("a bridge to a life I’d never know"). Distractor 1 takes it literally; distractors 2–3 project unrelated associations.

Correct: A sense of connection to their aunt’s past and emotional closure.

Explanation:

Topic: a personal account of a journey I took to trace my family's history

Text:
When I set out to trace my family’s history, I never expected the journey to take me to the heart of an urban sprawl where my great-grandfather had once led a gang of laborers. The city was a maze of brick buildings and cobblestone streets, but I was woefully unprepared—I hadn’t equipped myself with more than a map and my grandmother’s faded letters. Within days, I hit a dead end, my questions met with shrugs or silence. It was only when I struck up a conversation with an elderly woman at a café that I became conscious of how little I truly understood.

The woman’s stories led me to a small town on the outskirts, where the air still carried whispers of the past. I learned that decades earlier, the area had been exposed to radiation from a nearby factory, and my ancestor had fought to document the workers’ suffering. A scholarship he had won funded his research, and though the town had moved on, the scars remained. Visiting the abandoned factory site was physically hurtful—the rusted machinery and faded protest signs made the injustice feel painfully real.

One aspect of my heritage I hadn’t considered was how deeply my family’s struggles were tied to resilience. The more I dug, the more I realized that my grandmother’s warnings about "never forgetting" weren’t just sentimental—they were a call to action. The most moving moment came when I found a newspaper clipping in the town archives: my great-grandfather’s name listed among the activists who had pushed for compensation. It wasn’t just history; it was a living legacy.

By the time I left, I understood that tracing my roots wasn’t about collecting facts. It was about the wisdom of the stories that had been passed down, stories that made me conscious of how much I didn’t know—and how much I still had to learn.

The gang refers to laborers, not criminals or wealth, and is part of historical context, not the author’s personal traits. A weak reader might assume "gang" implies danger or wealth, overlooking the B2-level nuance of "laborers."

"Hurt" here is metaphorical (emotional impact of the site), not literal. A weak reader might focus on physical details or ignore the context of radiation and injustice.

Correct: It was awarded for documenting workers’ suffering after radiation exposure.
Distractors:

Explanation:
The scholarship is tied to the ancestor’s activism, not the author’s present. A weak reader might confuse timelines or overlook the link to radiation.

Correct: She realized her family’s history was tied to resilience and social justice.
Distractors:
1. She learned her family had hidden secrets. / She discovered her family was involved in crime. / She found out her family was famous.
Explanation:
The aspect is about activism and resilience, not secrecy or infamy. A weak reader might misinterpret "gang" or "aspect" negatively.

Correct: It emphasizes that memory and storytelling are central to preserving heritage.
Distractors:

Explanation:
The advice is about legacy, not safety or wealth. A weak reader might misread "never forget" as a cautionary tale.



Topic: a story about a time I had to live with a difficult person and what it taught me

Text:
When I moved in with Jake, I expected a typical roommate dynamic—shared groceries, the occasional movie night, and adequate respect for personal space. Instead, I found myself in pure chaos. Jake, a night-shift nurse, operated on a schedule that opposed my own. His humorous attempts to explain his habits ("I’m just a nocturnal owl!") did little to ease the frustration of his 6 AM laundry sessions or the puzzle of why he’d trade my favorite mug for a chipped one.

At first, I speculated whether he was deliberately difficult. We tried trading chores, but his definition of "clean" involved leaving socks on the floor. My expectations of a smooth roommate experience were shattered when I realized my adequate patience wouldn’t suffice. I initially opposed his late-night music, but after one too many arguments, I adjusted. The turning point came when he left me a note: "Sorry about the mess. I’ll do better." His sincerity, though late, made me reconsider.

Living with Jake was a full-time lesson in tolerance. I learned that flexibility isn’t weakness—it’s survival. What started as a daily battle became a story I’d laugh about years later. The experience taught me that people, like habits, aren’t always adequate to our plans, but they can become part of the pure joy of adjusting.

3. Jake claimed to be a morning person.

Distractor 1 is wrong because the text describes humor, not anger. Distractor 2 is wrong because the narrator is frustrated, not amused by the habits. Distractor 3 contradicts the text, which clearly states Jake prefers nights.

Distractors:

3. The narrator moved out in the end.
Explanation:
The narrator’s tone evolves from frustration ("chaos") to reflective humor ("a story I’d laugh about"). Distractor 1 ignores the ending’s positive tone. Distractor 2 misrepresents Jake’s note as permanent change. Distractor 3 contradicts the text, which implies the narrator stayed and learned.

3. The narrator gave up on Jake immediately.

"Trading chores" shows the narrator attempted compromise. Distractor 1 is wrong because they did compromise. Distractor 2 ignores the narrator’s agency in adjusting. Distractor 3 contradicts the text, which shows persistence.

Explanation:

Topic: a fantasy story about a librarian who discovers the books can rewrite themselves

Text:
Eleanor had spent thirty years in the old library, her fingers steady as she traced the spines of forgotten tomes. But nothing prepared her for the day she found The Alchemist’s Guide rewriting itself. The ink shifted before her eyes, sentences rearranging into new interpretations. Intrigued, she acquired the book and noticed others changing following her movements.

Her assistant, Leo, accompanied her as they searched for answers. They discovered a hidden cell beneath the stacks, where books pulsed with energy. Eleanor was optimistic despite the chaos. "If the books could change," she mused, "what else might be possible?"

After days of study, she elected to keep one rewritten volume—a poetry collection now filled with prophecies. The phenomenon defied explanation, but Eleanor smiled. The library wasn’t just a repository of knowledge; it was alive. And she had just begun to understand its secrets.

Topic: an adventure story about a mapmaker charting a continent that reshapes itself constantly

Text:
Elias Voss had spent years charting the continent of Lysara, a land where mountains rose overnight and rivers vanished without trace. Though the work was grueling, he found it hilarious how the locals treated the shifts as mere quirks—until an incident forced him to act. One evening, the earth trembled, and by dawn, half his mapped territory had been swallowed by a new gorge. He consulted the village elder, who chuckled and said, "You’re not the first to hit a snag here." Nevertheless, Elias firmly believed the changes followed a pattern.

A scandal erupted when rival explorers accused him of fabricating data. "Your maps are inconsistent!" they sneered. But Elias registered every anomaly in his logbook, noting how the land’s volatility could explain ancient legends. He observed the terrain closely, sketching corrections under the glow of his lantern.

Then came the theft: a gang of bandits stole his tools mid-camp. Though devastated, he nevertheless improvised, using charcoal and bark to redraw the map. The final version revealed Lysara’s true nature—not a stable land, but a living organism, reshaping itself under unseen forces. His reputation salvaged, Elias smiled. The continent might be unpredictable, but so was he.

Topic: a fantasy story where the main character realises that their world is an illusion

Text:
Lira had lived her entire life in a world where routine dominated her thoughts. Shortly after waking, she would follow the same path to the same job, where the same tasks unfolded with mechanical precision. Although the routine seemed normal, Lira often felt as though she were sleepwalking—her agency reduced to a series of preordained choices.

One morning, everything changed. While walking to the tram, she noticed a lottery ticket on the ground. It was dated randomly for the next day, and the numbers seemed to shift when she blinked. At first, she assumed it was a trick of the light, but when she scratched it, the prize was already hers—an unacceptable violation of the world’s unspoken rules. That night, she questioned everything. If the ticket’s existence defied logic, what else was an illusion? The streets she walked, the faces she passed—were they real, or merely constructs designed to keep her compliant?

The next day, the illusion began to fold into chaos. Shadows stretched unnaturally, and voices whispered in languages she didn’t know. Lira realized with a jolt that her entire life had been a carefully curated facade. There was no agency here, only the illusion of choice. As panic set in, she understood she had to act forward, even if it meant stepping into the unknown.

Questions:
Q1: Why does Lira feel like she’s "sleepwalking" in her routine?
Correct: She feels powerless and disconnected from her own life because her actions are predetermined.
Distractors:
1. She is literally asleep during parts of her day.
2. She is bored by the monotony of her life.
3. She is avoiding responsibility for her actions.
Explanation:

A certificate from the Grand Academy is awarded annually to scholars who study Tyrrion’s history, emphasizing its significance beyond mere survival.

Life in Veythar is a delicate balance. The elders place great emphasis on respecting Tyrrion, believing the creature possesses a deep soul that connects the city to the earth’s ancient rhythms. This reverence is not just spiritual but practical—trade routes following Tyrrion’s path ensure prosperity, and rare artifacts purchased from distant lands are prized above all else. Yet, the city faces severe challenges. Storms erupt when Tyrrion’s pace quickens, and the people must endure the challenge of rebuilding homes after each shift.

To outsiders, Veythar is a metaphor for resilience—a living testament to humanity’s ability to thrive in harmony with nature. But for its inhabitants, it is simply home, a place where every stone, every hearth, and every life is tied to the creature beneath them.

Topic: an essay on the societal impact of the four-day work week

Text:
The idea of a four-day workweek has become one of the most relevant subjects in modern workplace reform. Initially met with skepticism, early attempts in countries like Iceland revealed surprising benefits. Employees reported improved nutrition and mental health, as their extra day allowed time to cook balanced meals or exercise—a far cry from the exhausted workers of traditional five-day schedules. Economically, the shift wasn’t just about trading hours for leisure; it was about redirecting energy, like a canal guiding water to more productive fields. Productivity often matched or exceeded five-day outputs, while companies found workers more engaged and creative.

Not all sectors have embraced the change so enthusiastically. Retail or healthcare workers might beg for flexibility, but their roles demand neutral assessments of feasibility. Some studies remain neutral, citing mixed results, particularly in industries where customer service hours can’t be compressed. However, nations like Belgium and Spain have made the four-day week a signature policy, proving its scalability. The key lies in fulfilling both personal and professional needs without sacrificing efficiency. As work cultures evolve, the four-day week may well redefine the global standard—if companies dare to fulfil the promise of its potential.

Distractors:
1. Free time always leads to increased productivity.
2. Employees who work fewer hours tend to neglect their health.
3. The four-day week eliminates the need for workplace wellness programs.
Explanation:
The text explicitly links "improved nutrition and mental health" to extra free time, making the correct answer the only supported inference. Distractors 1 and 2 are overgeneralizations or contradictions, while 3 is irrelevant (no mention of wellness programs).
---

Distractors:
1. To compare the workweek to a physical infrastructure project.
2. To suggest that workers will physically move to new locations.
3. To imply that the four-day week is an outdated system.
Explanation:
The metaphor is abstract ("redirecting energy"), not literal. Distractors 1–3 misinterpret it as concrete, physical, or negative, which contradicts the text’s positive framing.
---
Q3: According to the text, which industries might struggle to adopt a four-day workweek without adjustments?
Correct: Retail and healthcare sectors, where service hours are inflexible.
Distractors:
1. Technology companies, due to their high demand for innovation.

3. Creative fields, as they require long hours for brainstorming.

The text names retail/healthcare explicitly. Distractors 1–3 introduce unsupported assumptions (tech companies are early adopters; resistance/worker behavior isn’t mentioned; creative fields aren’t discussed).

Distractors:

2. Their workers were less resistant to the change.
3. The policies were temporary, unlike Iceland’s permanent shift.
Explanation:
The text highlights "signature policy" (national adoption), not timing or worker attitudes. Distractors 1–3 confuse chronology, unsupported claims, or contradictions.

Explanation:

Topic: a reflective essay on the concept of home in an increasingly globalized and mobile society

Text:
Home in a Mobile World
The concept of home has evolved considerably in our globalized society. For early settlers, home was land—a marker of ownership and security. Today, home is less about bricks and more about bonds: the cheeky laughter of a neighbor, the routine of morning coffee in a café. Yet, economic upheavals, like the 2008 recession, force families to relocate, disrupting these ties. A child’s golden days in one place may fade as they become a settler in another, their identity reshaped by displacement.

Migration challenges the apparent stability of home. Routines dissolve; new ones form, but the old bond to a place lingers. A family photo becomes a marker of belonging, while a passport symbolizes the fluidity of modern life. Thus, home is no longer static but a shifting idea—rooted in memory, not geography. Where is home when you’re always moving? Perhaps it’s not a place at all, but the people and moments that make you feel at home.

   - Explanation: The text contrasts land (physical) vs. memory/bonds (emotional), not investment vs. diversity.

   - Explanation: The text implies settlers saw home as stable (land), but migrants’ home is fluid—not that settlers saw it as transient.

   - Explanation: The text mentions photos (tangible) and neighbors (physical), not digital tools.

Topic: an article exploring the ethics of digital legacies and social media profiles after death

Text:
The Ethics of Digital Legacies: What Happens to Your Social Media After Death?

As social media use grows, so do questions about what happens to our online presence after death. Broadly, this issue involves legal, emotional, and technical challenges. Many people possess accounts but fail to plan for their digital afterlife, leaving families to overcome unexpected hurdles. For example, even with a will, accessing a deceased person’s emails or photos can be nearly impossible due to platform policies.

One key concern is the emission of personal data into the public sphere. Private messages, location history, and even unfinished posts may become mortal remnants, raising ethical questions. Is it wise to allow platforms to control these digital legacies? Some argue that memorializing accounts honors the dead, while others believe this exceeds reasonably privacy expectations. The "acid test" of ethics here is balancing respect with consent—how far should we go to preserve an online identity?

Consider the case of a friend’s family who had to delete his account after his death. Though they removed his profile, his photos and posts remained scattered across other platforms. Such scenarios highlight how broadly policies vary: some sites offer memorial tools, while others provide no guidance. Without clear rules, families are left guessing, often struggling to overcome the emotional and technical obstacles.

Ultimately, society must address these issues reasonably. Planning ahead—such as designating a digital executor or setting account preferences—can prevent future conflicts. The goal should be to honor the dead while protecting the living from unintended emissions of their private lives.

Questions:
Q1: What does the author suggest is the primary ethical dilemma surrounding digital legacies?
Correct: The conflict between honoring a person’s digital memory and respecting their privacy, especially when platforms may not act in the deceased’s best interests.
Distractors:
1. The author believes all digital legacies should be deleted immediately after death to protect privacy.
2. The main issue is that families don’t know how to technically delete accounts.
3. Platforms are legally required to handle digital legacies poorly.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures the balance described in the text ("balancing respect with consent"). Distractor 1 ignores the memorialization option; distractor 2 confuses the example (deleting an account) with the core dilemma; distractor 3 misrepresents the text’s claim about variability in policies as a legal requirement.
---
Q2: How does the author use the phrase "acid test" in paragraph 3?
Correct: To describe the most critical ethical challenge in determining how to handle digital legacies without violating privacy.
Distractors:
1. It refers to the difficulty of chemically altering digital data after death.
2. The author is suggesting that digital legacies are like a scientific experiment.
3. It means the platforms themselves are the problem, like a corrosive substance.
Explanation:
The correct answer explains the metaphorical use of "acid test" as a defining challenge. Distractor 1 misinterprets it literally; distractor 2 ignores the idiomatic meaning; distractor 3 confuses the subject (the ethical dilemma) with the object of criticism (platforms).
---
Q3: According to the text, why might families struggle to access a deceased person’s social media accounts?
Correct: Due to technical barriers (e.g., passwords), unclear platform policies, and the deceased person’s lack of prior planning.
Distractors:
1. Because social media companies actively block access to deceased users’ accounts.
2. Families lack the emotional strength to handle the deceased’s digital life.
3. Most people’s accounts are set to auto-delete after death.
Explanation:
The correct answer lists the three factors mentioned: passwords, policies, and lack of planning. Distractor 1 assumes malicious intent; distractor 2 introduces emotional (not technical/legal) barriers; distractor 3 misrepresents the example as a rule.
---

Distractors:
1. Some platforms charge fees to memorialize accounts, making it inaccessible.
2. Other platforms automatically delete all accounts upon death.
3. Platforms that don’t offer tools are generally more ethical because they respect privacy.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the contrast ("some offer tools, others provide no guidance... exceed reasonable expectations"). Distractor 1 introduces cost (not mentioned); distractor 2 assumes binary options; distractor 3 overgeneralizes the ethical stance, ignoring the nuance of "exceeding expectations."
---
Q5: What practical solution does the author propose to prevent conflicts over digital legacies?
Correct: Individuals should proactively plan for their digital afterlife, such as designating a digital executor or setting account preferences.
Distractors:
1. Governments should create universal laws regulating digital legacies.
2. Families should sue platforms that fail to provide access.
3. Social media companies must be forced to offer memorialization tools.
Explanation:
The correct answer summarizes the individual action suggested ("Planning ahead... can prevent future conflicts"). Distractor 1 assumes systemic change; distractor 2 introduces legal action; distractor 3 misrepresents the critique of gaps as a call for forced solutions.



Topic: a reflective essay on the value of solitude in a hyper-connected world

Text:
In a world where notifications ping and algorithms dictate our output, solitude is often dismissed as laziness. Yet, as social media operators expand their coverage, we’ve become creatures of fragmented attention, chasing convenience over meaning. The pursuit of constant connectivity may feel productive, but analysts argue it stifles creativity. Studies show that even brief periods of withdrawal—stepping away from screens—can sharpen focus and reduce stress.

Solitude isn’t isolation; it’s the space to reassess priorities. When we silence the noise, we rediscover our own thoughts. A firm belief persists that busyness equals success, but this ignores the fragment of time we lose to mindless scrolling. The output of our minds—innovative ideas, emotional resilience—thrives in quiet. Tech operators profit from our distraction, but the cost is a life lived half-present.

Perhaps the most radical act today is to withdraw intentionally. To sit without a device, to let the mind wander, to acknowledge that solitude isn’t selfish but necessary. In a hyper-connected world, it’s the one thing no algorithm can replace.

Topic: an article about the paradox of choice - why more options often make us less happy

Text:
The Paradox of Choice: Why More Options Often Make Us Miserable

In a world where we’re bombarded with volume of choices—from streaming services to career paths—why do we often feel more stressed than satisfied? Psychologists call this the paradox of choice: the more options we have, the harder it is to decide, and the less happy we become.

Consider a shopping trip. A decade ago, you might have browsed three stores; today, you scroll through endless circuits of online retailers. Each logo represents a brand promising the perfect product, but the sheer number leaves us paralyzed. Studies show that when faced with too many draft options—whether in dating apps or job applications—our minds chop at the sheer cognitive load. We resist the temptation to commit, fearing we’ll make the wrong choice.

This isn’t just a modern affair. Urban areas thrive on variety, but rural residents often report higher life satisfaction. Why? Fewer choices mean less anxiety. Even corporations now embrace simplicity: Apple’s minimalist logo isn’t just a design choice; it’s a promise of ease. Meanwhile, immigration of endless possibilities into our lives—from social media algorithms to career pivots—creates a cycle of dissatisfaction.

The solution? Moderation. Instead of seeking infinite options, we should ask: What truly matters? Sometimes, fewer choices lead to greater contentment.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author mention Apple’s logo in the text?
Correct: To illustrate how simplicity in branding can reduce decision fatigue.
Distractors:

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of remote work

Text:
The origin of remote work traces back to tech companies in the 1990s, but its popularity has surged post-pandemic. While it offers unparalleled flexibility—motivating employees to work during peak productivity hours—it also demands self-discipline. Professionals can specialize in global markets without relocating, and tasks are often completed with greater efficiency, as commutes are eliminated and delivery times shrink. However, the lack of in-person coverage from IT support can ruin a day’s work if technical issues arise.

A critical drawback is the absence of non-verbal cues, such as tone or body language, which can lead to misunderstandings. For instance, a sarcastic remark in an email might be misinterpreted, ruining professional relationships. Additionally, the lyric quality of office collaboration—spontaneous brainstorming or watercooler chats—is lost, leaving employees feeling isolated. This can narrow one’s career lane, as opportunities for mentorship or networking diminish.

Remote work’s success hinges on accountability. Without clear deadlines or supervision, some employees may procrastinate. Conversely, those who thrive in structured environments might hit a snag without the routine of a physical office. Ultimately, remote work’s advantages—flexibility, specialization, and efficient delivery—must be weighed against its challenges: technical coverage gaps, lost communication cues, and the risk of professional stagnation.

Questions:
Q1: What is the author’s primary argument about remote work?
Correct: Remote work has both significant benefits (flexibility, specialization) and drawbacks (isolation, accountability challenges), but its success depends on individual adaptability.
Distractors:
1. Remote work is always more productive than office work.
2. The author believes remote work should be banned.
3. Technical issues are the only problem with remote work.
Explanation:
The correct answer captures the balanced view (pros/cons + adaptability). Distractor 1 ignores drawbacks; 2 misreads the neutral tone; 3 oversimplifies by focusing solely on tech.
---

Correct: Without non-verbal signals like tone or body language, written messages can be easily misunderstood, damaging professional relationships.
Distractors:
1. Lost cues make remote work impossible.
2. Employees in offices never face communication problems.
3. Lost cues only affect creative jobs.
Explanation:
The correct answer paraphrases the sarcastic email example. Distractor 1 overstates severity; 2 ignores comparative context; 3 incorrectly limits the scope to creativity.
---
Q3: Why might remote work ruin career opportunities for some employees?
Correct: Employees may miss mentorship and networking opportunities that typically arise in office settings.
Distractors:
1. Remote work reduces job opportunities entirely.
2. Only introverts struggle with remote work.
3. Career stagnation is inevitable for remote workers.
Explanation:
The correct answer rephrases "career lane... narrows." Distractor 1 exaggerates; 2 introduces irrelevant traits; 3 misinterprets "can" as absolute.
---
Q4: What example does the author use to illustrate the risk of miscommunication?
Correct: A sarcastic email might be taken literally, leading to conflict.
Distractors:
1. Miscommunication always leads to job loss.
2. Only written messages cause problems.
3. Sarcasm is the only dangerous tone in remote work.
Explanation:
The correct answer restates the sarcastic remark example. Distractor 1 overgeneralizes; 2 ignores calls; 3 narrows the scope.

Correct: Self-discipline and clear accountability systems.
Distractors:

2. Only managers need to set accountability systems.
3. Technology eliminates the need for accountability.
Explanation:
The correct answer combines "self-discipline" and "accountability." Distractor 1 contradicts the text; 2 ignores employee responsibility; 3 misrepresents tech’s role.



Topic: a case study on the use of gamification in advertising

Text:
Gamification—integrating game mechanics into advertising—has revolutionized how brands grab consumer attention. Nike’s "Run Club" app exemplifies success: by offering badges for milestones, it turned fitness into a victory lap. However, not all campaigns thrive. When rewards feel arbitrary, users may experience bombing from irrelevant ads, leading to disengagement. Worse, harmful practices like fake scarcity ("Only 3 spots left!") can erode trust.

The key lies in incentives tied to real value. Without timely feedback, users may feel starved of progress, abandoning the app. Funding also plays a critical role: low-budget campaigns risk shallow mechanics, while excessive spending may enhance short-term metrics at the cost of long-term saving—user loyalty.

Nike’s victory stemmed from balancing rewards with authenticity. Brands must prioritize feedback loops and ethical incentives to avoid starving users of genuine motivation. The lesson? Gamification works when it feels like a partnership, not a bombing raid.

Questions:
Q1: What is the primary reason Nike’s "Run Club" app succeeded in gamification?
Correct: It aligned rewards with authentic user goals.
Distractors:
1. It used fake scarcity to create urgency.
2. It relied on excessive funding to attract users.
3. It avoided all forms of feedback to simplify design.
Explanation:
The text emphasizes "balancing rewards with authenticity" as the success factor. Distractor 1 contradicts the critique of fake scarcity; 2 misrepresents funding’s role; 3 ignores the feedback requirement.
---
Q2: How does the text describe the risk of "bombing" in gamification?
Correct: It refers to overwhelming users with irrelevant ads or rewards.
Distractors:
1. It means using too much funding to create flashy campaigns.
2. It describes a strategy where users are forced to compete aggressively.
3. It implies that gamification should be abandoned entirely.
Explanation:
"Bombing" is defined as "irrelevant ads" or "fake scarcity." Distractor 1 conflates funding with content; 2 invents competition; 3 misrepresents the text’s constructive tone.
---
Q3: Why might users feel "starved" in a gamified ad campaign?
Correct: Because they receive delayed or insufficient feedback on their progress.
Distractors:
1. They are given too many rewards at once, causing confusion.
2. The campaign requires excessive physical effort.
3. They are not provided with enough funding to participate.
Explanation:
The text states: "Without timely feedback, users may feel starved." Distractor 1 inverts the cause; 2/3 are unrelated.

Topic: an article about trends in urban planning

Text:
Urban planning is evolving at a pace unseen in decades, driven by both crises and opportunities. Cities worldwide are establishing stricter zoning laws to combat low housing affordability, with a recent bill in New York mandating mixed-income developments. Critics argue these measures stifle growth, but supporters point to genuine success stories like Vienna, where social housing has been a cornerstone for decades.

One emerging trend is the "15-minute city" package, where residents can access essential services within a short walk or bike ride. This hypothesis—first proposed by Paris mayor Anne Hidalgo—has impressed planners in Barcelona and Melbourne, who are now testing similar models. However, rapid adoption risks overlooking signature architectural heritage, as seen in Shanghai, where skyscrapers are rising at a rocket pace, erasing historic neighborhoods.

Balancing innovation with tradition requires careful adjustment. For example, Singapore’s high-density housing, though efficient, has led to low satisfaction scores due to cramped living spaces. Meanwhile, Copenhagen’s bike-friendly infrastructure proves that sustainability and livability can coexist—if planners prioritize people over profit.

The future of urban planning lies in flexibility. Cities that adjust to climate change, demographic shifts, and economic pressures will thrive, while others may face genuine backlash from displaced communities. The challenge is not just building smarter but building fairly.

The elevator advanced without his input, descending past the affordable office spaces below. The city’s lively streets blurred into a streak of neon. Daniel’s pulse quickened. The story gained a dark spice—what if the floor wasn’t a mistake?

Topic: a fantasy story about finding an "impossible object" in an ancient fossil layer

Text:
Dr. Elara Voss had spent years studying the ancient fossil layers beneath the coastal cliffs of Lyria, but nothing prepared her for what she found that morning. While examining a newly exposed line of sediment, her gloved fingers brushed against something smooth and metallic—an exception to the usual bone fragments and petrified wood. At first, she assumed it was a trick of the light, but the object’s external surface gleamed under her lamp, untouched by time. "This isn’t possible," she muttered, her pulse quickening.

The discovery seemed like a coincidence, yet the plot of her career had always hinted at something extraordinary. The fossil layer, a natural harbour for prehistoric life, had preserved not just remains but an artifact that defied explanation. Using her electronics, she scanned the object, revealing intricate engravings—patterns that looked almost like writing. "It’s not just a fossil," she whispered. "It’s something else entirely." The object’s internal structure was astonishing, with layers that seemed to shift when viewed from different angles, as if resisting the laws of physics.

Hence, the implications were staggering. If this were real, it would rewrite history. Elara clutched the object, her mind racing. Was this a relic from a lost civilization? Or had it fallen from heaven itself? The plot thickened as she realized the object pulsed faintly, reacting to her touch. She had found more than a fossil—she had uncovered a secret buried for millennia.

Topic: a review of different architectural approaches to building in earthquake-prone zones

Text:
Earthquake-prone zones demand architectural solutions that possess both strength and adaptability. Modern engineers often devote resources to designing flexible structures, such as buildings with wind-resistant frames and base isolators that absorb seismic waves. These approaches are well-documented in case studies, where cities like Tokyo and San Francisco cite their success. However, critics argue that such methods may not always be appropriate for low-income regions, where materials like reinforced concrete are costly.

Traditional building techniques, though less studied, offer an alternative. Indigenous communities in Peru and Japan have long used earthen walls and wooden frameworks, which an ongoing enquiry by a leading institute suggests are surprisingly resilient. Architects defend these methods, pointing to their sustainability and cultural significance, but sceptics question their scalability. The diversity of solutions reflects a broader debate: Should regions prioritise cutting-edge technology, or should they cite historical evidence to justify traditional designs?

Balancing innovation with practicality remains a challenge. While some governments institute strict building codes, others struggle to enforce them due to limited funding. The key may lie in fostering collaboration—where engineers and anthropologists work together to tailor designs to local needs.

Topic: an article about the architecture of modern libraries

Text:
Modern libraries are far more than repositories of books; they are dynamic spaces where architecture and function merge seamlessly. While their origin lies in ancient scriptoria, today’s libraries are reimagined as super-efficient hubs, blending technology with traditional roles. Architects approach these projects with the precision of a surgeon’s scalpel, ensuring every detail—from lighting to seating—serves a purpose. There’s almost a fever of innovation in this field, as designers experiment with open-plan layouts and modular furniture to maintain accessibility for all ages.

A key feature is their adaptability. Unlike static buildings, modern libraries host workshops, generate revenue through events, and even function as community centers. Junior architects, often supervised by senior teams, play a crucial role in balancing aesthetics with practicality. For example, the appropriately scaled reading nooks in the Toronto Reference Library encourage quiet study, while the super-bright collaborative zones foster group work. Yet, despite their super-modern facades, libraries face short-term funding challenges, forcing designers to prioritize cost-effective materials without compromising quality.

Visitors can infer the library’s purpose from its layout: open shelves invite exploration, while hidden study pods cater to focused work. The result is a space that feels both timeless and cutting-edge—a testament to how architecture reflects society’s evolving needs.

A portion of my old self remained, but the rest had dissolved into uncertainty. The team’s dynamics had evolved, and my absence had left a gap. By week three, I began to reintegrate, though the lingering sense of being an outsider persisted.

Frustration turned to disappointment when Elena’s first attempts failed. However, she persisted, convinced the painting could bear no more secrets. Finally, with meticulous precision, she revealed the hidden portrait—a positive surprise that left the art world in awe. The completion of the puzzle marked the end of an era, as the painting’s mysteries were at last laid bare.

Many wellness brands profit from the very anxiety they claim to cure.

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of minimalism in interior design

Text:
Minimalism in interior design has become a prime choice for many, establishing itself as a dominant trend in both homes and workplaces. Its numerous advantages—such as cleanliness, simplicity, and a focus on functionality—make it appealing. For instance, minimalist offices often improve productivity by reducing distractions. Additionally, minimalism creates a perception of spaciousness, which can be particularly beneficial in urban environments.

However, minimalism is not without its drawbacks. Some argue that its lack of diversity in decor can make spaces feel sterile or monotonous. Others find the style confusing, especially when executed poorly, leading to a sterile, impersonal look. Critics also warn that if overused, minimalism can bomb, resulting in interiors that lack warmth or character.

Feedback from designers and homeowners is mixed. While some praise minimalism for its calming effect, others prefer more entertaining or eclectic styles. The key, many agree, lies in balance—using minimalism as a foundation while incorporating personal touches to avoid a cold, unwelcoming atmosphere.

Topic: a commentary on the modern phenomenon of over-posting a travel experience

Text:
The modern phenomenon of over-posting travel experiences has erupted as a defining trait of urban life, where every moment must be curated for social media consumption. An economist might argue that this behavior is a rational response to the algorithmic demands of platforms, which reward frequency over depth. Yet, the soul of travel—its spontaneity, its quiet discoveries—often gets lost in the rush to beat the next post. What was once a personal journal has become a performance, with users scrambling to meet the acceptable standards set by peers and influencers.

A referee-like algorithm now polices authenticity, while vast databases of curated content dictate what constitutes a "good" travel experience. The pressure to conform is relentless, leaving little room for unfiltered moments. Even a trip to a remote county risks being overshadowed by the polished feeds of urban adventures, where every snapshot is optimized for likes rather than memory.

Critics warn that this obsession with over-posting is unsustainable, both psychologically and creatively. The exhaustion of constantly "beating" the system—of chasing the next viral moment—leaves little energy for the journey itself. Perhaps it’s time to question whether we’re truly capturing travel or just feeding an insatiable machine.

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was perpetuating a cycle I had sworn to break

Text:
The Moment I Realized I Was Perpetuating a Cycle

As a child, I remember standing before Mr. Hayes, the chairman of our debate club, after winning a regional competition. "You have sophisticated ideas," he said, "but don’t let impatience ruin your potential." I nodded solemnly, vowing to break my habit of procrastinating on important tasks. Years later, though, I found myself in a familiar cycle. Whereas I had once sworn to change, I was subsequently justifying delays with a troop of excuses—each one more elaborate than the last.

The breaking point came during a work project. My team relied on me to finalize a report, but I kept pushing deadlines, convinced I’d do it "later." My colleague, amused by my cute but futile attempts to organize my chaos, handed me a book on psychology: "The concept of ‘implementation intentions’ might help," she said. I scoffed—until I realized I’d been a terrorist of my own productivity, sabotaging myself with half-hearted plans.

That night, I stared at my notes. I had spent years studying psychology, yet I’d ignored the simplest truth: habits thrive on repetition. My impatience had morphed into a self-fulfilling prophecy. Subsequently, I replaced my excuses with a single, actionable goal: "If I want this done, I’ll do it now." No more cycles.

Questions:
Q1: What does the narrator attribute their childhood promise to change to?
Correct: The praise of Mr. Hayes, the chairman of the debate club.
Distractors:
1. A cute childhood habit they wanted to lose.
2. Their own realization that procrastination was sophisticated.
3. A psychology book they read as a child.
Explanation:
The text explicitly links the promise to Mr. Hayes’ feedback ("You have sophisticated ideas..."). Option 1 misattributes cute to the habit; Option 2 confuses sophisticated with procrastination; Option 3 misplaces the book’s role (it’s mentioned later).
---

Correct: A troop of excuses—elaborate and futile justifications for delays.
Distractors:
1. A single, impatient outburst.

3. A sophisticated psychological defense mechanism.
Explanation:

Correct: It triggers the narrator’s realization that they’ve been ignoring psychological principles they studied.
Distractors:
1. It provides a cute analogy to explain procrastination.
2. It confirms that the narrator is a terrorist of their own habits.
3. It offers a sophisticated excuse for the narrator’s delays.
Explanation:
The book introduces implementation intentions, prompting the narrator’s self-critique. Option 1 misreads cute as descriptive of the book; Option 2 reverses cause/effect; Option 3 contradicts the book’s purpose.
---
Q4: Why does the narrator call their procrastination a "terrorist"?
Correct: Because procrastination was actively sabotaging their own productivity, like an enemy.
Distractors:
1. Because it was as cute as a childhood habit.
2. Because it involved a troop of people enabling the habit.
3. Because Mr. Hayes, the chairman, warned them about it.

Distractors:

2. They become impatient with their old habits.

Topic: a story about mediating a conflict between two people I care about deeply

Text:
My two closest friends, Maria and James, had always been in high regard among our community—Maria as a compassionate nurse and James as a respected teacher. But their recent conflict over how to care for Maria’s elderly aunt had become unbearable. Maria insisted on modern, flexible nursing practices, while James, with his conservative values, argued for compulsory adherence to textbook protocols. "She’s not just a patient; she’s family!" Maria snapped, her voice trembling.

I had been expected to mediate, but the ethical dilemma weighed heavily on me. Although I admired them both, I knew mediating this would be difficult because their stubbornness bordered on childish. One evening, after another failed meeting, James accidentally bumped his elbow into Maria’s coffee, sending it spilling onto her notes. To my shock, she didn’t react—she just lay unconscious on the floor, her head having struck the edge of the table.

Panic set in. I rushed to call an ambulance, but not before noticing how their usual hostility had vanished, replaced by genuine concern. As I helped James lift her onto the couch, I realized this wasn’t just about nursing or education—it was about love. My "textbook" solution of splitting responsibilities fairly felt hollow now. If I sided with one, the other might feel betrayed. The conflict wasn’t about right or wrong; it was about two creatures of principle refusing to yield.

3. The narrator realizes they should have called an ambulance sooner.
