Topic: an essay on whether smartphones should be banned in schools

Text:
Should Smartphones Be Banned in Schools?
The debate over smartphones in schools rages on, with educators divided over whether these devices should be banned outright. While some argue that smartphones transmit valuable educational content—such as research tools or communication platforms—others claim they create more harm than good. The truth likely lies somewhere in between, depending on circumstance and implementation.

Proponents of smartphones argue that tech corporations have developed apps tailored for learning, from language translation tools to interactive textbooks. Banning them entirely might insert an illusion of control without addressing the real issue: poor digital literacy. Moreover, students today rely on smartphones for essential tasks, such as contacting parents or accessing grocery list apps for family responsibilities. A blanket ban could disproportionately affect students from lower-income backgrounds who depend on these devices for basic needs.

However, the downsides are undeniable. Studies show that students who use smartphones in class suffer from constant notifications, leading to a form of mental crash—their ability to focus transmits to social media feeds instead of lectures. In one high school, a student’s phone crashed onto the floor during a chair-led assembly, causing a minor injury that led to a costly insurance claim. Schools already struggle with agency over student behavior; without clear policies, smartphones become a distraction magnet. Some argue that if smartphones were banned, schools could redirect funds from rising insurance premiums to better resources.

Ultimately, the answer isn’t a simple ban but a nuanced approach. Younger students might benefit from restrictions, whereas older classes could use them responsibly under supervision. Schools should insert clear guidelines—perhaps led by a chair of a tech committee—to balance innovation and safety. The circumstance of each school dictates the solution, but one thing is clear: smartphones aren’t going away. The challenge is managing their use, not erasing it entirely.

Questions:
Q1: The author suggests that banning smartphones in schools might create a problem because it could
Correct: overlook the need for digital literacy among students.
Distractors:
- make students more reliant on outdated technology.
- prevent students from using smartphones for emergency calls.
- increase the cost of purchasing new devices for schools.
Explanation:
The text states that a ban "might insert an illusion of control without addressing the real issue: poor digital literacy." The correct answer captures this by focusing on digital literacy. Distractors fail because:
1. Outdated tech: Irrelevant; the text critiques literacy, not obsolescence.
2. Emergency calls: Not the primary concern (text emphasizes education).
3. Device costs: The text discusses insurance, not procurement.
---
Q2: According to the paragraph on downsides, what specific incident is used to illustrate the risks of smartphones in schools?
Correct: A student’s phone fell during an assembly, causing an injury that led to an insurance claim.
Distractors:
- A student was caught using a smartphone to cheat during an exam.
- A teacher’s phone crashed during a lesson, disrupting the class.
- Students started a fight over a smartphone in the school hallway.
Explanation:
The text describes "a student’s phone crashed onto the floor during a chair-led assembly, causing a minor injury that led to a costly insurance claim." The correct answer paraphrases this. Distractors are wrong because:
1. Cheating: Not mentioned (focus is on accidents).
2. Teacher’s phone: The incident involves a student.
3. Fight: The text doesn’t mention conflicts.
---
Q3: The author’s attitude toward smartphones in schools can best be described as
Correct: cautiously optimistic, with support for regulated use rather than outright bans.
Distractors:
- strongly opposed to smartphones due to their distracting nature.
- indifferent, as the topic is too complex to form an opinion.
- in favor of complete bans to eliminate all risks.
Explanation:
The conclusion advocates for "a nuanced approach" and "clear guidelines," showing regulated optimism. Distractors are incorrect because:
1. Strongly opposed: The text acknowledges benefits (e.g., apps).
2. Indifferent: The author provides a clear stance.
3. Complete bans: Rejected in the conclusion.
---
Q4: How does the author use the word "transmit" in the second paragraph?
Correct: To describe how smartphones share educational content, such as research tools.
Distractors:
- To explain how smartphones spread misinformation in schools.
- To refer to the physical act of passing phones between students.
- To illustrate how smartphones cause mental fatigue by overloading the brain.
Explanation:
The second paragraph uses "transmit" for "educational content—such as research tools." The correct answer reflects this. Distractors are wrong because:
1. Misinformation: Not mentioned (focus is on education).
2. Physical passing: Not discussed.
3. Mental fatigue: "Transmit" is used for data/signals, not overload (which is discussed later).
---
Q5: What does the author imply about the role of schools in managing smartphone use?
Correct: Schools should take an active role in creating and enforcing policies, rather than relying on bans.
Distractors:
- Schools lack the authority to control smartphone use effectively.
- Parents should be solely responsible for regulating their children’s smartphone habits.
- Smartphone use should be decided by tech corporations, not educators.
Explanation:
The conclusion suggests "Schools should insert clear guidelines... to balance innovation and safety," implying proactive management. Distractors are incorrect because:
1. Lack of authority: The text argues for agency, not helplessness.
2. Parental responsibility: Not emphasized; schools are tasked with policy.
3. Corporate control: The author critiques corporations, not advocating for their role.



Topic: a story about a friendship that ended not with a fight but with a slow, mutual drifting apart

Text:
Alex and Jamie’s friendship had once burned as brightly as the bonfires at their favorite regional festivals. For years, they had embarked on weekend exploration trips, their bond consistent despite life’s demands. But as their careers began to advance, they found themselves with less time for each other. One evening, after a chance encounter at a mutual friend’s party, Alex nudged Jamie’s elbow playfully, as they used to. Yet the laughter that followed felt forced, literally and metaphorically. Their conversations, once deep and frequent, now consisted of polite small talk. The flame of their friendship flickered, not extinguished in a fight, but slowly smothered by distance and distraction.

Jamie had once hosted dinner parties where Alex was a regular guest, but now invitations were rare. Their shared love for regional festivals had faded too; Alex’s work schedule conflicted with the dates, and Jamie’s new circle of friends preferred urban events. One night, Alex reflected on how their friendship had become a metaphor for gradual change—like a river shifting its course, imperceptibly but inevitably. They were no longer in each other’s lives, not because they had argued, but because life had simply pulled them in different directions.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author describe the laughter between Alex and Jamie as "forced"?
Correct: Their friendship had weakened, making their interactions feel unnatural.
Distractors:
1. They had a fight and were pretending to be friendly.
2. Jamie was in a bad mood that night.
3. Alex was trying to impress someone else.
Explanation: The text emphasizes gradual emotional distance ("slowly smothered by distance"), not conflict or mood. Distractors assume specific events not mentioned.
---
Q2: What replaced the deep conversations between Alex and Jamie?
Correct: Superficial or polite small talk.
Distractors:
1. They started arguing instead.
2. They avoided each other entirely.
3. They focused only on work-related topics.
Explanation: The text explicitly states "polite small talk." Distractors introduce unsupported behaviors.
---
Q3: How does the "river shifting its course" metaphor apply to their friendship?
Correct: It illustrates how their friendship changed slowly and naturally.
Distractors:
1. It suggests their friendship was doomed from the start.
2. It compares their friendship to a physical obstacle.
3. It means they actively tried to change their friendship.
Explanation: The metaphor conveys imperceptible change ("imperceptibly but inevitably"). Distractors misrepresent the metaphor’s meaning.
---
Q4: What caused their weekend exploration trips to end?
Correct: Jamie moved cities, and their schedules no longer aligned.
Distractors:
1. Alex lost interest in exploration.
2. They had a disagreement about the trips.
3. They found new hobbies.
Explanation: The text cites "Jamie moved cities" and "Alex’s work schedule conflicted." Distractors introduce unrelated causes.
---
Q5: How does Alex feel about the changes in their friendship?
Correct: Nostalgic and accepting.
Distractors:
1. Alex regrets not fighting to save the friendship.
2. Alex blames Jamie for the drift.
3. Alex is angry about the changes.
Explanation: The text describes reflection ("Alex reflected...") and acceptance ("inevitably"). Distractors assume negative emotions not present.



Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was jealous of a friend's success

Text:
The moment I confessed my jealousy to myself was like pulling a scab off an old wound. It wasn’t just about Mark’s new business—it was about the universal sting of watching someone else’s life beat against the shore of my own stagnation. His scheme to launch a startup had seemed so effortless, while my years of half-hearted attempts felt like sand slipping through my fingers. I told myself I was happy for him, but the infection of resentment had already spread, gnawing at my nerve endings.

At first, I rationalized it: his success was the equivalent of what I could have achieved if I’d had the courage. But the truth was uglier. My jealousy wasn’t just envy—it was the evil twin of admiration, a shadow that thrived in the gaps of my own participation. I had attended his launch party, clapped along, even laughed at his jokes, but inside, I resented the way his status made mine feel like a house of cards.

That night, I stared at my reflection and asked: When did I become this person? The answer wasn’t simple. It was the slow infection of comparison, the way his ambition had become a mirror held up to my excuses. I realized then that envy isn’t just about wanting what others have—it’s about the fear of what you’ve become when you stop trying to get it yourself.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the narrator compare jealousy to an "infection"?
Correct: The narrator uses "infection" to describe how jealousy spreads uncontrollably and corrupts their emotions.
Distractors:
1. To emphasize that jealousy is a physical illness that requires medical treatment.
2. To show that jealousy is a rare emotion that only affects a few people.
3. To illustrate how jealousy can be easily cured with time.
Explanation:
The correct answer fits because the text describes jealousy as an uncontrollable and corrupting force ("spread," "gnawing"). Distractor 1 misinterprets infection literally; 2 contradicts the universal nature of envy; 3 ignores the text’s focus on spread, not cure. A weak reader might overlook the metaphorical use or misconnect universal to rarity.
---
Q2: How does the narrator’s use of the word equivalent reveal their conflicted feelings?
Correct: The word equivalent shows the narrator acknowledges their success could have matched their friend’s, but their inaction makes them feel like a fraud.
Distractors:
1. It suggests the narrator believes their achievements are just as valuable as their friend’s, despite appearances.
2. The narrator is trying to convince themselves that their lack of success is actually the same as their friend’s.
3. The word emphasizes that the narrator’s jealousy is justified because their friend’s success is not truly superior.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the narrator’s self-doubt ("wondered if my participation was the equivalent of betraying myself"). Distractor 1 ignores the fraud implication; 2 misrepresents the narrator’s questioning as convincing; 3 misreads the text’s critique of jealousy. A distracted reader might focus on equivalent without noting the negative context.
---
Q3: What specific event triggers the narrator’s realization about jealousy?
Correct: The moment Mark’s business launched and the narrator attended his party, feeling resentment despite public support.
Distractors:
1. When the narrator saw their friend working harder than them at the office.
2. After receiving an invitation to their friend’s wedding, realizing they had nothing to celebrate.
3. When their friend posted about their success on social media, making the narrator feel inadequate.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s description of the launch party. Distractor 1 introduces an unrelated office context; 2 misidentifies the event as a wedding; 3 assumes a digital trigger. A weak reader might project modern biases or overlook the specific party detail.
---
Q4: What does the "shore" metaphor symbolize in the context of the narrator’s life?
Correct: The shore represents stability or safety, contrasting with the narrator’s feeling of being adrift in their own life.
Distractors:
1. The shore symbolizes the narrator’s financial security, which their friend’s success threatens.
2. The shore is where the narrator wants to be, representing their friend’s successful life.
3. The shore is a place the narrator can never reach, symbolizing their inability to achieve anything.
Explanation:
The correct answer fits the stability contrast ("beaten by the tide against the shore"). Distractor 1 misattributes financial security; 2 conflates the shore with the friend’s life; 3 misinterprets proximity as impossibility. A careless reader might assume the shore is the friend’s success.
---
Q5: What lesson does the narrator ultimately draw from their experience?
Correct: Envy isn’t just about desiring others’ success but also about confronting one’s own lack of effort or ambition.
Distractors:
1. Jealousy is a natural human emotion that should be embraced rather than suppressed.
2. The narrator realizes that their friend’s success is built on luck, not hard work.
3. True happiness comes from comparing oneself to others and striving to be better.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s focus on self-confrontation ("fear of what you’ve become"). Distractor 1 contradicts the evil framing; 2 introduces luck, not mentioned; 3 misrepresents the text’s warning against comparison. A weak reader might overlook the critical tone or misread striving as positive.



Topic: a story about how an unusual compromise resolved a conflict

Text:
The final round of the InnovateX robotics competition was tense. Team Alpha’s leader, Lena, was wise enough to suspect Team Beta of using unauthorized technological shortcuts. Their opponent, Mark, acted unconsciously aggressive, dismissing her concerns with a smirk. "You’re just bitter because we’re ahead," he sneered, dominating the leaderboard with their AI-assisted coding.

Lena’s frustration grew as the scores widened. She accused Mark’s team of working out of spite, not skill. The argument escalated until the judges intervened, announcing a depressing rule: only one team could advance. Hence, the loser would be eliminated. The crowd murmured—until a technological glitch froze both robots mid-competition.

An opening appeared. The judges suggested a joint debug session. Reluctantly, Lena and Mark agreed. As they worked side by side, they reinforced their trust by troubleshooting together. By the time the robots reactivated, they weren’t just fixing code—they were building a partnership. The judges, impressed, declared a shared victory, proving that even the fiercest conflicts could yield wise solutions when collaboration took precedence over competition.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author describe Mark as acting "unconsciously aggressive"?
Correct: His aggression was instinctive, not deliberate, making him unaware of how it fueled the conflict.
Distractors:
1. It implies Mark was trying to hide his true intentions from Lena.
2. It suggests Mark was physically violent toward Lena.
3. It means Mark was unaware of the competition rules.
Explanation:
The correct answer fits because "unconsciously" (B2 lexical item) signals lack of awareness, not deception or violence. Distractor 1 misinterprets "unconscious" as "subconscious intent." Distractor 2 ignores the text’s verbal aggression (smirk, dismissal). Distractor 3 conflates behavior with rules.
---
Q2: How does the technological glitch contribute to resolving the conflict?
Correct: It forces both teams to pause and collaborate, creating an opportunity for dialogue they previously rejected.
Distractors:
1. It proves Team Beta was cheating, so Lena dropped her accusations.
2. It allowed the judges to disqualify both teams, making the competition fair.
3. It gave Team Alpha time to hack Team Beta’s AI system.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s joint debugging. Distractor 1 assumes the glitch proves cheating (no evidence). Distractor 2 misrepresents the judges’ suggestion as an action. Distractor 3 introduces hacking, unsupported by the text.
---
Q3: What does Lena’s accusation of "spite" reveal about her emotional state?
Correct: It shows she felt personally attacked and believed Team Beta was sabotaging the competition unfairly.
Distractors:
1. It means Lena was jealous of Team Beta’s success.
2. It proves Team Beta was indeed cheating, as Lena suspected.
3. It shows Lena was overly emotional and irrational.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects Lena’s justified frustration. Distractor 1 confuses "spite" (deliberate harm) with jealousy. Distractor 2 equates accusation with proof. Distractor 3 contradicts her "wise" portrayal.
---
Q4: Why is the judges’ announcement described as "depressing"?
Correct: It implies a zero-sum outcome (only one winner), which heightened the stakes and emotional tension.
Distractors:
1. It means the competition was canceled, leaving both teams disappointed.
2. It suggests the judges were biased against Team Alpha.
3. It indicates the robots would malfunction, ruining the event.
Explanation:
The correct answer links "depressing" to exclusion. Distractor 1 misreads "only one team could advance" as cancellation. Distractor 2 introduces bias without evidence. Distractor 3 misplaces the glitch’s timing.
---
Q5: What unusual compromise do Lena and Mark reach, and how does it differ from their initial approach?
Correct: They agree to debug the robots together, shifting from rivalry to teamwork, unlike their earlier confrontational dynamic.
Distractors:
1. They agreed to share their AI code with each other.
2. They decided to split the prize money equally.
3. They called a timeout to cool down before continuing the competition.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches collaborative debugging. Distractor 1 assumes code-sharing (not mentioned). Distractor 2 introduces prizes (irrelevant). Distractor 3 misrepresents active collaboration as a pause.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of developing tourism

Text:
Tourism has long been driven by humanity’s desire for exploration and leisure, formerly viewed as an unmitigated boon. In its golden age, it fueled economies, creating jobs and lifting millions out of poverty. Countries openly courted visitors with promises of cultural immersion, from the bustling markets of Marrakech to the serene steam-filled spas of Iceland. Yet, this rapid growth has not been without consequences.

Economically, tourism’s rating as a sector is undeniable—it accounts for 10% of global GDP. Locals benefit from infrastructure improvements, and cross-cultural exchanges foster understanding. However, the environmental toll is staggering. Fragile ecosystems, described as tissue-thin, succumb to overcrowding, while steam-like emissions from cruise ships and resorts temporarily blur the horizon. Critics argue that what was once acceptable—seasonal foot traffic—now borders on exploitation, with wildlife habitats shrinking on the heels of every new hotel chain.

Socially, the divide widens. While tourists enjoy luxury, locals often bear the brunt: rising costs and strained resources. A village’s charm, once its greatest asset, can become a liability when trampled by mass tourism. The debate rages: Is tourism’s legacy one of progress or parasitic dependence?

Questions:
Q1: What does the author imply about the former perception of tourism?
Correct: It was overwhelmingly positive and uncritical.
Distractors:
1. It was secretly criticized by economists.
2. It focused primarily on environmental sustainability.
3. It was limited to cultural exchanges without economic goals.
Explanation: The text states "formerly viewed as an unmitigated boon" (line 1), supporting the correct answer. Distractors 1–3 contradict the text’s emphasis on economic/cultural combined benefits and lack of early criticism.
---
Q2: How does the author use the phrase "tissue-thin" to describe ecosystems?
Correct: To emphasize their extreme fragility and vulnerability to tourism.
Distractors:
1. To suggest ecosystems are easily repaired.
2. To compare ecosystems to medical bandages.
3. To argue that ecosystems are naturally resistant to change.
Explanation: "Tissue-thin" implies delicacy (fragile). Distractor 2 misreads the metaphor; 3 ignores the context of succumb to overcrowding.
---
Q3: What does "on the heels of" suggest about the relationship between tourism growth and environmental harm?
Correct: Environmental damage occurs immediately after tourism expansion.
Distractors:
1. Tourism growth and harm are unrelated.
2. Environmental harm precedes tourism growth.
3. The harm is temporary and reversible.
Explanation: The idiom means immediately after. Distractor 3 misapplies temporarily to ecosystems.
---
Q4: Why might the author mention cruise ships and resorts in the same sentence as steam?
Correct: To illustrate the visible, polluting impact of tourism infrastructure.
Distractors:
1. To compare modern tourism to industrial-era pollution.
2. To argue that cruise ships are the only major polluters.
3. To suggest tourism has no environmental impact.
Explanation: Steam symbolizes emissions. Distractor 1 overinterprets the metaphor historically.
---
Q5: What does the author suggest about the acceptable levels of tourism in modern society?
Correct: They are increasingly questioned and may no longer be justified.
Distractors:
1. Acceptable levels are clearly defined by governments.
2. Tourism’s acceptable levels have always been high.
3. Locals universally support higher tourism limits.
Explanation: The text describes a debate ("now borders on exploitation"). Distractor 3 ignores the divide between tourists and locals.



Topic: an essay about the benefits of playing computer games

Text:
The Hidden Benefits of Playing Computer Games

Many people dismiss computer games as a waste of time, but research shows they offer distinct advantages. For instance, games like Minecraft or Civilization require players to solve complex puzzles, improving memory and problem-solving skills. Studies suggest that even 30 minutes of weekly gaming can sharpen focus—like a mental wind clearing the fog of daily stress.

Beyond cognitive benefits, games provide protection from isolation. Multiplayer titles like Fortnite or Among Us foster teamwork, helping players develop social skills in a safe, virtual environment. Players often feel satisfied after completing challenges, a positive emotional boost akin to finishing a chapter in a notebook—except with instant rewards.

Critics argue that gaming is sedentary, but many games demand physical precision, such as quick reflexes in Call of Duty or spatial awareness in Tetris. These skills translate to real life: surgeons rely on hand-eye coordination honed in games, and architects use strategy skills learned in SimCity. Even creative fields benefit—game designers often sketch ideas in a notebook before building virtual greenhouses of imagination.

Of course, moderation is key. Excessive gaming can strain the eyes or lead to sedentary habits, but when balanced, it’s a tool for growth. Far from being a distraction, gaming is like dancing on your heels—unexpectedly graceful when done right.

Questions:
Q1: What does the author suggest is the primary cognitive benefit of playing strategy games like Civilization?
Correct: They enhance memory and problem-solving skills.
Distractors:
1. They primarily improve physical fitness.
2. They help players learn new languages.
3. They reduce the need for traditional education.
Explanation:
The text explicitly states that Civilization improves "memory and problem-solving skills." Option 1 is wrong because the focus is cognitive, not physical. Option 2 ignores the lack of language-related content. Option 3 contradicts the idea that gaming complements learning.
---
Q2: How does the text describe the emotional impact of completing in-game challenges?
Correct: Players often feel a sense of accomplishment or satisfaction.
Distractors:
1. They experience increased aggression.
2. They feel isolated from real-life relationships.
3. They develop anxiety about future challenges.
Explanation:
The text uses "satisfied" and "positive emotional boost" to describe the impact. Option 1 contradicts the positive tone. Option 2 misrepresents the social benefits. Option 3 ignores the emphasis on accomplishment.
---
Q3: What real-world skill improved by gaming does the author mention as crucial for surgeons?
Correct: Hand-eye coordination.
Distractors:
1. Quick typing speed.
2. Advanced mathematical calculations.
3. Long-term memory retention.
Explanation:
The text links "hand-eye coordination" to surgeons. Option 1 is unrelated; typing isn’t mentioned. Option 2 overestimates Civilization’s role. Option 3, while true for memory, isn’t the surgeon-specific skill highlighted.
---
Q4: Why does the author compare gaming to "dancing on your heels"?
Correct: It implies that gaming can be unexpectedly beneficial when done well.
Distractors:
1. To suggest that gaming is physically demanding.
2. To criticize gaming as unstable or risky.
3. To compare gaming to a competitive sport.
Explanation:
The metaphor conveys unexpected grace, not physicality (Option 1) or criticism (Option 2). Option 3 is incorrect because no sports analogy is made.
---
Q5: According to the text, what is one potential drawback of excessive gaming mentioned in the conclusion?
Correct: It can strain the eyes or lead to a sedentary lifestyle.
Distractors:
1. It causes addiction in all players.
2. It reduces creativity.
3. It makes players antisocial.
Explanation:
The text warns of "straining the eyes" and "sedentary habits." Option 1 is too absolute; addiction isn’t claimed. Option 2 contradicts the "virtual greenhouse" metaphor. Option 3 ignores the social benefits discussed.



Topic: a case study of a company making a positive impact on the environment and society

Text:
Patagonia’s Firework: How One Company Lit Up Sustainability
The company Patagonia, founded by Maria Chen after witnessing a tragedy—the rapid deforestation of her hometown—has become a global leader in eco-friendly innovation. When local tribes opposed outdated logging methods, Maria saw an opportunity: via sustainable agriculture and renewable energy, she could revive the land. Her idea took off like a firework, sparking a movement that now operates wherever communities need it most.

Patagonia’s approach is stunning in its simplicity. By replacing harmful pesticides with organic farming, they’ve restored soil health and reduced the carbon footprint. Over time, their model has expanded, proving that profit and planet can coexist. Even in remote villages, Patagonia’s input—training locals in green jobs—has empowered entire tribes to protect their forests.

Critics once argued that such change was impossible, but Patagonia’s success has silenced doubters. Today, their initiatives thrive wherever they’re needed, turning environmental crises into opportunities. Maria’s vision proves that even the smallest spark—like a firework in the night—can illuminate the path forward.

Questions:
Q1: What motivated Maria Chen to found Patagonia?
Correct: She was inspired by witnessing deforestation destroy her hometown.
Distractors:
1. She wanted to create a fashion brand.
2. Local tribes asked her to start the company.
3. She inherited a logging business and wanted to reform it.
Explanation: The text states Maria acted "after witnessing a tragedy—the rapid deforestation of her hometown," so her motivation was environmental, not commercial or tribal.
---
Q2: How does the text describe the initial reaction of local tribes to Patagonia’s methods?
Correct: They opposed outdated logging methods.
Distractors:
1. They initially supported the company but later changed their minds.
2. They ignored Patagonia’s efforts completely.
3. They demanded financial compensation for their forests.
Explanation: The tribes "opposed outdated logging methods," so they were resistant, not indifferent or financially motivated.
---
Q3: What metaphor does the author use to explain Patagonia’s rapid growth?
Correct: Like a firework.
Distractors:
1. A slow-burning flame.
2. A seedling growing in soil.
3. A tidal wave.
Explanation: The text explicitly compares growth to a "firework," so other options misrepresent the imagery.
---
Q4: In what ways has Patagonia’s model expanded over time?
Correct: It has spread to remote villages and trained locals in green jobs.
Distractors:
1. By opening chain stores worldwide.
2. Through government partnerships only.
3. By replacing all logging with tourism.
Explanation: The text states expansion "wherever communities need it" and includes "training locals in green jobs," so other options distort the scope.
---
Q5: Why does the author mention critics in the final paragraph?
Correct: To show that Patagonia’s success disproved skepticism.
Distractors:
1. To argue that critics were right about the company’s failures.
2. To explain that critics later joined the company.
3. To warn readers that opposition is inevitable in such projects.
Explanation: Critics "once argued that such change was impossible," but Patagonia’s success "has silenced doubters," so the mention supports its triumph.



Topic: an essay on whether zoos should be banned

Text:
The subject of whether zoos should be banned remains contentious. While some argue that zoos exploit animals, others claim they play a vital role in conservation. Zoos provide secure environments where endangered species can breed, and skilled staff ensure proper nutrition and veterinary care. Additionally, they motivate public interest in wildlife, educating millions annually.

However, critics highlight psychological harm to animals, arguing that captivity is unnatural. Some even portray zoos as monsters, ignoring their conservation successes. Ethical concerns also arise, as animals may suffer despite good intentions.

An alternative is overseas sanctuaries, where animals live in larger, more natural spaces. Interestingly, some cultures, like those in certain mosques, house animals humanely without exploitation. This raises questions: If zoos were banned, how would wildlife be protected? We must evaluate both sides fairly—zoos are not perfect, but neither are the alternatives.

Questions:
Q1: What is the author’s main argument regarding zoos?
Correct: Zoos should not be banned outright but assessed for their benefits and drawbacks.
Distractors:
1. "Zoos should be banned immediately because they harm animals." / Wrong – text calls for balanced evaluation.
2. "Zoos are unnecessary because wildlife can survive without them." / Wrong – text mentions conservation benefits.
3. "Zoos are only useful for entertainment, not conservation." / Wrong – text emphasizes education and breeding.

Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the nuanced stance in the text. Distractor 1 ignores the call for evaluation. Distractor 2 misrepresents conservation efforts. Distractor 3 contradicts the educational role described.
---
Q2: How do zoos contribute to wildlife conservation according to the text?
Correct: They help endangered species breed and educate the public about wildlife.
Distractors:
1. "They release animals back into the wild successfully." / Wrong – text doesn’t mention reintroduction.
2. "They provide natural habitats for animals." / Wrong – text says "secure enclosures," not natural.
3. "They prevent animal extinction single-handedly." / Wrong – text uses "play a vital role," not absolute.

Explanation:
The correct answer covers breeding and education. Distractor 1 assumes breeding = reintroduction. Distractor 2 misinterprets "secure" as "natural." Distractor 3 overstates the claim.
---
Q3: Why might critics call zoos "monsters"?
Correct: To exaggerate ethical concerns and criticize exploitation.
Distractors:
1. "Because zoos actually harm animals physically." / Wrong – text mentions psychological harm.
2. "Because zoos keep dangerous animals like real monsters." / Wrong – no mention of dangerous species.
3. "Because zoos are run by unskilled staff." / Wrong – text praises "skilled professionals."

Explanation:
The correct answer explains the rhetorical use of "monsters." Distractor 1 confuses types of harm. Distractor 2 takes the term literally. Distractor 3 contradicts the positive description of staff.
---
Q4: What alternative to zoos does the text suggest?
Correct: Overseas sanctuaries with larger, natural spaces.
Distractors:
1. "Wildlife reserves in urban areas." / Wrong – text specifies "overseas."
2. "Private animal collections by wealthy individuals." / Wrong – no mention of private collectors.
3. "Digital zoos (virtual reality experiences)." / Wrong – text doesn’t mention technology.

Explanation:
The correct answer matches the explicit suggestion. Distractor 1 mislocates the alternative. Distractor 2 introduces an unrelated concept. Distractor 3 is completely off-topic.
---
Q5: What does the mention of mosques imply about animal care?
Correct: Mosques demonstrate humane treatment without exploitation, unlike some zoos.
Distractors:
1. "Mosques are better than zoos because they never keep animals." / Wrong – text says "house animals humanely."
2. "Mosques use animals for religious rituals, unlike zoos." / Wrong – no mention of rituals.
3. "Mosques only keep small, harmless animals." / Wrong – text doesn’t specify size.

Explanation:

Topic: an opinion essay on whether university education should be free for everyone

Text:
Should University Education Be Free for Everyone?

The latest reports reveal that student debt in many countries has reached crisis levels, with graduates fundamentally trapped by a load of loans they can barely repay. In such circumstances, the idea of free university education is not just idealistic—it is a necessary revolution to rescue students from financial ruin. Currently, the system acts as a barrier, making higher education a distant dream for many. For example, low-income students often detect early on that university is unattainable without substantial support, while wealthier peers glide through the process with ease. This is not merely a financial issue; it is a moral one.

Critics argue that free education would place an unbearable load on taxpayers. However, the destruction caused by the current system—where talented individuals are denied opportunities due to cost—far outweighs the temporary burden of funding. Moreover, studies show that educated populations drive economic growth, benefiting society as a whole. Even bureaucratic hurdles, such as the need for a clerk’s approval for scholarships, delay access for those who need it most.

Ultimately, free education would democratize opportunity, ensuring that merit—not bank accounts—determines success. It is time to act before another generation is lost to debt and inequality.

The word "destruction" is used metaphorically to describe the systemic exclusion of students based on financial status, not literal harm or intentional sabotage. However, those opposed to this view argue that individuals must take responsibility for their own carbon footprints. Ultimately, the debate implies that both parties share responsibility. While corporations must lead systemic change, individuals can amplify pressure through consumer choices and advocacy. The challenge lies in balancing collective action with personal accountability.

Topic: an essay on the psychology of risk-taking: why some people seek danger and others avoid it

Text:
Psychologists study risk-taking from multiple angles, revealing why some people crave danger while others avoid it. Biological factors play a key role: the brain releases dopamine during thrilling activities, creating a cycle where completion of a risky task becomes addictive. For example, skydivers describe the recognition of bravery as a major motivator, even if their wrist trembles before the jump.

Social influences also shape behavior. People often follow role models who take risks, whether it’s a loyal friend encouraging reckless driving or a celebrity promoting extreme sports. Conversely, opponents of risk-taking—like parents or safety advocates—may discourage such behavior by highlighting potential harm. Studies show that individuals with a disability to process fear (e.g., due to trauma) might seek danger to regain control.

Cultural norms further edit perceptions of risk. In some societies, loyalty to tradition discourages deviation, while in others, rebellion is celebrated. Ultimately, risk-taking is a balance between instinct and environment—where excitement meets caution.

3. It causes physical symptoms like wrist injuries.
Explanation:
The correct answer reflects the text’s focus on dopamine’s role in making risky completion rewarding. Distractor 1 misinterprets the text’s emphasis on reward over fear suppression. Distractor 2 overgeneralizes the skydiver example, and Distractor 3 confuses metaphor ("wrist trembles") with literal harm.

Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s discussion of role models and opponents. Distractor 1 ignores lifelong social learning; Distractor 2 contradicts cultural variations; Distractor 3 undermines the text’s balanced view of social and biological influences.

Topic: a speculative essay on what one piece of knowledge should be passed to future generations

Text:
What One Piece of Knowledge Should We Pass to Future Generations?

If we could preserve only one piece of knowledge for future generations, what should it be? Many would argue for scientific breakthroughs or technological skills, but these are concrete tools that can be relearned. What matters more is the ability to interpret history without repeating its mistakes.

Consider the tale of settler colonialism, a frequent theme in global history. Future societies must understand not just the facts but the moral rank of justice and reparations. A highway of knowledge leads to progress, but if we pave it with only material wealth, we risk ignoring the ethical zone where fairness and equity reside.

Some might say we should teach resilience—how to survive crises. Yet, societies immune to exploitation are those that prioritize ethics over efficiency. Concrete skills fade; moral frameworks endure.

The greatest gift is not a manual for building machines but a tale of how to build a just world. Pass this down, and future generations will navigate their highways wisely.

Topic: an essay discussing the ethics of using animals in medical research

Text:
The ethics of using animals in medical research is a debate that adds a flavour of moral complexity to scientific progress. While opponents argue that animals deserve independence from human experimentation, proponents insist that breakthroughs—like vaccines and organ transplants—fare well against the costs. One aspect often overlooked is the brief history of medical advancements; without animal testing, many lives would have been lost.

However, critics highlight the suffering involved, comparing lab conditions to jail cells. Protests often turn into a parade of moral outrage, demanding stricter regulations. Some scientists lean toward cruelty-free methods, such as AI simulations or cell cultures, but these alternatives remain limited. Ethical referees—like institutional review boards—must weigh suffering against progress, ensuring improved housing and humane treatment where possible.

Ultimately, the question is not whether animal research should cease but how far we should go to balance fare in human health and ethical responsibility. Striking this equilibrium requires transparency, innovation, and a commitment to refining our methods.

Topic: an article about the placebo effect

Text:
The placebo effect—a phenomenon where patients experience healing simply from believing they are receiving treatment—has long fascinated scientists. Historically, physicians established its existence through anecdotes, but modern research has demonstrated its real power. Studies show that placebos can trigger neurochemical responses, healing symptoms like pain or depression in some cases. Yet, the mechanism remains debated: While some argue that placebos merely reflect the power of suggestion, others believe they tap into a deeper mind-body unity.

Critics warn that overemphasizing placebos could litter discussions with myths, undermining evidence-based medicine. Ethical concerns also arise—should doctors wage a battle against misinformation while still acknowledging the effect’s potential? Skeptics dismiss placebos as mere soul-soothing tricks, but proponents see them as a password to unlocking new therapeutic pathways. Like climbing a vertical ladder of understanding, researchers inch closer to harnessing this tool responsibly.

3. To suggest that historical cases are the only evidence needed.
Explanation:

The text specifies "believing they are receiving treatment" as the catalyst. Distractors introduce unsupported mechanisms (physical drug production, genetic changes, or dependency on real drugs).

Distractors:

Topic: a case study on a country that recovered successfully from a major financial crisis

Text:
Iceland’s recovery from the 2008 financial crisis is often cited as a remarkable success story. When its major banks collapsed, the country faced bankruptcy, and its currency, the krona, plummeted in value. The initial scare was immense, but the government, inheriting policies from earlier reforms, acted swiftly to stabilize the economy. Austerity measures were introduced, and the territory’s small size forced creative solutions.

One of the first sectors to revive was construction, as demand for housing and infrastructure surged. Tourism also boomed, with visitors drawn to Iceland’s stunning landscapes. Surprisingly, the crisis led to a reduction in emissions, as cheaper energy and a shift toward renewable sources gained public preference. However, the recovery was not without social costs. Income distribution became uneven, with some regions struggling more than others.

Economists and scholars argue that Iceland’s ability to rebuild its capacity for governance was critical. The central bank, for instance, regained independence, and transparency improved. Yet, challenges remained. While GDP grew rapidly, critics pointed to overreliance on a few industries. As one scholar noted, “The recovery was impressive, but sustainability required more than economic growth—it needed institutional resilience.”

Today, Iceland stands as a case study in crisis management, proving that even in a small territory, determined policy and adaptability can turn disaster into opportunity.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the text mention Iceland’s small territory as a constraint?
Correct: It forced the government to implement efficient, innovative solutions quickly.
Distractors:
1. It limited the types of industries that could grow.
2. It made it harder to attract foreign investment.
3. It reduced the country’s ability to borrow internationally.
Explanation:
The text specifies “the territory’s small size forced creative solutions,” linking constraint to efficiency, not industry limits or investment barriers. Weak readers might assume “constraint” = “limitation” without connecting it to policy adaptability.
---

Distractors:
1. They allowed the government to avoid blame for unpopular decisions.

3. They were designed specifically to handle financial crises like the one in 2008.

The text states “policies were inherited from earlier reforms” and “acted swiftly to stabilize,” emphasizing pre-existing frameworks, not blame avoidance or environmental focus. Distractor 3 is wrong because policies weren’t crisis-specific.

The text highlights “a reduction in emissions” as “surprising,” while other outcomes (jobs, tourism) are expected or not framed as positive. Weak readers might overlook “unexpected” and focus on economic recovery.

Topic: an article about the brain drain phenomenon and its effect on developing countries

Text:
The Brain Drain: When Talent Becomes an Exodus

The phenomenon of brain drain—where skilled professionals emigrate from developing nations—has reshaped economies and societies for decades. Unlike colonial-era settlers who often returned, today’s professionals frequently become permanent settlers, leaving behind gaps that are difficult to fill. The evolution of this issue reflects broader global disparities: while wages in developed nations offer ten times more, local hospitals struggle with understaffing, a detail that exposes the fragility of the system.

Historically, brain drain accelerated after independence, as educated elites sought opportunities abroad. Now, the rhythm of departures has intensified, fueled by digital connectivity and the promise of a better life—often described as a password to unlock prosperity. Economic analysts warn that without intervention, this trend will deepen inequalities. For instance, a recent demonstration by Nigerian doctors in London highlighted the lighting of underfunded hospitals back home, where patients wait for care that skilled migrants could provide.

The consequences are stark. Countries lose not just individuals but institutional knowledge, as experienced professionals expose systemic flaws before leaving. While some governments offer incentives to retain talent, the wage gap remains a formidable barrier. Many who depart never return, becoming part of a silent exodus that reshapes the global distribution of human capital. The question remains: can developing nations break this cycle, or will the brain drain continue to drain their future?

Q1: What is the primary reason the author describes brain drain as a "password to a better life"?
Correct: It symbolizes how emigration offers an easy solution to economic hardship for skilled professionals.
Distractors:
1. It refers to the literal use of passwords by migrants to access foreign job markets.
2. It highlights the difficulty of returning home once professionals emigrate.
3. It suggests that developing nations use passwords to track emigrating professionals.
Explanation:
The metaphor "password" implies accessibility to opportunities abroad ("unlock prosperity"). Distractor 1 misinterprets it literally; 2 and 3 misrepresent the text’s focus on individual agency.

Explanation:
The text explicitly cites the doctors’ demonstration as an example ("a recent demonstration by Nigerian doctors"). Other options describe broader trends or opinions.

Distractors:

Explanation:

Topic: an article about neurolinguistics

Text:
Neurolinguistics, the study of how the brain processes language, reveals fascinating insights into human cognition. Language is not dependent on a single brain region but involves multiple networks, particularly in the left hemisphere. Damage to these areas—whether from organic causes like strokes or traumatic injury—can impair speech, yet the brain’s remarkable plasticity often allows individuals to recover some functions over time.

One memorable case involves patients who, after suffering nasty side effects from transcranial radiation, struggled to recall words. However, structured therapy—such as singing in a choir—helped them rebuild neural pathways. Music, it seems, acts as a bridge, activating conservation mechanisms that preserve language skills even when direct speech is difficult.

The brain’s consumption of energy during language tasks is also a key focus. Studies show that memorable phrases or complex sentences require more neural resources, while repetitive drills (like choir practice) optimize efficiency. This dependent relationship between effort and retention has led to therapies combining speech exercises with rhythmic stimulation, reducing recovery time.

Ultimately, neurolinguistics suggests that language is not just a skill but a dynamic, adaptable system. By understanding how the brain processes and conserves linguistic information, researchers can develop better treatments for disorders—and perhaps even enhance learning in healthy individuals.

Questions:
Q1: Why does the author mention choir singing in the context of language recovery?
Correct: It activates neural conservation mechanisms that help rebuild language pathways.
Distractors:
1. It provides a rhythmic structure that makes speech exercises easier.
2. Patients with radiation damage prefer singing over speaking.
3. Singing reduces the brain’s overall energy usage during language tasks.
Explanation:
Weak readers might choose 1 because rhythm is implied, but the text’s focus is on neural pathway rebuilding. 2 is unsupported (preferences aren’t discussed), and 3 contradicts the energy-consumption section (which links effort to complexity, not singing).
---
Q2: How does the brain’s energy consumption relate to learning language?
Correct: Complex or memorable phrases demand more neural energy, while repetitive drills improve efficiency.
Distractors:
1. High energy consumption prevents language recovery after brain damage.
2. Singing in a choir reduces the brain’s overall energy usage.
3. Memorable phrases require less energy because they’re easier to recall.
Explanation:
1 misinterprets the relationship (energy use doesn’t block recovery). 2 confuses singing’s role with energy dynamics. 3 reverses the cause-effect (memorable phrases increase energy demand).
---
Q3: What does the text imply about the long-term effects of transcranial radiation on speech?
Correct: It may cause permanent speech impairments, though therapy can partially mitigate effects.
Distractors:
1. Radiation therapy always leads to complete recovery with enough therapy.
2. Patients who sing in choirs are less affected by radiation damage.
3. The brain’s plasticity ensures full recovery regardless of radiation side effects.
Explanation:
1 ignores the "permanent" qualifier. 2 overgeneralizes choir singing’s role. 3 contradicts the text’s mention of lasting impairments.
---
Q4: According to the text, which brain region is primarily responsible for language processing?
Correct: The left hemisphere (with multiple supporting networks).
Distractors:

2. The brainstem, which regulates energy consumption during speech.
3. Neural pathways distributed equally across both hemispheres.
Explanation:
1 confuses music (right hemisphere) with language (left). 2 introduces an irrelevant region. 3 misrepresents the text’s emphasis on the left hemisphere’s dominance.
---

Correct: It enables the brain to adapt and rebuild damaged pathways, allowing partial recovery.
Distractors:
1. Plasticity prevents the brain from forming new neural connections after damage.

Explanation:
1 misrepresents plasticity as a limitation. 2 ignores the "partial" recovery caveat. 3 introduces an unsupported age-related claim.



Topic: an essay on the economic arguments for and against raising the minimum wage

Text:
The debate over raising the minimum wage remains ongoing, with economists divided on its economic impact. Proponents argue that increasing wages is essential for reducing poverty and boosting consumer spending, which forms the core of economic growth. A higher minimum wage could also improve employee loyalty and productivity, as workers feel more valued and motivated. Some economists urge policymakers to adjust wages to keep pace with inflation, ensuring that workers earn wages equivalent to their purchasing power.

However, critics warn that a sudden increase could push small businesses over the cliff, leading to job losses. They argue that higher labor costs may force companies to cut positions or reduce hours, delivering a financial blow to struggling sectors. Additionally, some fear that wage hikes could spark inflation, as businesses pass costs to consumers.

Supporters tear apart these arguments, pointing out that studies show minimal job loss in regions with higher minimum wages. They also highlight that businesses often become more efficient when workers are fairly compensated, reducing turnover and training costs. Despite criticism, many argue that the benefits of fair wages outweigh the risks, provided the increase is gradual and tied to economic conditions.

Distractors:

- It would reduce business taxes.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text links higher wages to consumer spending and economic growth.
- Distractors:
  - "Eliminate unemployment" is an exaggeration (text mentions potential benefits).
  - "Higher profits" contradicts the financial blow argument.
  - "Reduce taxes" is irrelevant.

Explanation:

Today, people can buy virtual estate in Metaverse platforms, even taking out mortgages on digital land. The prospect of owning property that doesn’t physically exist was once unthinkable, yet here we are.

The rush of technology has also changed how we interact with machines. In Her (2013), humans fell in love with AI—something now explored in real-life chatbots. Meanwhile, concerns about digital privacy grow. Could we one day delete our entire online history? Sci-fi warns that unchecked power over data could lead to dystopia. Yet, for all its warnings, sci-fi remains a well of inspiration. What once seemed like fantasy now feels like the future.

Topic: a sci-fi story about a child who makes friends with a robot

Text:
Leo had always loved exploring the abandoned park on the edge of town, but after last night’s storm, even its usual charm had been ruined. Among the fallen branches and scattered debris, he spotted something unusual—a sophisticated robot half-buried in the mud. Its characteristic glowing eyes flickered weakly, and one arm lay twisted from the destruction of the storm.

Leo knelt beside it, determined to help. "You’re not just a machine," he muttered, trying to assess its condition. The robot’s voice crackled to life: "Functionality at 37%. Require restoration." Leo grinned. "No problem. I’ll fix you up."

Over the next few days, they negotiated a deal—Leo would teach the robot about human emotions, and in return, it would help him with his intellectual challenges. They matched perfectly; the robot could solve math problems instantly, while Leo’s creativity helped it understand jokes. Soon, they were inseparable, playing games and dreaming up inventions.

But not everyone approved. A group of older kids sneered, "Robots don’t belong here!" Leo’s hands clenched. He knew he had to resolve this. "This isn’t just a machine," he argued. "It’s my friend." The robot stepped forward, its voice firm: "I possess emotions too. Would you deny a friend because they are different?" The kids fell silent, and Leo embraced the robot tightly. Together, they would restore the park—and maybe even the town’s trust in the future.

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of being famous

Text:
Fame is a double-edged sword that offers both opportunities and challenges. On one hand, it can aid career growth by opening doors to exclusive networks. For instance, many entrepreneurs find that fame accelerates their business ventures, as it attracts investors and media attention. A single viral moment—a flash on social media—can be the first step toward stardom, leading to lucrative recruitment offers in film, advertising, or activism. Fame also grants access to circles otherwise closed to the public; it acts like a password to elite events and collaborations.

However, the downsides are significant. Privacy is often suspended under the glare of publicity, and personal lives become fair game for scrutiny. The constant stream of criticism can take a toll, while the pressure to maintain an image may lead to destruction of one’s mental health or relationships. Not all fame lasts; what begins as a flash of glory can fade quickly, leaving individuals struggling to rebuild their lives. Even those who harness fame’s potential must navigate its risks carefully, taking a step back when necessary to protect their well-being.

In conclusion, fame is neither inherently good nor bad—it depends on how one wields it. While it can propel careers and inspire change, it demands resilience and boundaries to avoid its darker consequences.

Distractors:
1. It guarantees long-term success for all businesses.
2. It eliminates the need for marketing strategies.
3. It provides a direct path to political power.

The text explicitly states fame "accelerates their business ventures" by attracting investors/media. Distractor 1 ignores the "can fade quickly" warning; 2 conflates fame with replacing marketing; 3 introduces an unsupported claim.

Topic: an article about gardening without soil

Text:
Soil-less gardening, such as hydroponics or aeroponics, is gaining considerable traction among urban residents who seek sustainable ways to grow food. Unlike traditional methods, these systems use lower costs and minimal space, making them appealing even to those without green thumbs. The cry for eco-friendly solutions has led communities to adopt these techniques, often integrating them with gaming apps that monitor plant health in real time.

Local spectators frequently attend workshops where enthusiasts showcase their setups, creating a democratic space for knowledge-sharing. Beyond personal use, these gardens can generate revenue by selling produce at farmers' markets. Additionally, soil-less systems integrate well with wildlife, attracting pollinators like bees and butterflies—something traditional gardens might struggle with.

Critics argue that the initial setup costs are high, although long-term savings lower the barrier. For cities, this method offers a practical way to combat food insecurity while reducing water usage. As technology advances, soil-less gardening might become as commonplace as indoor gaming consoles—proving that innovation can grow roots in unexpected places.
